### TailorKV: A Hybrid Framework for Long-Context Inference via Tailored KV Cache Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19586v1)
- **Authors**: Dingyu Yao, Bowen Shen, Zheng Lin, Wei Liu, Jian Luan, Bin Wang, Weiping Wang
- **Abstract**: The Key-Value (KV) cache in generative large language models (LLMs) introduces substantial memory overhead. Existing works mitigate this burden by offloading or compressing the KV cache. However, loading the entire cache incurs significant latency due to PCIe bandwidth bottlenecks in CPU-GPU communication, while aggressive compression causes notable performance degradation. We identify that certain layers in the LLM need to maintain global information and are unsuitable for selective loading. In contrast, other layers primarily focus on a few tokens with dominant activations that potentially incur substantial quantization error. This observation leads to a key insight that loading dominant tokens and quantizing all tokens can complement each other. Building on this insight, we propose a hybrid compression method, TailorKV, which seamlessly integrates quantization and offloading. TailorKV develops an inference framework along with a hardware-friendly implementation that leverages these complementary characteristics. Extensive long-context evaluations exhibit that TailorKV achieves nearly lossless performance under aggressive compression settings, outperforming the state-of-the-art. Particularly, the Llama-3.1-8B with 128k context can be served within a single RTX 3090 GPU, reaching 82 ms per token during decoding.
- **Summary**: The paper "TailorKV: A Hybrid Framework for Long-Context Inference via Tailored KV Cache Optimization" proposes a novel approach to KV cache optimization for long-context LLM inference. The core idea revolves around identifying layer-specific compression preferences (quantization-friendly vs. sparsity-friendly) and applying tailored compression techniques accordingly. Quantization is used for layers that focus on global information, while dynamic retrieval of dominant tokens is employed for layers emphasizing crucial local information. The paper introduces a metric to classify layers and co-designs the algorithm and system to combine quantization and offloading, maintaining accuracy and efficiency. Extensive experiments on long-context benchmarks demonstrate near-lossless performance with reduced GPU memory usage. Specifically, they can fit a Llama-3.1-8B model with 128k context into a single RTX 3090.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the hybrid approach to KV cache compression, specifically tailoring compression strategies based on layer characteristics rather than applying a uniform approach. The idea of identifying and classifying layers based on their information processing characteristics (global vs. local) and applying different compression techniques is a good one. This contrasts with previous work that typically employed either quantization or sparsity across all layers, or applied different sparsity rates across layers, but using the *same* compression strategy. The dynamic retrieval algorithm and its co-design with CPU/GPU offloading is also a non-trivial contribution. Overall, this targeted, layer-specific approach increases efficiency.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in deploying long-context LLMs: the high memory footprint of the KV cache. By significantly reducing memory usage without sacrificing performance, the paper makes it possible to deploy these models on resource-constrained devices. The experimental results are compelling, showcasing a substantial reduction in GPU memory consumption. The ability to fit a Llama-3.1-8B 128k-context model within a single RTX 3090 is practically significant.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-motivated:** The paper identifies a concrete problem and provides a detailed analysis of the shortcomings of existing solutions.
    *   **Technically sound:** The proposed approach is well-defined and incorporates effective techniques like quantization and dynamic retrieval. The asynchronous CPU/GPU co-execution contributes to reducing latency.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** Extensive experiments across different models, benchmarks, and hardware setups demonstrate the effectiveness of TailorKV. Ablation studies further highlight the contribution of each component.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Offline profiling:** The layer classification process relies on offline profiling. The profiling overhead needs to be explicitly discussed. Also, ideally, one would want an adaptive, online profiling system to handle changes in model behavior during inference.
    *   **Generalizability:** The layer classification metric and associated threshold value may be sensitive to model architecture and task distribution. The paper claims the metric P is consistent across datasets; this could be made more rigorous. The experiments don't show results on many very different backbone architectures, limiting confidence in the general applicability.
    *   **Dynamic Retrieval Overhead:** Dynamic retrieval adds latency. While the paper claims this is masked by asynchronous execution, more detailed breakdown of the latency components could be provided. For example, the PCIe bottleneck and prefetching mechanisms can benefit from a deeper dive into latencies. Also, it doesn't completely eliminate retrieval overhead.
    *   **Hyperparameter tuning:** The success relies on careful selection of thresholds and layer-specific compression settings. The overhead and sensitivity to these hyperparameter configurations should be discussed.
*   **Impact:** The paper could have a moderate impact on the field of LLM inference, particularly for resource-constrained environments. The idea of adapting compression based on layer characteristics could inspire further research in this area.

Score: 7
The paper presents a significant practical contribution to long-context LLM inference, enabling deployment on resource-limited devices. The hybrid compression approach, tailored to layer-specific characteristics, is novel and effective. However, the reliance on offline profiling, potential sensitivity of layer classification, and dynamic retrieval latencies prevent a higher score. While the results are compelling, there's room for future work to improve the adaptability, generalizability, and robustness of the approach.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning to Reason without External Rewards
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19590v1)
- **Authors**: Xuandong Zhao, Zhewei Kang, Aosong Feng, Sergey Levine, Dawn Song
- **Abstract**: Training large language models (LLMs) for complex reasoning via Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) is effective but limited by reliance on costly, domain-specific supervision. We explore Reinforcement Learning from Internal Feedback (RLIF), a framework that enables LLMs to learn from intrinsic signals without external rewards or labeled data. We propose Intuitor, an RLIF method that uses a model's own confidence, termed self-certainty, as its sole reward signal. Intuitor replaces external rewards in Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with self-certainty scores, enabling fully unsupervised learning. Experiments demonstrate that Intuitor matches GRPO's performance on mathematical benchmarks while achieving superior generalization to out-of-domain tasks like code generation, without requiring gold solutions or test cases. Our findings show that intrinsic model signals can drive effective learning across domains, offering a scalable alternative to RLVR for autonomous AI systems where verifiable rewards are unavailable. Code is available at https://github.com/sunblaze-ucb/Intuitor
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Learning to Reason without External Rewards":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Reinforcement Learning from Internal Feedback (RLIF), a novel framework where Large Language Models (LLMs) learn from intrinsic signals, specifically their own confidence, without relying on external rewards or labeled data. The authors propose INTUITOR, an RLIF method that uses a model's "self-certainty" as the sole reward signal. They demonstrate that INTUITOR, when applied to Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), achieves comparable performance to GRPO on mathematical benchmarks while generalizing better to code generation tasks, all without requiring gold solutions or test cases. The key idea is that LLMs inherently exhibit lower confidence on difficult problems, and optimizing for confidence can improve reasoning capabilities across domains.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the explicit formulation and exploration of the RLIF framework and the practical implementation of INTUITOR. While using model confidence or entropy as a reward signal isn't entirely new (as the paper acknowledges), the authors present a cohesive system leveraging this within a policy optimization context and demonstrating its advantages over verifiable reward methods (RLVR) in terms of generalization. The focus on self-generated signals to replace costly external supervision is a meaningful shift.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to overcome limitations of current LLM training paradigms (RLHF and RLVR). Reliance on human annotation (RLHF) or domain-specific verifiers (RLVR) hinders scalability and adaptability. RLIF, particularly with INTUITOR's ease of implementation, offers a pathway toward more autonomous and widely applicable AI systems.  The demonstration of improved generalization, especially in code generation, strengthens the case that intrinsic rewards can unlock latent capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem formulation and well-motivated research direction.
    *   Simple and efficient implementation of INTUITOR.
    *   Empirical evidence supporting the benefits of RLIF, particularly in generalization.
    *   Insightful analysis of the emergent reasoning patterns induced by INTUITOR (e.g., pre-code reasoning).
    *  The paper also showed good experimental results and compared intuitor with other state-of-the-art methods, achieving the same level of the performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on "self-certainty" has limitations. It's not a perfect proxy for correctness, and there's a risk of the model becoming overly confident without actually improving its reasoning. The paper mitigates this somewhat through the use of the KL divergence penalty term and the adoption of group relative policy optimization.
    *   Experiments are primarily on relatively small models (Qwen2.5-3B), and the results are constrained by the amount of resources.
    *   The paper should contain more theoretical insights for this new proposed framework.
    *   Code implementation should be detailed.
    *  It may be better if the authors can provide more failure examples of RLIF.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence research on:
    *   Autonomous AI systems and self-improvement.
    *   Intrinsic motivation and reinforcement learning.
    *   Leveraging model uncertainty for training.
    *   Scalable alternatives to human annotation and verifiable rewards.

**Overall Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing and exploring RLIF. While the use of model confidence as a reward signal is not entirely novel, the authors demonstrate its practical viability and advantages, particularly in generalization. The potential for creating more autonomous and scalable AI systems is significant. The paper is well-written, clearly motivated, and supported by empirical evidence.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Orchestration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19591v1)
- **Authors**: Yufan Dang, Chen Qian, Xueheng Luo, Jingru Fan, Zihao Xie, Ruijie Shi, Weize Chen, Cheng Yang, Xiaoyin Che, Ye Tian, Xuantang Xiong, Lei Han, Zhiyuan Liu, Maosong Sun
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable results across diverse downstream tasks, but their monolithic nature restricts scalability and efficiency in complex problem-solving. While recent research explores multi-agent collaboration among LLMs, most approaches rely on static organizational structures that struggle to adapt as task complexity and agent numbers grow, resulting in coordination overhead and inefficiencies. To this end, we propose a puppeteer-style paradigm for LLM-based multi-agent collaboration, where a centralized orchestrator ("puppeteer") dynamically directs agents ("puppets") in response to evolving task states. This orchestrator is trained via reinforcement learning to adaptively sequence and prioritize agents, enabling flexible and evolvable collective reasoning. Experiments on closed- and open-domain scenarios show that this method achieves superior performance with reduced computational costs. Analyses further reveal that the key improvements consistently stem from the emergence of more compact, cyclic reasoning structures under the orchestrator's evolution.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Multi-Agent Collaboration via Evolving Orchestration." Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new framework for multi-agent collaboration using LLMs, inspired by the concept of a puppeteer controlling puppets. The framework features a centralized "orchestrator" (puppeteer) that dynamically selects and sequences agents (puppets) based on the evolving task state. This orchestrator is trained using reinforcement learning to adaptively prioritize agents and enable flexible, evolvable collective reasoning. The authors demonstrate that this approach achieves superior performance with reduced computational costs on both closed- and open-domain scenarios. They further analyze the evolved topologies, highlighting the emergence of more compact and cyclic reasoning structures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of a centralized, dynamically evolving orchestrator for multi-agent collaboration is relatively novel. While multi-agent systems and reinforcement learning for agent coordination are not new, the puppeteer analogy and the specific implementation details present a unique approach. The emphasis on learning task-specific agent sequences and evolving the interaction topology sets it apart from many static or pre-defined multi-agent architectures.
*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is considerable. Efficiently coordinating multiple LLMs is crucial for tackling complex problems that exceed the capabilities of a single model. The paper addresses the scalability and efficiency limitations of existing multi-agent systems by introducing adaptive agent selection and dynamic pruning, which could lead to more practical and cost-effective applications of multi-agent LLMs. Demonstrating performance gains with reduced computational costs is a significant contribution.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear and well-motivated problem statement:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of monolithic LLMs and existing multi-agent systems.
    *   **Novel framework with a strong analogy:** The puppeteer analogy provides a compelling and intuitive framework for understanding the proposed approach.
    *   **Empirical validation on diverse tasks:** The experiments cover both closed- and open-domain scenarios, providing a comprehensive evaluation of the framework's effectiveness.
    *   **Detailed analysis of evolved topologies:** The analysis of the emergent compact and cyclic reasoning structures provides valuable insights into the underlying mechanisms driving performance improvements.
    *   **Cost-effectiveness:** The authors show that their method achieves better performance and reduces computational cost which is significant for real-world implementation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Baseline Comparisons:** While the baselines are well defined, additional comparisons with existing, more complex or hybrid multi-agent approaches would strengthen the experimental results, demonstrating more explicitly the improvement in relation to current state-of-the-art.
    *   **Complexity of Orchestrator Training:** The reinforcement learning component adds complexity to the system. The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the training process, including hyperparameter tuning and potential challenges related to convergence and stability.
    *   **Generality to Diverse Tasks:** Although the experiments covers different tasks, they are limited to NLP applications. The effectiveness of the framework should be demonstrated on other complex problems that require different agent behaviors.
    *   **Ablation studies:** Ablation studies regarding the components involved in the architecture should be considered for better understanding the contribution of each of them.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in multi-agent LLM systems, particularly in areas such as dynamic coordination, adaptive agent selection, and reinforcement learning for topology optimization. The insights into compact and cyclic reasoning structures could also inform the design of more efficient and robust collaborative reasoning frameworks. The puppeteer paradigm could inspire new approaches to managing and orchestrating large-scale agent interactions.

**Justification:**

While the paper presents a novel and potentially significant approach to multi-agent collaboration, it's not without its limitations. The relatively narrow scope of evaluation, and complexity of the RL component, prevents it from achieving a higher score. The insights presented in the paper related to efficiency are significant, however, the limitations in the evaluation of agent selection are still present.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Accelerating Diffusion-based Text-to-Speech Model Training with Dual Modality Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19595v1)
- **Authors**: Jeongsoo Choi, Zhikang Niu, Ji-Hoon Kim, Chunhui Wang, Joon Son Chung, Chen Xie
- **Abstract**: The goal of this paper is to optimize the training process of diffusion-based text-to-speech models. While recent studies have achieved remarkable advancements, their training demands substantial time and computational costs, largely due to the implicit guidance of diffusion models in learning complex intermediate representations. To address this, we propose A-DMA, an effective strategy for Accelerating training with Dual Modality Alignment. Our method introduces a novel alignment pipeline leveraging both text and speech modalities: text-guided alignment, which incorporates contextual representations, and speech-guided alignment, which refines semantic representations. By aligning hidden states with discriminative features, our training scheme reduces the reliance on diffusion models for learning complex representations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that A-DMA doubles the convergence speed while achieving superior performance over baselines. Code and demo samples are available at: https://github.com/ZhikangNiu/A-DMA
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces A-DMA (Accelerating training with Dual Modality Alignment), a novel training strategy for diffusion-based text-to-speech (TTS) models.  A-DMA employs two alignment pipelines: text-guided alignment using CTC to align text information with hidden states, and speech-guided alignment incorporating self-supervised learning (SSL) speech features to refine semantic representations. The approach aims to reduce reliance on the implicit guidance of diffusion models by aligning hidden states with discriminative features from both modalities. Experiments demonstrate that A-DMA can double the convergence speed while improving performance on zero-shot TTS benchmarks. The approach doesn't modify the underlying model architecture, preserving inference speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using dual modality alignment to accelerate diffusion-based TTS training has merit. The specific implementation details, particularly the text-guided alignment with CTC at intermediate layers and speech-guided alignment with SSL models, seem relatively novel for the TTS domain, although modality alignment in general has been explored in other areas of deep learning.  The combination and application to the specific challenges of diffusion-based TTS are significant. Using the early layers for text and later layers for speech alignment seems like a logical and insightful choice.

*   **Significance:** TTS models, particularly diffusion-based ones, have a high computational cost. An effective method for accelerating training is a highly desirable and significant contribution to the field.  The paper demonstrates tangible benefits in terms of convergence speed and potentially improves quality in low resource settings. The fact that A-DMA doesn't alter the inference pipeline is a major strength, preserving the advantages of NAR models. The provided code and demo samples are extremely valuable for reproducibility and adoption by other researchers.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured paper with a detailed explanation of the method.
    *   The A-DMA approach is simple to implement and doesn't require modifying the model architecture.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in convergence speed and speech quality (WER and SIM).
    *   Extensive ablation studies to validate design choices.
    *   Competitive results on a standard benchmark (LibriSpeech).
    *   Maintains the advantages of the non-autoregressive structure (fast inference).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty, while present, isn't completely groundbreaking.  Modality alignment and using SSL features are not entirely new concepts. The novelty is in the specific combination and application within the specific context of accelerating training of a specific architecture (diffusion TTS). The general idea of using SSL pre-training is also getting quite common.
    *   The gains, while significant, are somewhat incremental. While doubling convergence speed is substantial, the paper doesn't revolutionize the TTS field.
    *   The analysis and explanation of why certain layers are more effective for text or speech alignment could be deepened.  While they suggest a logical reasoning, further investigation of the learned representations would strengthen the claims.
    *   A more thorough comparison of inference time against other accelerated training methods would be valuable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence research in TTS by highlighting the importance of alignment for improving training efficiency of diffusion models. The A-DMA strategy could be adapted to other TTS architectures and potentially extended to other generative tasks. The idea of combining discriminative losses (CTC and SSL) with generative objectives is a generally useful technique.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a valuable contribution to accelerating diffusion-based TTS training, it has some limitations. It demonstrates a clear improvement in convergence speed and speech quality by leveraging the dual modality alignment in the TTS architecture. The approach is fairly simple and the method maintains the fast inference speed of NAR models, which is significant. However, the novelty of using modality alignment in general is not entirely groundbreaking, and the performance gains are somewhat incremental. Despite these limitations, the work is well-executed and contributes to solving a significant challenge in TTS. The experimental validation is thorough, and the results are convincing. The impact in low-resource settings suggests potential real-world applications.

### Preference Optimization by Estimating the Ratio of the Data Distribution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19601v1)
- **Authors**: Yeongmin Kim, Heesun Bae, Byeonghu Na, Il-Chul Moon
- **Abstract**: Direct preference optimization (DPO) is widely used as a simple and stable method for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. This paper investigates a generalized DPO loss that enables a policy model to match the target policy from a likelihood ratio estimation perspective. The ratio of the target policy provides a unique identification of the policy distribution without relying on reward models or partition functions. This allows the generalized loss to retain both simplicity and theoretical guarantees, which prior work such as $f$-PO fails to achieve simultaneously. We propose Bregman preference optimization (BPO), a generalized framework for ratio matching that provides a family of objective functions achieving target policy optimality. BPO subsumes DPO as a special case and offers tractable forms for all instances, allowing implementation with a few lines of code. We further develop scaled Basu's power divergence (SBA), a gradient scaling method that can be used for BPO instances. The BPO framework complements other DPO variants and is applicable to target policies defined by these variants. In experiments, unlike other probabilistic loss extensions such as $f$-DPO or $f$-PO, which exhibit a trade-off between generation fidelity and diversity, instances of BPO improve both win rate and entropy compared with DPO. When applied to Llama-3-Instruct-8B, BPO achieves state-of-the-art performance among Llama-3-8B backbones, with a 55.9\% length-controlled win rate on AlpacaEval2.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Bregman Preference Optimization (BPO), a generalized framework for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) aimed at aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. BPO reframes the DPO objective as a ratio-matching problem between the data preference ratio and the model ratio, extending the loss using Bregman divergence. This allows for a family of objective functions, with DPO being a special case. The paper also introduces scaled Basu's power (SBA) divergence as a gradient scaling method for BPO instances. The key claim is that BPO retains the simplicity and theoretical guarantees of DPO while offering improved performance, particularly in balancing generation fidelity (win rate) and diversity (entropy). The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that BPO instances, particularly with SBA, improve both win rate and entropy compared to DPO, and achieve state-of-the-art performance among Llama-3-8B backbones on AlpacaEval2.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper offers a well-motivated generalization of DPO. The reframing of the DPO objective as a ratio-matching problem using Bregman divergence provides a solid theoretical foundation for the proposed BPO framework. The derivation is clear and the connection to existing literature on likelihood ratio estimation is well-established.

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the generalization of the DPO loss using Bregman divergence, along with the SBA gradient scaling technique. While extensions of DPO exist, this approach offers a more systematic way to explore different loss functions without sacrificing the core advantages of DPO, like stability and simplicity. Compared to f-PO/EXO which requires approximations, BPO claims exact target optimization, and simplicity, setting it apart from other probabilistic loss extensions. Also, the application of gradient scaling with SBA divergence further contributes to practical performance improvements.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the alignment of LLMs with human preferences in a more flexible and efficient way. The experiments suggest that BPO can overcome the trade-off between fidelity and diversity, a crucial aspect of LLM alignment. The reported state-of-the-art results on AlpacaEval2 for a Llama-3-8B model provides significant empirical evidence supporting the paper's claims. The fact that the framework allows easy implementation by modifying a few lines of code increases its potential impact. Also, the BPO provides an orthogonal improvement over existing DPO variations like SimPO and f-DPO.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical justification for the BPO framework.
    *   Clear derivation and connection to existing literature.
    *   Empirical evidence demonstrating improved performance compared to DPO and other extensions.
    *   Easy implementation.
    *   Orthogonal improvement over existing DPO variations

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental evaluation, while strong, could be expanded to include a wider range of datasets and models. Although the paper shows an orthogonal improvement over SimPO, it focuses primarily on comparing it against original DPO.
    *   The hyperparameter tuning for the SBA divergence, while effective, requires additional effort. More guidance on selecting appropriate values for the hyperparameter lambda based on the data/model would be valuable.
    *   Although the BPO framework offers the ability to choose `h` function arbitrarily, the experiments focus only on a specific `h` function, especially the scaling version.

Overall, this is a strong paper that provides a valuable contribution to the field of LLM alignment. The theoretical framework, combined with strong experimental results, makes a compelling case for the BPO framework.

### Rep3D: Re-parameterize Large 3D Kernels with Low-Rank Receptive Modeling for Medical Imaging
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19603v1)
- **Authors**: Ho Hin Lee, Quan Liu, Shunxing Bao, Yuankai Huo, Bennett A. Landman
- **Abstract**: In contrast to vision transformers, which model long-range dependencies through global self-attention, large kernel convolutions provide a more efficient and scalable alternative, particularly in high-resolution 3D volumetric settings. However, naively increasing kernel size often leads to optimization instability and degradation in performance. Motivated by the spatial bias observed in effective receptive fields (ERFs), we hypothesize that different kernel elements converge at variable rates during training. To support this, we derive a theoretical connection between element-wise gradients and first-order optimization, showing that structurally re-parameterized convolution blocks inherently induce spatially varying learning rates. Building on this insight, we introduce Rep3D, a 3D convolutional framework that incorporates a learnable spatial prior into large kernel training. A lightweight two-stage modulation network generates a receptive-biased scaling mask, adaptively re-weighting kernel updates and enabling local-to-global convergence behavior. Rep3D adopts a plain encoder design with large depthwise convolutions, avoiding the architectural complexity of multi-branch compositions. We evaluate Rep3D on five challenging 3D segmentation benchmarks and demonstrate consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines, including transformer-based and fixed-prior re-parameterization methods. By unifying spatial inductive bias with optimization-aware learning, Rep3D offers an interpretable, and scalable solution for 3D medical image analysis. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/leeh43/Rep3D.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Rep3D: Re-parameterize Large 3D Kernels with Low-Rank Receptive Modeling for Medical Imaging" introduces a novel convolutional framework for 3D medical image segmentation. It addresses the challenges of training large kernel 3D CNNs, particularly optimization instability and performance degradation. The core idea is to incorporate a learnable spatial prior into the training process that mirrors the spatial bias observed in effective receptive fields (ERFs). The authors derive a theoretical connection between element-wise gradients and optimization, showing that structurally re-parameterized convolution blocks inherently induce spatially varying learning rates.  Based on this, Rep3D uses a lightweight two-stage modulation network to generate a receptive-biased scaling mask that adaptively re-weights kernel updates. This approach enables local-to-global convergence behavior and avoids the architectural complexity of multi-branch designs. The framework is evaluated on five 3D segmentation benchmarks, demonstrating improvements over state-of-the-art baselines, including transformers and fixed-prior re-parameterization methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel re-parameterization strategy tailored for large 3D kernels in medical imaging. The key novelties lie in:
    *   The theoretical analysis linking CSLA blocks to spatially varying learning rates and ERF behavior.
    *   The learnable spatial prior modulated by a lightweight generator network to guide kernel update dynamics.
    *   The plain encoder design that simplifies architecture while retaining representational capacity.

    While the idea of re-parameterization is not entirely new, its application to 3D large kernel CNNs with an ERF-aware adaptive learning rate is a significant contribution.  Prior works have explored re-parameterization, but often with fixed priors or in 2D contexts. The idea of spatial bias in large kernel training, and modeling it with a lightweight modulation network seems conceptually sound and effective. The theoretical grounding linking different kernel branches to varying learning rates is also a strong point.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from:
    *   Addressing a key challenge in 3D medical image analysis: effectively training large kernel CNNs.
    *   Achieving state-of-the-art performance on multiple challenging segmentation benchmarks, demonstrating practical applicability.
    *   Providing an interpretable framework by linking modulation masks with ERF patterns, offering insights into convolution kernel learning.
    *   Demonstrating a scalable alternative to transformers for high-resolution 3D data, potentially lowering computational costs.

    The improvements over SOTA baselines are empirically significant and well-documented. The ablation studies are detailed and convincingly demonstrate the value of the learned spatial prior and the architecture design choices. The interpretabilty aspect is also a significant advantage, addressing the "black box" nature of many deep learning methods. The paper tackles practical issues related to deploying deep learning for 3D medical imaging, where compute resources are often constrained, providing an alternative to memory-intensive transformers.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong theoretical justification for the proposed approach.
    *   Well-designed and thorough experimental validation across multiple datasets.
    *   Clear and concise writing with detailed ablation studies.
    *   Competitive results and empirical validation against multiple SOTA algorithms

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the lightweight generator network mitigates complexity, the training cost with large 3D kernels is still substantial. The paper addresses this in the discussion by explaining limitations with the training and optimization of the large kernel mechanism in 3D.
    *   The performance is inherently tied to the input volume resolution, potentially requiring careful pre-processing. While they address this in the discussion, further investigation into resolution-invariant strategies could be useful.
    *   The distance decay prior, while effective, could be seen as a somewhat simplistic representation of ERF behavior.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The paper will likely influence future research on training large kernel CNNs in 3D medical imaging.
    *   The proposed re-parameterization strategy could be adopted in other domains where spatial priors are relevant.
    *   The insights into spatially varying learning rates could inspire new optimization techniques.
    *   Code availability enhances reproducibility and adoption by the community.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper presents a significant contribution by addressing a key practical challenge in 3D medical imaging. The theoretical grounding, novel re-parameterization strategy, competitive empirical results, and focus on interpretability justify a high score. While there are minor limitations regarding computational cost and potential sensitivity to resolution, the overall impact and novelty are substantial.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Skrull: Towards Efficient Long Context Fine-tuning through Dynamic Data Scheduling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19609v1)
- **Authors**: Hongtao Xu, Wenting Shen, Yuanxin Wei, Ang Wang, Guo Runfan, Tianxing Wang, Yong Li, Mingzhen Li, Weile Jia
- **Abstract**: Long-context supervised fine-tuning (Long-SFT) plays a vital role in enhancing the performance of large language models (LLMs) on long-context tasks. To smoothly adapt LLMs to long-context scenarios, this process typically entails training on mixed datasets containing both long and short sequences. However, this heterogeneous sequence length distribution poses significant challenges for existing training systems, as they fail to simultaneously achieve high training efficiency for both long and short sequences, resulting in sub-optimal end-to-end system performance in Long-SFT. In this paper, we present a novel perspective on data scheduling to address the challenges posed by the heterogeneous data distributions in Long-SFT. We propose Skrull, a dynamic data scheduler specifically designed for efficient long-SFT. Through dynamic data scheduling, Skrull balances the computation requirements of long and short sequences, improving overall training efficiency. Furthermore, we formulate the scheduling process as a joint optimization problem and thoroughly analyze the trade-offs involved. Based on those analysis, Skrull employs a lightweight scheduling algorithm to achieve near-zero cost online scheduling in Long-SFT. Finally, we implement Skrull upon DeepSpeed, a state-of-the-art distributed training system for LLMs. Experimental results demonstrate that Skrull outperforms DeepSpeed by 3.76x on average (up to 7.54x) in real-world long-SFT scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Skrull: Towards Efficient Long Context Fine-tuning through Dynamic Data Scheduling":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on long-context tasks (Long-SFT). It observes that the mixed sequence lengths (long and short) in typical Long-SFT datasets lead to inefficiencies in existing distributed training frameworks. To solve this, the authors propose Skrull, a dynamic data scheduler designed to balance the computation requirements of long and short sequences. Skrull consists of two main components: Distributed-Aware Context Parallelism (DACP), which intelligently shards and schedules sequences across workers, and Global Data Scheduling (GDS), which improves GPU utilization by optimizing the micro-batching strategy. The scheduling process is formulated as a joint optimization problem, and a lightweight heuristic algorithm is used for runtime optimization. Experimental results show that Skrull outperforms DeepSpeed in real-world Long-SFT scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel perspective on data scheduling for Long-SFT, specifically addressing the challenges posed by heterogeneous sequence lengths. The idea of dynamic data scheduling, embodied in the DACP and GDS components, is well-motivated and has the potential to significantly improve training efficiency. The distributed-aware context parallelism is an interesting idea, especially in optimizing for the different computational characteristics for long and short sequences. The formulation of the scheduling problem as a joint optimization problem is a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to make Long-SFT more practical and efficient. With the increasing importance of long-context capabilities for LLMs, improving the training efficiency of these models is crucial. The 3.76x average speedup compared to DeepSpeed is a substantial improvement and demonstrates the practical impact of the proposed approach. The thorough experimental evaluation on real-world datasets further strengthens the paper's significance. It would be helpful to include energy consumption analysis to further solidify the impact of the proposed method.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed system architecture with two complementary components (DACP and GDS).
    *   Formal formulation of the scheduling problem as a joint optimization.
    *   Lightweight heuristic algorithm for runtime efficiency.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on real-world datasets.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The heuristic algorithm, while effective, might not always achieve the optimal solution. A discussion of the limitations of the heuristic and potential future directions for improvement would be valuable.
    *   The detailed implementation aspects of the DACP and GDS components could be elaborated further. For example, more information on memory management, communication strategies, and specific hardware optimizations would be beneficial.
    *   Although comparison to state-of-the-art DeepSpeed is provided, ablation studies about how the DACP/GDS affect short/long sequences respectively are missing. More specific analysis about the performance of DACP/GDS separately are needed.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of efficient Long-SFT for LLMs. It introduces a novel data scheduling approach that addresses a critical challenge and demonstrates significant performance improvements over a strong baseline. While there are some limitations regarding the optimality of the heuristic algorithm and the depth of implementation details, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. Overall, Skrull has the potential to influence future research in this area and contribute to the broader adoption of long-context LLMs.

**Score: 7**

### TESSER: Transfer-Enhancing Adversarial Attacks from Vision Transformers via Spectral and Semantic Regularization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19613v1)
- **Authors**: Amira Guesmi, Bassem Ouni, Muhammad Shafique
- **Abstract**: Adversarial transferability remains a critical challenge in evaluating the robustness of deep neural networks. In security-critical applications, transferability enables black-box attacks without access to model internals, making it a key concern for real-world adversarial threat assessment. While Vision Transformers (ViTs) have demonstrated strong adversarial performance, existing attacks often fail to transfer effectively across architectures, especially from ViTs to Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) or hybrid models. In this paper, we introduce \textbf{TESSER} -- a novel adversarial attack framework that enhances transferability via two key strategies: (1) \textit{Feature-Sensitive Gradient Scaling (FSGS)}, which modulates gradients based on token-wise importance derived from intermediate feature activations, and (2) \textit{Spectral Smoothness Regularization (SSR)}, which suppresses high-frequency noise in perturbations using a differentiable Gaussian prior. These components work in tandem to generate perturbations that are both semantically meaningful and spectrally smooth. Extensive experiments on ImageNet across 12 diverse architectures demonstrate that TESSER achieves +10.9\% higher attack succes rate (ASR) on CNNs and +7.2\% on ViTs compared to the state-of-the-art Adaptive Token Tuning (ATT) method. Moreover, TESSER significantly improves robustness against defended models, achieving 53.55\% ASR on adversarially trained CNNs. Qualitative analysis shows strong alignment between TESSER's perturbations and salient visual regions identified via Grad-CAM, while frequency-domain analysis reveals a 12\% reduction in high-frequency energy, confirming the effectiveness of spectral regularization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TESSER, a novel adversarial attack framework designed to improve the transferability of adversarial examples generated from Vision Transformers (ViTs), particularly to CNNs and hybrid models.  TESSER employs two key strategies: Feature-Sensitive Gradient Scaling (FSGS), which modulates gradients based on the importance of each token derived from intermediate feature activations, and Spectral Smoothness Regularization (SSR), which suppresses high-frequency noise in perturbations using a differentiable Gaussian prior.  The authors demonstrate through experiments on ImageNet that TESSER achieves significant improvements in attack success rate (ASR) compared to state-of-the-art methods, especially on CNNs and adversarially trained models. They also provide qualitative analysis showing alignment between TESSER's perturbations and salient visual regions, as well as a reduction in high-frequency energy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its combination of feature-sensitive gradient scaling and spectral smoothness regularization specifically tailored to enhance the transferability of adversarial attacks from ViTs to other architectures. While gradient scaling and regularization techniques have been explored previously, their application in this specific combination, targeting token-level semantics and spectral properties for ViT transferability, is a notable contribution. FSGS and SSR tackle specific problems of ViTs, making the approach quite specific to them, which can limit their transfer to other models. However, the use of token importance derived from inner embeddings is not completely new, as the authors themselves acknowledge the connection with earlier work in NLP and vision.  The module-wise gradient modulation strategy, while inspired by ATT, is also a new element that contributes to the overall effectiveness.
* **Significance:** The significance of this work is that it addresses a critical challenge in adversarial robustness research: the limited transferability of attacks across different neural network architectures. The ability to generate adversarial examples that reliably transfer from ViTs to CNNs and defended models is crucial for real-world security assessments.  The paper provides compelling evidence that TESSER significantly improves transferability, making it a valuable tool for evaluating the robustness of diverse models. The findings suggest that carefully crafted perturbations, considering both semantic importance and spectral properties, can be more effective in exploiting vulnerabilities across different architectures.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on ImageNet across a diverse set of models, consistently demonstrating the superiority of TESSER over existing methods. The improvements in ASR on CNNs and defended models are particularly noteworthy.
    *   **Clear and well-motivated approach:** The paper clearly explains the rationale behind each component of TESSER (FSGS and SSR) and provides a theoretical justification for FSGS.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper includes ablation studies, Grad-CAM visualizations, and frequency-domain analyses to provide a comprehensive understanding of TESSER's behavior and effectiveness. The Grad-CAM images provide qualitative evidence of improved semantic alignment.
    *   **Practicality:** The method is computationally efficient and compatible with existing attack pipelines.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **ViT-specific focus:** The design of TESSER is heavily focused on ViTs and may not generalize well to other types of models without significant modifications. The reliance on token embeddings is a key factor.
    *   **Limited theoretical depth:** While the paper provides a theoretical justification for FSGS, a more rigorous and comprehensive theoretical analysis of the overall framework would be beneficial.
    *   **Incremental novelty:** The paper builds upon existing gradient scaling and regularization techniques. The novelty lies primarily in the specific combination and application to the ViT transferability problem.
    *   **Hyperparameter tuning:** The method requires tuning several hyperparameters. The sensitivity of the results to these hyperparameters should be further investigated.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on adversarial attacks and robustness by highlighting the importance of semantic and spectral properties for transferability. It provides a strong baseline for future work on improving cross-architecture attack transferability.  The techniques developed in TESSER could also be adapted for use in other domains where transferability is important.

* **Overall Assessment:** The paper presents a well-executed and thoroughly analyzed method for improving the transferability of adversarial attacks from ViTs to other architectures. The empirical results are compelling, and the insights gained from the qualitative and quantitative analyses are valuable. The approach is clearly explained and justified, although a more in-depth theoretical analysis would be desirable. While the novelty is incremental, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field by addressing a critical challenge and providing a strong baseline for future work.

### Diagnosing and Mitigating Modality Interference in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19616v1)
- **Authors**: Rui Cai, Bangzheng Li, Xiaofei Wen, Muhao Chen, Zhe Zhao
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities across tasks, yet they often exhibit difficulty in distinguishing task-relevant from irrelevant signals, particularly in tasks like Visual Question Answering (VQA), which can lead to susceptibility to misleading or spurious inputs. We refer to this broader limitation as the Cross-Modality Competency Problem: the model's inability to fairly evaluate all modalities. This vulnerability becomes more evident in modality-specific tasks such as image classification or pure text question answering, where models are expected to rely solely on one modality. In such tasks, spurious information from irrelevant modalities often leads to significant performance degradation. We refer to this failure as Modality Interference, which serves as a concrete and measurable instance of the cross-modality competency problem. We further design a perturbation-based causal diagnostic experiment to verify and quantify this problem. To mitigate modality interference, we propose a novel framework to fine-tune MLLMs, including perturbation-based data augmentations with both heuristic perturbations and adversarial perturbations via Projected Gradient Descent (PGD), and a consistency regularization strategy applied to model outputs with original and perturbed inputs. Experiments on multiple benchmark datasets (image-heavy, text-heavy, and VQA tasks) and multiple model families with different scales demonstrate significant improvements in robustness and cross-modality competency, indicating our method's effectiveness in boosting unimodal reasoning ability while enhancing performance on multimodal tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the "Modality Interference" problem in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), where models struggle to distinguish relevant from irrelevant signals across modalities.  The authors define this as a broader "Cross-Modality Competency Problem," where MLLMs fail to fairly evaluate all modalities. They design a perturbation-based causal diagnostic experiment to quantify this issue. To mitigate it, they propose a fine-tuning framework using perturbation-based data augmentation (heuristic and adversarial via PGD) and consistency regularization. Experiments on multiple datasets and model families show improved robustness and cross-modality competency, enhancing unimodal reasoning and multimodal task performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a clear conceptual framework ("Cross-Modality Competency Problem" and "Modality Interference") that provides a useful lens for understanding limitations of MLLMs. The perturbation-based causal diagnosis is a strong methodological contribution. The combination of heuristic perturbations, adversarial training with modality masking, and consistency regularization is a novel fine-tuning approach.

*   **Significance:** The identification and quantification of Modality Interference is significant because it highlights a critical vulnerability of MLLMs that isn't always apparent from standard benchmark results.  The proposed mitigation strategy demonstrably improves robustness and addresses a real-world limitation in applying MLLMs. The focus on maintaining unimodal task performance during multimodal fine-tuning is also essential, preventing the degradation of modality-specific strengths.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly defines the problem and its scope, distinguishing it from related issues like catastrophic forgetting or knowledge conflicts.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The perturbation-based evaluation methodology is well-designed to isolate and quantify the effect of modality interference.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The experiments are conducted on a range of models, datasets, and scales, adding credibility to the findings.  The ablation studies effectively demonstrate the contribution of each component of the proposed fine-tuning framework.
    *   **Practical Solution:** The fine-tuning framework offers a practical approach to mitigating modality interference in real-world MLLM applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Heuristic Perturbations:** While effective, the heuristic perturbations are task-specific and may not generalize to all scenarios. A more theoretically grounded or automated approach to perturbation generation could be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Scope of PGD:** As the authors acknowledge, the PGD-based adversarial training is limited to a specific input space (embedding-level noise).  More robust defense strategies that generalize across semantic and modality perturbations would be more desirable for real-world applications.
    *   **Compute Cost:** The computational overhead of adversarial training and the proposed fine-tuning strategy might limit its accessibility to researchers and practitioners with limited resources. The authors mention the use of DeepSpeed to mitigate these issues but do not provide concrete details for reproducing.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by shifting the focus from simply improving multimodal alignment to explicitly addressing modality interference and ensuring cross-modality competency. The evaluation methodology and fine-tuning framework can serve as a foundation for future research in this area. The framework also highlights the significance of unimodal reasoning and how it should be maintained throughout a training procedure involving multimodality.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a well-defined problem, a rigorous evaluation methodology, and a practical solution with convincing experimental results. While the limitations regarding perturbation generality and computational cost are valid, the contributions are significant enough to warrant a high score. The conceptual framing is valuable, and the fine-tuning framework offers a practical approach to a pressing issue in MLLMs.
