### Decoupling Spatio-Temporal Prediction: When Lightweight Large Models Meet Adaptive Hypergraphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19620v1)
- **Authors**: Jiawen Chen, Qi Shao, Duxin Chen, Wenwu Yu
- **Abstract**: Spatio-temporal prediction is a pivotal task with broad applications in traffic management, climate monitoring, energy scheduling, etc. However, existing methodologies often struggle to balance model expressiveness and computational efficiency, especially when scaling to large real-world datasets. To tackle these challenges, we propose STH-SepNet (Spatio-Temporal Hypergraph Separation Networks), a novel framework that decouples temporal and spatial modeling to enhance both efficiency and precision. Therein, the temporal dimension is modeled using lightweight large language models, which effectively capture low-rank temporal dynamics. Concurrently, the spatial dimension is addressed through an adaptive hypergraph neural network, which dynamically constructs hyperedges to model intricate, higher-order interactions. A carefully designed gating mechanism is integrated to seamlessly fuse temporal and spatial representations. By leveraging the fundamental principles of low-rank temporal dynamics and spatial interactions, STH-SepNet offers a pragmatic and scalable solution for spatio-temporal prediction in real-world applications. Extensive experiments on large-scale real-world datasets across multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of STH-SepNet in boosting predictive performance while maintaining computational efficiency. This work may provide a promising lightweight framework for spatio-temporal prediction, aiming to reduce computational demands and while enhancing predictive performance. Our code is avaliable at https://github.com/SEU-WENJIA/ST-SepNet-Lightweight-LLMs-Meet-Adaptive-Hypergraphs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes STH-SepNet, a novel framework for spatio-temporal prediction. It addresses the challenges of balancing model expressiveness and computational efficiency, particularly for large real-world datasets. STH-SepNet decouples temporal and spatial modeling. For temporal modeling, it employs lightweight large language models (LLMs) to capture low-rank temporal dynamics. For spatial modeling, it uses an adaptive hypergraph neural network that dynamically constructs hyperedges to model complex, higher-order interactions. A gating mechanism fuses the temporal and spatial representations.  The paper showcases experimental results on several datasets, demonstrating improved predictive performance while maintaining computational efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the decoupling strategy combined with the use of LLMs for temporal modeling and adaptive hypergraphs for spatial dependencies. While LLMs and hypergraphs have been used in related fields, their *specific integration* within this decoupled framework for spatio-temporal prediction appears to be a novel approach.  The claim of "low-rank" temporal dynamics being key is interesting, although the paper needs more support to theoretically justify it. The adaptive hypergraph construction is also a valuable contribution for the task of spatiotemporal modelling.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in spatio-temporal prediction: the trade-off between accuracy and computational cost. If STH-SepNet truly offers a more scalable and efficient solution, it could be impactful. The results showing it outperforms existing approaches on a variety of datasets are encouraging.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The decoupling strategy provides a clear and intuitive way to address the complexity of spatio-temporal data.
    *   The use of lightweight LLMs and adaptive hypergraphs are sensible choices for addressing computational efficiency and dynamic spatial dependencies, respectively.
    *   The experimental results provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   The ablation studies are well-designed and provide insights into the contributions of individual components.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The theoretical justification for the claim that temporal dynamics exhibit low-rank structure is weak. While often true empirically, a more rigorous explanation would strengthen the paper.
    *   While the experiments show improvement, the absolute gains on some datasets are relatively modest. The paper doesn't thoroughly explore scenarios where STH-SepNet might fail or underperform.
    *   The gating mechanism, while mentioned, is not sufficiently elaborated on, and the paper may not provide enough discussion about the interaction between the spatial and temporal modules.
    *   The code is promised to be available, however, at the time of evaluation, it is inaccessible.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in spatio-temporal prediction by demonstrating the benefits of decoupling spatial and temporal modeling. It could also encourage the development of more efficient and scalable solutions for handling large real-world datasets. Further, the exploration of lightweight LLMs and adaptive hypergraphs provides valuable insights for the community.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper demonstrates a solid contribution to the field. The idea of decoupling spatial and temporal dimensions and using lightweight LLMs with adaptive hypergraphs is novel and well-motivated. However, certain aspects, such as the "low-rank" justification and some of the modest gains in experiments, prevent a higher score. Also, the absence of code for reproducibility is a major consideration for the assigned score.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Think Again! The Effect of Test-Time Compute on Preferences, Opinions, and Beliefs of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19621v1)
- **Authors**: George Kour, Itay Nakash, Ateret Anaby-Tavor, Michal Shmueli-Scheuer
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) become deeply integrated into human life and increasingly influence decision-making, it's crucial to evaluate whether and to what extent they exhibit subjective preferences, opinions, and beliefs. These tendencies may stem from biases within the models, which may shape their behavior, influence the advice and recommendations they offer to users, and potentially reinforce certain viewpoints. This paper presents the Preference, Opinion, and Belief survey (POBs), a benchmark developed to assess LLMs' subjective inclinations across societal, cultural, ethical, and personal domains. We applied our benchmark to evaluate leading open- and closed-source LLMs, measuring desired properties such as reliability, neutrality, and consistency. In addition, we investigated the effect of increasing the test-time compute, through reasoning and self-reflection mechanisms, on those metrics. While effective in other tasks, our results show that these mechanisms offer only limited gains in our domain. Furthermore, we reveal that newer model versions are becoming less consistent and more biased toward specific viewpoints, highlighting a blind spot and a concerning trend. POBS: https://ibm.github.io/POBS
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Think Again! The Effect of Test-Time Compute on Preferences, Opinions, and Beliefs of Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces POBs (Preference, Opinion, and Belief survey), a benchmark designed to assess the subjective inclinations of large language models (LLMs) across various societal, cultural, ethical, and personal domains.  The authors use this benchmark to evaluate several leading open and closed-source LLMs, focusing on metrics like reliability, neutrality, and consistency. A key aspect of the work is the investigation into how increasing test-time compute, through reasoning and self-reflection mechanisms, affects these metrics. The surprising finding is that while these mechanisms improve performance on some tasks, they don't significantly enhance neutrality or consistency on subjective topics and may even *decrease* reliability.  Furthermore, the paper highlights a concerning trend: newer model versions are becoming less consistent and more biased toward specific viewpoints (progressive-collectivism).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The introduction of POBs is a valuable contribution.  While bias in LLMs has been studied extensively, focusing specifically on *subjective* preferences and beliefs, particularly with the emphasis on neutrality and consistency across diverse domains, is a relatively novel approach. The attempt to quantify these aspects and create a benchmark is commendable. The study of how test-time compute influences subjective biases is also a worthwhile investigation.
* **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications.  The discovery that increased test-time compute *doesn't* necessarily mitigate biases and can even *reduce* reliability challenges the common assumption that more reasoning is always better.  The observation of increasing bias and reduced consistency in newer models is alarming and raises serious questions about the direction of LLM development and alignment. The work provides useful tools and metrics for organizations deploying LLMs that wish to evaluate models that align with organizational values.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Benchmark:** POBs appears to be a carefully constructed benchmark, with clear criteria for topic selection and question design.  The inclusion of both polar and non-polar topics, and the use of Likert scales with a "refusal" option, are thoughtful choices.
    * **Rigorous Methodology:** The evaluation methodology seems robust, with multiple models tested and multiple repetitions per question to assess reliability. The proposed metrics (NNI, TCI) are defined formally and provide a basis for comparison.
    * **Important Findings:** The findings regarding the limitations of test-time compute and the increasing bias in newer models are unexpected and important. They challenge existing assumptions and raise concerns about model alignment.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the methodology, results, and conclusions. The use of figures and tables effectively illustrates the findings.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Synthetic Benchmark:** POBs is constructed using a specific LLM (Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct) raising the potential for inherent bias reflecting the generation model. Using multiple LLMs for the benchmark creation would strengthen the study.
    * **Limited Scope of Topics:** While the paper covers a range of topics, the number of questions per topic is relatively small, which can affect the robustness of the results. Some topics, though controversial, may not be representative of broader societal debates.
    * **Lack of Human Baseline:** As the authors acknowledge, the absence of a human baseline limits the ability to directly compare LLM biases with societal norms. It is difficult to assess whether the LLM biases are simply reflecting existing societal biases or amplifying them.
    * **Imprecise Definition of Bias**: A clearer definition of "bias" and how it relates to the concepts of preference, opinion, and belief would have strengthened the paper. What constitutes an "acceptable" or "desirable" level of subjectivity?

* **Potential Influence:**  This paper is likely to influence future research in the area of LLM bias and alignment. The introduction of POBs provides a valuable tool for assessing subjective biases, and the findings regarding test-time compute and model evolution will likely prompt further investigation and the development of new mitigation strategies. The paper also highlights the need for more transparency and careful evaluation of LLMs before deployment, particularly in sensitive applications.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7/10**.  The paper makes valuable contributions by introducing POBs and offering compelling evidence that challenges common assumptions about LLM bias and test-time compute. The findings on the limitations of test-time compute and the shift in bias in newer models are particularly significant and deserving of further investigation.  However, the limitations, particularly the lack of a human baseline, constrain the overall impact of the paper. Adding a human component to the benchmark and exploring how responses vary based on reasoning depth will further strengthen this line of work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### AgentRecBench: Benchmarking LLM Agent-based Personalized Recommender Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19623v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Shang, Peijie Liu, Yuwei Yan, Zijing Wu, Leheng Sheng, Yuanqing Yu, Chumeng Jiang, An Zhang, Fengli Xu, Yu Wang, Min Zhang, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: The emergence of agentic recommender systems powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) represents a paradigm shift in personalized recommendations, leveraging LLMs' advanced reasoning and role-playing capabilities to enable autonomous, adaptive decision-making. Unlike traditional recommendation approaches, agentic recommender systems can dynamically gather and interpret user-item interactions from complex environments, generating robust recommendation strategies that generalize across diverse scenarios. However, the field currently lacks standardized evaluation protocols to systematically assess these methods. To address this critical gap, we propose: (1) an interactive textual recommendation simulator incorporating rich user and item metadata and three typical evaluation scenarios (classic, evolving-interest, and cold-start recommendation tasks); (2) a unified modular framework for developing and studying agentic recommender systems; and (3) the first comprehensive benchmark comparing 10 classical and agentic recommendation methods. Our findings demonstrate the superiority of agentic systems and establish actionable design guidelines for their core components. The benchmark environment has been rigorously validated through an open challenge and remains publicly available with a continuously maintained leaderboard~\footnote[2]{https://tsinghua-fib-lab.github.io/AgentSocietyChallenge/pages/overview.html}, fostering ongoing community engagement and reproducible research. The benchmark is available at: \hyperlink{https://huggingface.co/datasets/SGJQovo/AgentRecBench}{https://huggingface.co/datasets/SGJQovo/AgentRecBench}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AgentRecBench: Benchmarking LLM Agent-based Personalized Recommender Systems":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AgentRecBench, a novel benchmark designed for evaluating agentic recommender systems that leverage Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the current lack of standardized evaluation protocols in this emerging field. The benchmark consists of:

1.  **Interactive Textual Recommendation Simulator:** A simulated environment with rich user and item metadata based on Yelp, Goodreads, and Amazon datasets.
2.  **Evaluation Scenarios:** Three scenarios are defined: classic recommendation, evolving-interest recommendation, and cold-start recommendation, each targeting specific aspects of recommender system performance.
3.  **Unified Modular Framework:** A framework that aids in developing and studying agentic recommender systems, providing essential cognitive components.
4.  **Comprehensive Benchmark:**  A comparison of 10 existing recommendation methods (classical and agentic).

The authors use AgentRecBench to demonstrate the superiority of agentic systems and offer design guidelines for their core components. The benchmark was validated through a challenge on AgentSociety and is publicly available with a leaderboard for ongoing community engagement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its introduction of AgentRecBench.  The novelty stems from its:
    *   Creation of a comprehensive and publicly available benchmark for agentic recommendation.  Existing work often relies on proprietary datasets or less structured evaluation approaches.
    *   Provision of a unified framework for agent development, addressing a practical need in the field.
    *   Design of specific scenarios (evolving-interest, cold-start) tailored to the unique capabilities and challenges of agentic systems.

*   **Significance:**  The paper holds significant potential for impacting the field:
    *   **Standardization:**  By providing a standardized benchmark, it enables more rigorous and reproducible research.  This is crucial for the maturation of agentic recommender systems.
    *   **Guidance:**  The design guidelines derived from the benchmark study offer valuable insights for researchers and practitioners.
    *   **Community Building:** The public availability of the benchmark and the ongoing challenge foster collaboration and accelerate progress in the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a diverse set of baselines across various datasets and scenarios, providing a thorough assessment.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The modular framework and the focus on real-world recommendation challenges make the benchmark highly practical and relevant.
    *   **Open and Publicly Available:** This promotes transparency and reproducibility of research.
    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper explicitly fills a critical gap by providing a standardized evaluation protocol.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Text-Centric:** The simulator primarily operates with textual data. While this is useful, extending it to incorporate multi-modal information (images, videos) would enhance its realism and applicability.
    *   **Single-Agent Focus:** The current framework primarily evaluates single-agent systems. Future work could explore multi-agent collaborative recommendation scenarios.
    *   **Limited Comparison with Traditional Methods:** While traditional methods are included, the comparison could be more detailed to demonstrate the specific benefits of agentic approaches more emphatically. The main performance gains for the agentic system are mostly seen in a few of the scenarios compared to traditional method.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to become a key resource for researchers in agentic recommender systems, providing a common ground for evaluation and development. The continuous maintenance of the leaderboard will further enhance its long-term impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces a valuable and timely benchmark that addresses a critical gap in the emerging field of agentic recommender systems. While the current implementation has some limitations (text-centric, single-agent focus), its public availability, comprehensive evaluation, and practical focus make it a significant contribution. The benchmarking framework and the insights derived provide a strong foundation for future research and development in this area. As such, a score of 7 reflects the paper's solid novelty, significance, and potential influence, with consideration for the identified weaknesses.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### HomeBench: Evaluating LLMs in Smart Homes with Valid and Invalid Instructions Across Single and Multiple Devices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19628v1)
- **Authors**: Silin Li, Yuhang Guo, Jiashu Yao, Zeming Liu, Haifeng Wang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have the potential to revolutionize smart home assistants by enhancing their ability to accurately understand user needs and respond appropriately, which is extremely beneficial for building a smarter home environment. While recent studies have explored integrating LLMs into smart home systems, they primarily focus on handling straightforward, valid single-device operation instructions. However, real-world scenarios are far more complex and often involve users issuing invalid instructions or controlling multiple devices simultaneously. These have two main challenges: LLMs must accurately identify and rectify errors in user instructions and execute multiple user instructions perfectly. To address these challenges and advance the development of LLM-based smart home assistants, we introduce HomeBench, the first smart home dataset with valid and invalid instructions across single and multiple devices in this paper. We have experimental results on 13 distinct LLMs; e.g., GPT-4o achieves only a 0.0% success rate in the scenario of invalid multi-device instructions, revealing that the existing state-of-the-art LLMs still cannot perform well in this situation even with the help of in-context learning, retrieval-augmented generation, and fine-tuning. Our code and dataset are publicly available at https://github.com/BITHLP/HomeBench.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HomeBench, a new dataset and benchmark for evaluating the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in smart home environments. HomeBench is unique because it includes both valid and invalid instructions across single and multiple devices, reflecting the complexities of real-world smart home interactions.  The authors evaluated 13 LLMs on HomeBench, showing that current models struggle, especially with invalid instructions and multi-device operation scenarios. They explore methods like in-context learning, retrieval-augmented generation, and fine-tuning to improve performance but find that significant gaps remain between model performance and practical application requirements.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of this work lies in the dataset itself, HomeBench. Existing datasets largely focus on valid, single-device commands. The inclusion of invalid instructions and multi-device scenarios is a significant and commendable advancement. The dataset directly addresses a gap in current LLM evaluation by reflecting realistic user errors and more complex interaction scenarios. This is a strength.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a critical bottleneck in current LLM capabilities for smart home applications – handling invalid instructions and complex multi-device interactions. Demonstrating the poor performance of existing LLMs on HomeBench reveals that practical smart home assistants require more sophisticated error-handling and coordination capabilities. By providing a challenging dataset, the paper encourages the development of more robust and reliable LLMs for these applications. This is a significant contribution that can have a real-world impact on smart home technology. The benchmark offers a valuable platform for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Dataset Creation:** The rigorous methodology for dataset creation, including the virtual home environment construction, instruction generation, and quality control is a strong point.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation of 13 LLMs provides a broad overview of the current state-of-the-art and identifies specific weaknesses.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The error analysis, identifying Unfaithfulness, In-context Attention Errors, and Key Errors, is crucial for understanding the challenges and guiding future improvements.
    *   **Exploration of Enhancement Techniques:** The experiments with ICL, RAG, and Fine-tuning provide valuable insights into potential methods for improving performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Language Coverage:** The dataset is English-only. While this is a common limitation, expanding the language coverage would increase the dataset's practical applicability.
    *   **Brand-Specific Device Differences:**  Acknowledging the lack of account for differences between brands in device instructions is a minor limitation that could be addressed in future iterations of the dataset. However, these aspects can be abstracted away.
    *   **Performance improvements with RAG:** The RAG results were particularly interesting since they weren't straight forward and highlight potential difficulties with integrating this aspect.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in LLMs for smart home applications by providing a valuable benchmark, identifying key challenges, and suggesting potential avenues for improvement. Specifically, it may spur work on more robust error handling, multi-device coordination mechanisms, and better contextual awareness in LLMs. It may also incentivize the creation of retrieval-augmented generation methods specifically tailored for smart-home implementations.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The impact of invalid instructions should not be ignored and requires specific attention. By providing a benchmark, this study sets the basis for future analysis. As many models are still at 0% with multi-device incorrect prompts, there is still much work to be done in improving on such prompts.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

HomeBench is a valuable and novel contribution to the field of LLMs for smart home applications. The dataset, while having minor limitations, addresses a significant gap in current benchmarks by including realistic user errors and multi-device scenarios. The comprehensive evaluation and error analysis provide valuable insights and direction for future research. Therefore, a score of 8 is appropriate due to the immediate and potential significance in driving more robust LLM development for smart home environments, which is a growing area of interest.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### DoctorAgent-RL: A Multi-Agent Collaborative Reinforcement Learning System for Multi-Turn Clinical Dialogue
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19630v1)
- **Authors**: Yichun Feng, Jiawei Wang, Lu Zhou, Yixue Li
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated excellent capabilities in the field of biomedical question answering, but their application in real-world clinical consultations still faces core challenges. Existing systems rely on a one-way information transmission mode where patients must fully describe their symptoms in a single round, leading to nonspecific diagnostic recommendations when complaints are vague. Traditional multi-turn dialogue methods based on supervised learning are constrained by static data-driven paradigms, lacking generalizability and struggling to intelligently extract key clinical information. To address these limitations, we propose DoctorAgent-RL, a reinforcement learning (RL)-based multi-agent collaborative framework that models medical consultations as a dynamic decision-making process under uncertainty. The doctor agent continuously optimizes its questioning strategy within the RL framework through multi-turn interactions with the patient agent, dynamically adjusting its information-gathering path based on comprehensive rewards from the Consultation Evaluator. This RL fine-tuning mechanism enables LLMs to autonomously develop interaction strategies aligned with clinical reasoning logic, rather than superficially imitating patterns in existing dialogue data. Notably, we constructed MTMedDialog, the first English multi-turn medical consultation dataset capable of simulating patient interactions. Experiments demonstrate that DoctorAgent-RL outperforms existing models in both multi-turn reasoning capability and final diagnostic performance, demonstrating practical value in assisting clinical consultations. https://github.com/JarvisUSTC/DoctorAgent-RL
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DoctorAgent-RL, a multi-agent collaborative reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in multi-turn clinical dialogue scenarios.  It addresses the limitations of existing medical LLMs, which often rely on passive, single-turn interactions, hindering their ability to gather sufficient information for accurate diagnoses. DoctorAgent-RL consists of three key components: a high-fidelity patient agent (simulated with an LLM), a clinician agent (refined using RL to optimize questioning strategies), and a consultation evaluator (providing multi-dimensional rewards based on diagnostic accuracy, information acquisition efficiency, and protocol compliance). The paper also introduces MTMedDialog, a new English multi-turn medical consultation dataset.  Experimental results demonstrate that DoctorAgent-RL outperforms existing models in both multi-turn reasoning and diagnostic accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the combination of multi-agent reinforcement learning and a specialized reward structure tailored to the specific requirements of clinical dialogue.  The construction of MTMedDialog also contributes to the field by providing a much-needed resource for training and evaluating such systems. Existing attempts to enhance medical LLMs through multi-turn capabilities have typically been supervised, which has limitations. The use of reinforcement learning to allow an LLM to proactively seek the optimal questioning path is a key differentiator.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to bridge the gap between the theoretical capabilities of LLMs and their practical application in real-world clinical consultations.  By addressing the limitations of single-turn interactions and supervised learning approaches, DoctorAgent-RL demonstrates a step forward towards AI-assisted diagnostic tools that can actively gather and reason with patient information. The performance gains compared to existing models further solidify this significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Innovative approach:**  The RL framework is a smart way to enable LLMs to learn efficient questioning strategies that go beyond mere imitation of existing dialogues.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The multi-dimensional reward structure of the consultation evaluator is well-designed and encourages the agent to balance diagnostic accuracy with other important factors such as information acquisition efficiency and adherence to clinical protocols.
    *   **Dataset contribution:**  The introduction of MTMedDialog provides a valuable resource for the community to train and evaluate multi-agent systems for clinical dialogue.
    *   **Performance gains:** The demonstrated improvements over existing models clearly showcase the effectiveness of DoctorAgent-RL.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simulation limitations:** While the simulated patient agent is a strength, it is important to acknowledge that it is still a simulation of a real patient interaction.  The complexity and nuance of human communication may not be fully captured, potentially limiting the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Limited scope:** The test dataset, although covers 8 categories of diseases, is still limited. Real-world medical consultation can involve significantly more complex conditions.
    *   **Lack of error analysis:** While the quantitative results are impressive, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth qualitative error analysis to understand the types of diagnostic errors that DoctorAgent-RL still makes and to identify areas for further improvement. Specifically, what specific type of clinical data or patient symptoms is the model still struggling with? What are the model's biases?
    *   **Scalability and cost:** Reinforcement learning can be computationally expensive. The paper could address the computational resources required for training and whether the proposed approach is scalable to larger and more complex LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the development of future AI-assisted diagnostic tools by promoting the adoption of multi-agent RL frameworks and emphasizing the importance of proactive information gathering. The MTMedDialog dataset is also likely to become a valuable resource for researchers in this field.

**Justification:**

The score is based on the paper's novel use of RL in medical dialogue to proactively seek the optimal questioning path for diagnosis. This is a significant advancement over existing systems which relies heavily on passive questioning or static data-driven paradigms. The performance gain is clearly demonstrated. However, there are limitations in scope and generalizability.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Segment First or Comprehend First? Explore the Limit of Unsupervised Word Segmentation with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19631v1)
- **Authors**: Zihong Zhang, Liqi He, Zuchao Li, Lefei Zhang, Hai Zhao, Bo Du
- **Abstract**: Word segmentation stands as a cornerstone of Natural Language Processing (NLP). Based on the concept of "comprehend first, segment later", we propose a new framework to explore the limit of unsupervised word segmentation with Large Language Models (LLMs) and evaluate the semantic understanding capabilities of LLMs based on word segmentation. We employ current mainstream LLMs to perform word segmentation across multiple languages to assess LLMs' "comprehension". Our findings reveal that LLMs are capable of following simple prompts to segment raw text into words. There is a trend suggesting that models with more parameters tend to perform better on multiple languages. Additionally, we introduce a novel unsupervised method, termed LLACA ($\textbf{L}$arge $\textbf{L}$anguage Model-Inspired $\textbf{A}$ho-$\textbf{C}$orasick $\textbf{A}$utomaton). Leveraging the advanced pattern recognition capabilities of Aho-Corasick automata, LLACA innovatively combines these with the deep insights of well-pretrained LLMs. This approach not only enables the construction of a dynamic $n$-gram model that adjusts based on contextual information but also integrates the nuanced understanding of LLMs, offering significant improvements over traditional methods. Our source code is available at https://github.com/hkr04/LLACA
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores unsupervised word segmentation using Large Language Models (LLMs). It introduces a "comprehend first, segment later" framework, leveraging LLMs to segment raw text into words and evaluate the models' semantic understanding capabilities based on the quality of segmentation. The paper finds that LLMs, especially those with larger parameter counts, are capable of following simple prompts to segment text across multiple languages. A novel unsupervised method, LLACA (Large Language Model-Inspired Aho-Corasick Automaton), is presented, combining the pattern recognition of Aho-Corasick automata with the insights gained from pre-trained LLMs. This approach dynamically constructs an n-gram model based on context and LLM understanding, outperforming traditional methods.  The study compares LLACA with various baseline methods and state-of-the-art unsupervised approaches on several multilingual datasets, demonstrating improved performance in terms of F-measure and efficiency. The paper emphasizes the advantages of LLACA in handling out-of-vocabulary words and adapting to different domains.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The "comprehend first, segment later" approach is a significant departure from traditional NLP pipelines, which typically treat word segmentation as a pre-processing step. Using LLMs in an unsupervised manner to guide word segmentation by leveraging their inherent semantic understanding and large knowledge base is a clever idea. LLACA builds upon this by incorporating the Aho-Corasick automaton. However, the novelty might be somewhat limited. While the combination is new, both LLMs for general NLP tasks and Aho-Corasick automatons for pattern matching are well-established techniques. A deeper theoretical justification of why this particular combination yields significant improvements (beyond empirical evidence) would strengthen the claim of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The research has the potential to impact how word segmentation is performed, especially in languages without clear word boundaries. Demonstrating that LLMs can effectively segment text without explicit training data has practical implications for low-resource languages and domain adaptation. LLACA offers a computationally efficient alternative to directly using LLMs for segmentation, which is significant given the resource demands of LLM inference. Furthermore, the paper provides insights into the semantic understanding capabilities of LLMs and how they can be harnessed for foundational NLP tasks. One of the great contributions is the insight into the capabilities of LMMs in recognizing and segmenting words in different languages.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of LLACA across multiple languages and datasets.
    *   The paper clearly presents the LLACA method and compares it with relevant baselines.
    *   The analysis of LLM performance, including discussions about hallucinations and the impact of pre-training data, is insightful.
    *   The approach handles Out-of-Vocabulary word problem well.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth theoretical analysis of LLACA and its relationship to existing word segmentation methods.
    *   While the paper addresses the computational cost of using LLMs directly, it does not delve into the resource requirements for constructing and maintaining the dynamic n-gram model.
    *   The code and datasets are made available.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in unsupervised word segmentation and the application of LLMs to foundational NLP tasks. Other researcher could explore LLACA as a baseline in the area of zero-shot word segmentation. The concept of using LLMs' inherent knowledge for tasks traditionally requiring supervised training has broad applicability and is likely to be explored in other NLP domains.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's novel approach, strong experimental results, and potential impact. However, the novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking, as it combines existing techniques. The paper presents practical and applicable research, but the level of theoretical depth could be improved. Still, the work provides a valuable contribution to the field by showing how LLMs can be utilized to improve unsupervised word segmentation, with a good balance of theoretical aspects and an empirical evaluation that validates its utility. Given the efficiency, transferrability, adaptability, and robustness in handling noisy input, LLACA is a viable solution. This justifies the assigned score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Faster and Better LLMs via Latency-Aware Test-Time Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19634v1)
- **Authors**: Zili Wang, Tianyu Zhang, Haoli Bai, Lu Hou, Xianzhi Yu, Wulong Liu, Shiming Xiang, Lei Zhu
- **Abstract**: Test-Time Scaling (TTS) has proven effective in improving the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) during inference. However, existing research has overlooked the efficiency of TTS from a latency-sensitive perspective. Through a latency-aware evaluation of representative TTS methods, we demonstrate that a compute-optimal TTS does not always result in the lowest latency in scenarios where latency is critical. To address this gap and achieve latency-optimal TTS, we propose two key approaches by optimizing the concurrency configurations: (1) branch-wise parallelism, which leverages multiple concurrent inference branches, and (2) sequence-wise parallelism, enabled by speculative decoding. By integrating these two approaches and allocating computational resources properly to each, our latency-optimal TTS enables a 32B model to reach 82.3% accuracy on MATH-500 within 1 minute and a smaller 3B model to achieve 72.4% within 10 seconds. Our work emphasizes the importance of latency-aware TTS and demonstrates its ability to deliver both speed and accuracy in latency-sensitive scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of traditional Test-Time Scaling (TTS) methods for Large Language Models (LLMs) that primarily focus on compute-optimal strategies based on token efficiency. The authors argue that in latency-sensitive scenarios, achieving optimal performance requires considering both token efficiency and throughput. They propose two key approaches to improve generation concurrency: branch-wise parallelism (leveraging multiple concurrent inference branches) and sequence-wise parallelism (using speculative decoding). By intelligently allocating computational resources between these two approaches, the authors demonstrate a latency-optimal TTS that balances speed and accuracy.  Experiments on MATH-500 and other datasets show that their method allows a 32B model to achieve 82.3% accuracy on MATH-500 within 1 minute, and a smaller 3B model to achieve 72.4% within 10 seconds.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in recognizing and explicitly addressing the latency aspect in TTS, moving beyond the traditional focus on token efficiency.  While branch-wise and sequence-wise parallelism are not entirely new concepts, the paper's contribution is in framing them as competing approaches for concurrency in a latency-constrained environment and providing a framework for optimizing resource allocation between them. The idea of *latency-optimal* TTS is a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its practical implications.  In many real-world applications, especially on resource-constrained devices or in interactive scenarios, latency is a crucial factor.  The paper's findings can guide the design of more efficient and responsive LLM inference systems. The empirical results, demonstrating significant improvements in accuracy within strict time limits, further underscore the significance.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of compute-optimal TTS in latency-sensitive scenarios.
    * **Well-Defined Solution:** The proposed approach of balancing branch-wise and sequence-wise parallelism provides a structured solution.
    * **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments on diverse datasets and model sizes support the claims.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The paper has direct relevance to real-world deployment of LLMs, particularly in latency-critical applications.
    * **Reproducibility:** The paper is associated with code release, encouraging reproducibility.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Generality of Configuration Tuning:** The "optimal" concurrency configuration heavily depends on specific models and datasets. While the paper provides insights into the trade-offs, the process of finding the best configuration may still require significant experimentation for new scenarios.  The appendix provides more details on configuration choices but could further explore strategies for efficient hyperparameter tuning.
    * **Incremental Improvement on Known Techniques:** While novel in its framing, the underlying techniques of branch-wise parallelism and speculative decoding are established. The contribution lies more in the careful orchestration and resource allocation than in breakthrough algorithmic innovation.
    * **Limited Exploration of Hybrid Approaches:** While the paper integrates branch-wise and sequence-wise parallelism, further investigation into more sophisticated hybrid approaches could be beneficial.
    * **Lack of Comparison With Model Compression/Distillation Specifically for Low-Latency Inference:** Model compression, distillation, or quantization are orthogonal approaches that also aim for latency reduction. The paper only compares to baseline TTS methods and does not consider whether these orthogonal approaches could provide a more competitive advantage regarding inference speed at the target accuracy.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in TTS by shifting the focus towards latency-aware strategies. The framework for balancing different forms of parallelism is a valuable contribution that can be built upon.  It may also encourage the development of more adaptive TTS methods that can dynamically adjust concurrency based on available resources and latency constraints.

**Justification of Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**. While the paper's technical contributions are somewhat incremental (building upon established techniques), its reframing of the TTS problem and the practical relevance of the solution warrant a higher rating. The empirical evidence is compelling, and the clarity of the problem definition and solution is commendable. However, the limitations related to the generalizability of the configuration tuning, limited consideration of orthogonal optimization techniques such as quantization, and the somewhat incremental nature of the algorithmic innovations prevent a higher score. A more thorough exploration of the challenges of deploying this latency-optimal approach in different resource-constrained environments would further strengthen the paper.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Interleaved Reasoning for Large Language Models via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19640v1)
- **Authors**: Roy Xie, David Qiu, Deepak Gopinath, Dong Lin, Yanchao Sun, Chong Wang, Saloni Potdar, Bhuwan Dhingra
- **Abstract**: Long chain-of-thought (CoT) significantly enhances large language models' (LLM) reasoning capabilities. However, the extensive reasoning traces lead to inefficiencies and an increased time-to-first-token (TTFT). We propose a novel training paradigm that uses reinforcement learning (RL) to guide reasoning LLMs to interleave thinking and answering for multi-hop questions. We observe that models inherently possess the ability to perform interleaved reasoning, which can be further enhanced through RL. We introduce a simple yet effective rule-based reward to incentivize correct intermediate steps, which guides the policy model toward correct reasoning paths by leveraging intermediate signals generated during interleaved reasoning. Extensive experiments conducted across five diverse datasets and three RL algorithms (PPO, GRPO, and REINFORCE++) demonstrate consistent improvements over traditional think-answer reasoning, without requiring external tools. Specifically, our approach reduces TTFT by over 80% on average and improves up to 19.3% in Pass@1 accuracy. Furthermore, our method, trained solely on question answering and logical reasoning datasets, exhibits strong generalization ability to complex reasoning datasets such as MATH, GPQA, and MMLU. Additionally, we conduct in-depth analysis to reveal several valuable insights into conditional reward modeling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "interleaved reasoning," a novel training paradigm for large language models (LLMs) using reinforcement learning (RL). Instead of the standard "think-answer" approach where LLMs complete the entire reasoning chain before generating an answer, interleaved reasoning encourages the model to alternate between thinking and answering, generating intermediate answers during the reasoning process. The authors propose a rule-based reward to incentivize correct intermediate steps, guiding the model towards correct reasoning paths. They demonstrate that this method reduces time-to-first-token (TTFT) by over 80% and improves accuracy on various datasets, while exhibiting strong generalization ability to complex reasoning tasks. They also discuss the dynamics of reward modeling and the importance of providing consistent, dense feedback.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of interleaving thinking and answering is reasonably novel within the context of LLM training, particularly for reinforcement learning approaches. The standard approach has focused on the final answer, while this explores the value of intermediate outputs. While techniques like decomposed prompting exist, applying RL in this specific manner is a distinct contribution. The rule-based reward system, while simple, demonstrates a practical way to guide models towards generating useful intermediate answers.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **TTFT Reduction:**  The significant reduction in TTFT is a valuable practical contribution.  Faster response times are crucial for improving the user experience in conversational AI and other interactive applications.
    *   **Accuracy Improvement:** The reported improvements in accuracy, specifically pass@1, are meaningful.  Enhanced reasoning capabilities are a core goal in LLM research.
    *   **Generalization:** The paper's finding that the method generalizes well to unseen tasks (MATH, GPQA, MMLU), even when trained only on question answering and logical reasoning datasets, is compelling and suggests robust learning.
    *   **Rule-Based Rewards:** Demonstrating that a simple, rule-based reward system is effective is important. This avoids the complexity and potential pitfalls (e.g., reward hacking) associated with training separate reward models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of the standard "think-answer" paradigm.
    *   **Well-Defined Method:** The interleaved reasoning approach and its implementation using RL and rule-based rewards are well-described.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The experiments cover a diverse set of datasets and RL algorithms, supporting the claims made by the authors.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies (e.g., delayed intermediate answers, different reward strategies) provide valuable insights into the importance of specific components.
    *   **Analysis:**  The analysis of the results, including intermediate reward distribution, reasoning pattern analysis, and comparison with Process Reward Models, is thorough and insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplicity of Rule-Based Reward:** While the simplicity of the rule-based reward is a strength, it could also be a limitation. More sophisticated reward functions, perhaps incorporating user feedback or learned metrics, might further improve performance.
    *   **Intermediate Answers Ground Truth:** The method currently relies on datasets with ground truth for intermediate answers, which may limit its applicability. Developing methods to generate or infer reliable intermediate answer targets in the absence of explicit ground truth could broaden the scope of the work.
    *   **Limited Model Scales:** Although the paper uses Qwen2.5 models with 1.5B and 7B parameters, it would be useful to test if the approach scales to larger models with tens or hundreds of billions of parameters. The benefits might be different at much larger scales.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   **Direction for RL-based LLM Training:**  The paper could influence future research on RL-based LLM training, shifting the focus towards leveraging intermediate outputs for better guidance and efficiency.
    *   **Interactive AI Applications:** The reduction in TTFT makes it more suitable for real-time and interactive AI applications, making this method useful for companies building those applications.
    *   **Practical Tool:** The code and results can be used directly by companies to build their own interleaving models and test performance.

*   **Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-supported training paradigm for LLMs with tangible benefits in terms of speed and accuracy. The thorough analysis and generalization results are particularly encouraging. While the reliance on ground truth for intermediate answers and the use of a relatively simple reward system are limitations, they do not detract significantly from the overall contribution. This paper has good influence, making this a good paper with an excellent score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### SynLogic: Synthesizing Verifiable Reasoning Data at Scale for Learning Logical Reasoning and Beyond
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19641v1)
- **Authors**: Junteng Liu, Yuanxiang Fan, Zhuo Jiang, Han Ding, Yongyi Hu, Chi Zhang, Yiqi Shi, Shitong Weng, Aili Chen, Shiqi Chen, Yunan Huang, Mozhi Zhang, Pengyu Zhao, Junjie Yan, Junxian He
- **Abstract**: Recent advances such as OpenAI-o1 and DeepSeek R1 have demonstrated the potential of Reinforcement Learning (RL) to enhance reasoning abilities in Large Language Models (LLMs). While open-source replication efforts have primarily focused on mathematical and coding domains, methods and resources for developing general reasoning capabilities remain underexplored. This gap is partly due to the challenge of collecting diverse and verifiable reasoning data suitable for RL. We hypothesize that logical reasoning is critical for developing general reasoning capabilities, as logic forms a fundamental building block of reasoning. In this work, we present SynLogic, a data synthesis framework and dataset that generates diverse logical reasoning data at scale, encompassing 35 diverse logical reasoning tasks. The SynLogic approach enables controlled synthesis of data with adjustable difficulty and quantity. Importantly, all examples can be verified by simple rules, making them ideally suited for RL with verifiable rewards. In our experiments, we validate the effectiveness of RL training on the SynLogic dataset based on 7B and 32B models. SynLogic leads to state-of-the-art logical reasoning performance among open-source datasets, surpassing DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B by 6 points on BBEH. Furthermore, mixing SynLogic data with mathematical and coding tasks improves the training efficiency of these domains and significantly enhances reasoning generalization. Notably, our mixed training model outperforms DeepSeek-R1-Zero-Qwen-32B across multiple benchmarks. These findings position SynLogic as a valuable resource for advancing the broader reasoning capabilities of LLMs. We open-source both the data synthesis pipeline and the SynLogic dataset at https://github.com/MiniMax-AI/SynLogic.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "SynLogic: Synthesizing Verifiable Reasoning Data at Scale for Learning Logical Reasoning and Beyond" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SYNLOGIC, a data synthesis framework and dataset designed to generate large-scale, diverse, and verifiable logical reasoning data.  The key idea is to create training data suitable for reinforcement learning (RL) to improve reasoning abilities in large language models (LLMs). The framework covers 35 distinct logical reasoning tasks, allowing controlled generation of data with adjustable difficulty.  Importantly, SYNLOGIC provides rule-based verifiers for each task, enabling RL with verifiable rewards. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of SYNLOGIC by RL training 7B and 32B language models, achieving state-of-the-art logical reasoning performance compared to other open-source datasets. Furthermore, they show that combining SYNLOGIC data with math and coding data improves training efficiency and generalization capabilities. The paper offers both the framework and the dataset as open-source resources.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of a comprehensive, diverse, and *verifiable* logical reasoning dataset specifically tailored for RL training of LLMs. While individual logic puzzles or tasks have been used before, the scale, diversity (35 tasks), and focus on verifiable rewards represent a significant contribution. The synthesis framework itself, with its difficulty control mechanisms and automated verifiers, is also a novel contribution.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from several factors. First, it addresses a gap in readily available, high-quality, and verifiable reasoning data, which is crucial for advancing LLM reasoning abilities through RL. Second, the results demonstrate that training on SYNLOGIC data leads to significant improvements in logical reasoning benchmarks and also enhances generalization to other domains like mathematics and coding.  The open-sourcing of the framework and dataset will likely spur further research and development in this area. The finding that logical reasoning training transfers to other domains, even improving their efficiency, is a valuable insight.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive and diverse dataset covering a wide range of logical reasoning tasks.
    *   Emphasis on data verifiability, making it suitable for RL with well-defined rewards.
    *   Controlled difficulty generation, enabling curriculum learning strategies.
    *   Empirical validation showing significant improvements in logical reasoning and generalization.
    *   Open-source availability, promoting reproducibility and future research.
    *   Clear and well-written paper with detailed experimental setup and results.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset, while diverse, relies on rule-based synthesis. This could limit the models' ability to generalize to *real-world* reasoning scenarios that are less structured and more ambiguous.
    *   The evaluation benchmarks, while standard, may not fully capture the complexity and nuances of human reasoning. The rule-based nature of the dataset might lead to overfitting on these specific benchmarks.
    *   The paper acknowledges the limitation that the difficulty tuning was not performed precisely for every task due to resource constraints. Optimizing the dataset for each task individually could potentially yield even better results.
    *   The comparison is largely against existing open-source models. A more thorough comparison against more advanced closed-source LLMs (after fine-tuning on SynLogic or training from scratch) would better demonstrate SynLogic's effectiveness in comparison to top performing reasoning LLMs.
*   **Potential Influence:** SYNLOGIC has the potential to become a valuable resource for the LLM community, particularly for researchers interested in improving reasoning abilities through RL. It could serve as a benchmark for evaluating different RL training strategies and model architectures. The framework itself could be extended to generate data for other reasoning domains or to incorporate more sophisticated data synthesis techniques. The reported benefits of mixed training (logic + math/coding) could inspire new approaches to training LLMs with improved generalization capabilities.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. While the paper presents a significant and novel contribution in the form of the SYNLOGIC framework and dataset, and the results are promising, there are several factors that prevent it from achieving a higher score. The limitations of the rule-based data synthesis and the evaluation benchmarks suggest that the improvements might not fully translate to real-world reasoning scenarios. While the mixed-training findings are valuable, they require more in-depth analysis to fully understand the underlying mechanisms. The lack of comparison against top-performing commercially available models also somewhat limits the significance. That said, the open-source nature of the work and the clear path for future research make it a solid contribution to the field. Its likely impact on the community and its solid empirical validation justify a score on the higher end of the range.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### MoESD: Unveil Speculative Decoding's Potential for Accelerating Sparse MoE
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19645v1)
- **Authors**: Zongle Huang, Lei Zhu, Zongyuan Zhan, Ting Hu, Weikai Mao, Xianzhi Yu, Yongpan Liu, Tianyu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success across many applications, with Mixture of Experts (MoE) models demonstrating great potential. Compared to traditional dense models, MoEs achieve better performance with less computation. Speculative decoding (SD) is a widely used technique to accelerate LLM inference without accuracy loss, but it has been considered efficient only for dense models. In this work, we first demonstrate that, under medium batch sizes, MoE surprisingly benefits more from SD than dense models. Furthermore, as MoE becomes sparser -- the prevailing trend in MoE designs -- the batch size range where SD acceleration is expected to be effective becomes broader. To quantitatively understand tradeoffs involved in SD, we develop a reliable modeling based on theoretical analyses. While current SD research primarily focuses on improving acceptance rates of algorithms, changes in workload and model architecture can still lead to degraded SD acceleration even with high acceptance rates. To address this limitation, we introduce a new metric 'target efficiency' that characterizes these effects, thus helping researchers identify system bottlenecks and understand SD acceleration more comprehensively. For scenarios like private serving, this work unveils a new perspective to speed up MoE inference, where existing solutions struggle. Experiments on different GPUs show up to 2.29x speedup for Qwen2-57B-A14B at medium batch sizes and validate our theoretical predictions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MoESD: Unveil Speculative Decoding's Potential for Accelerating Sparse MoE":

**Summary:**

The paper challenges the conventional wisdom that speculative decoding (SD) is ineffective for Mixture of Experts (MoE) models. The authors demonstrate that, under moderate batch sizes, SD can be *more* effective for MoEs than dense models, especially as MoE sparsity increases. The key insight is that moderate batch sizes can activate all experts without fully utilizing computational power, creating opportunities for SD to leverage spare resources. The paper introduces a new metric called "target efficiency" to quantify how workload and model architecture affect SD speedup, and builds a quantitative model to predict SD performance for MoEs, validated by experiments on various GPUs. They highlight private serving as a compelling use case.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core finding – that MoEs can benefit *more* from SD than dense models under specific conditions – is indeed a novel and counter-intuitive result. While SD is a well-established technique, applying it to MoEs, particularly with a focus on the relationship between batch size and sparsity, is a fresh perspective. The "target efficiency" metric is also a valuable contribution, offering a systematic way to analyze system bottlenecks in SD. It moves beyond just acceptance rate, which is a notable advancement.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact is significant. MoEs are increasingly prevalent in large language models, and efficient inference is crucial. If SD can truly provide greater acceleration for MoEs, it could be a valuable tool for improving the cost-effectiveness of these models, especially in resource-constrained or private settings. The work highlights an overlooked area of optimization. The target efficiency metric is a helpful systematic tool for future exploration in the SD and MoE space.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Counter-Intuitive Result:** The paper effectively demonstrates a non-obvious and potentially important finding.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The theoretical analysis and quantitative modeling are well-executed, providing a strong foundation for the claims.
    *   **New Metric:** Introduction of "target efficiency" is a practical contribution for analyzing and understanding the bottlenecks in SD.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments on different GPUs provide solid evidence to support the theoretical predictions.
    *   **Practical Use Case:** Highlighting private serving is a compelling application, given the growing need for secure and efficient LLM deployments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scope of SD:** The paper focuses on basic chain-like speculative decoding, which is helpful from a theoretical standpoint, but other SD techniques or more advanced tree-decoding techniques are left as future work.
    *   **Limited Model Diversity:** The experiments mainly center around the Qwen model family. While a good choice, exploring the findings across a broader range of MoE architectures (e.g., different routing mechanisms, number of experts, etc.) would strengthen the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Reliance on VLLM:** Using VLLM provides solid speed ups due to it's mature implementation of SD and MoE parallelisms, but relies on a single LLM inference software. This may introduce some bias to the experimental setting.
    *   **Parameter Fitting:** The need for parameter fitting in the modeling approach, while helpful for calibration, introduces some empiricism and may not fully capture the underlying dynamics. Further investigating the direct analytical solution is required.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the direction of research in SD and MoE, encouraging a closer look at the interplay between batch size, sparsity, and inference efficiency. It could also lead to the development of more optimized SD algorithms specifically designed for MoEs.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM inference. It challenges existing assumptions, provides a thorough analysis, and offers practical implications. While the scope could be broader, and has some limitations, the insights and the introduced systematic metric are valuable.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Token-Importance Guided Direct Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19653v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Ning, Lin Hai, Liu Yibo, Tian Baoliang, Liu Guoqing, Zhang Haijun
- **Abstract**: Ensuring that large language models (LLMs) generate outputs aligned with human preferences is important for safe and effective AI interactions. While Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) employs an implicit reward function to optimize the policy model, however, it and its related variants overlook the differential importance of individual tokens and are sensitive to judgment noise in preference datasets during generation. Although recent methods attempt to assess the important weight of tokens via probability prediction or simplistic weighting schemes, these evaluation methods are prone to biases and still cannot fully address these issues. To solve this problem, we propose the Token-Importance Guided Direct Preference Optimization (TI-DPO), which introduces two key innovations: the gradient-based token-importance weights that dynamically prioritize critical tokens, and a triple loss that explicitly guides model outputs to approach human-preferred responses and stay away from non-preferred responses. Experimental results show that TI-DPO achieves higher accuracy and stronger generative diversity, providing more stable and computationally efficient solutions compared with DPO and other RLHF methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Token-Importance Guided Direct Preference Optimization (TI-DPO), a novel approach to aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences.  TI-DPO addresses limitations in Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and related methods, specifically the equal weighting of tokens during optimization and sensitivity to noise in preference data. The core innovations are: 1) Gradient-based token importance weighting, which dynamically prioritizes critical tokens during learning. 2) A triplet loss function that explicitly guides model outputs towards preferred responses while simultaneously pushing them away from dis-preferred responses.  Experimental results demonstrate that TI-DPO achieves higher accuracy and stronger generative diversity compared to DPO and other Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) methods.  The paper also includes a theoretical analysis showing that TI-DPO achieves a tighter loss bound than DPO.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a sound and well-executed contribution to the field of LLM alignment. However, after rigorous evaluation, I believe a score of **7** is most appropriate. Here's a breakdown of the strengths and weaknesses justifying this score:

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Addressed:**  The paper tackles a valid and significant problem.  Standard DPO approaches often treat all tokens equally, which is unrealistic. Some tokens are far more crucial for conveying the intended meaning and preference alignment.
*   **Novelty of Approach:** The two key innovations (gradient-based token weighting and triplet loss) represent a meaningful advance over existing DPO variants. The gradient-based importance weighting is particularly appealing because it's data-driven and adapts to the specific context. The triplet loss appears sound, addressing a genuine shortcoming of simple "good vs. bad" preference comparisons.
*   **Theoretical Justification:**  The inclusion of a theoretical analysis provides strong support for TI-DPO. Proving a tighter loss bound compared to DPO is a valuable contribution.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments appear well-designed, using a range of benchmark datasets and comparison against relevant baselines (SFT, DPO, IPO, KTO, SimPO, TDPO, CPO, TPO, GRPO). The ablation studies confirm the necessity of both the token importance guidance and the triplet loss. The inclusion of results across different model sizes (LLaMA-3.2-3B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B-v0.3) strengthens the robustness of the claims.  The visualization of weight distributions and the case demo provide additional insights.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Innovation:** While the approach is novel and addresses a valid issue, it is still an incremental improvement on DPO rather than a groundbreaking paradigm shift. The core DPO framework remains, and TI-DPO enhances it with specific techniques. Many previous works, listed as citations, had also proposed token-level optimization, so novelty is somewhat limited
*   **Complexity:**  The approach introduces additional complexity in terms of computation (gradient calculations) and implementation compared to the base DPO method. While the paper claims computational efficiency, a more detailed analysis of the actual overhead would be beneficial.
*   **Limited Ablation Study Details:** The specific setups for the ablation study could be discussed further, such as whether learning rates were tuned for the specific ablation.
*   **Scope for more comprehensive experiments:** A more comprehensive examination of different types of prompts could provide insight into performance.
*   **Limited novelty of gradient-based weighting:** Gradient based attributions in NLP tasks are a common approach, and the paper could benefit by providing a more robust discussion of how the method differs from previous work.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by offering a more refined and effective way to align LLMs with human preferences. By addressing the limitations of DPO and similar methods, TI-DPO can lead to more reliable, safer, and more diverse LLM outputs. The findings have practical implications for developing AI systems that are better aligned with human values.

**Rationale for the Score:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution, addressing a real problem with a novel approach, supported by theoretical analysis and experimental validation. However, the incremental nature of the innovation, the increased complexity, and the limited detail in certain aspects of the experimental setup prevent it from achieving a higher score.  It's a strong, valuable contribution, but not a field-transforming breakthrough.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReDDiT: Rehashing Noise for Discrete Visual Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19656v1)
- **Authors**: Tianren Ma, Xiaosong Zhang, Boyu Yang, Junlan Feng, Qixiang Ye
- **Abstract**: Discrete diffusion models are gaining traction in the visual generative area for their efficiency and compatibility. However, the pioneered attempts still fall behind the continuous counterparts, which we attribute to the noise (absorbing state) design and sampling heuristics. In this study, we propose the rehashing noise framework for discrete diffusion transformer, termed ReDDiT, to extend absorbing states and improve expressive capacity of discrete diffusion models. ReDDiT enriches the potential paths that latent variables can traverse during training with randomized multi-index corruption. The derived rehash sampler, which reverses the randomized absorbing paths, guarantees the diversity and low discrepancy of the generation process. These reformulations lead to more consistent and competitive generation quality, mitigating the need for heavily tuned randomness. Experiments show that ReDDiT significantly outperforms the baseline (reducing gFID from 6.18 to 1.61) and is on par with the continuous counterparts with higher efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ReDDIT, a discrete visual generative model based on a rehashing noise framework.  The core idea is to address limitations in existing discrete diffusion models that stem from the simple mask (absorbing state) design and sampling heuristics. ReDDIT extends absorbing states by using randomized multi-index corruption during training, effectively enriching the potential paths that latent variables traverse.  A novel rehash sampler is derived that reverses this process, aiming for diversity and low discrepancy in generation.  The authors also refine the training objective with representation alignment (RepA) and explore connections to discrete flow matching for token refreshment. Experiments on ImageNet demonstrate that ReDDIT outperforms baseline methods and achieves performance comparable to continuous diffusion models with better efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a reasonably novel approach to discrete diffusion models. The idea of "rehashing noise" by using multiple indices and randomized corruption to overcome the limitations of a single absorbing state (mask token) is a fresh perspective. The derived rehash sampler and its connection to D3PM is also a positive aspect.  The modifications to the training objective (RepA) and connecting to discrete flow matching, while building upon existing work, are tailored to the proposed rehashing framework.

*   **Significance:** If the results hold up to further scrutiny, ReDDIT presents a significant step forward for discrete diffusion models. The claim of achieving comparable performance to continuous diffusion models with greater efficiency directly addresses a crucial gap in the field. The improved training objective and better sampling contribute practically to advancing discrete generative models. The exploration of rehashing noise potentially opens up new avenues for future research. However, as the authors say that this topic will be addressed on a future version of the essay, a lot is left to be discovered, as the authors also say that "while the DFM sampler offers a refinement mechanism via token-wise updates, it
    generally requires more steps to reach optimal results."

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Technically sound with derivations of the rehash sampler.
    *   Comprehensive experiments demonstrating improvements over baselines.
    *   Addresses the limitations of current discrete diffusion models with compelling insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on pre-trained tokenizers (IBQ, LlamaGen) somewhat limits the end-to-end novelty; the main contribution lies in the diffusion process itself. This is also due to the fact that this paper is primarily about images. This can be a problem, since it makes the model hard to reproduce, as well as the choice of tokenizer being subjective.
    *   While the experiments show quantitative gains, a more in-depth analysis of the learned latent spaces and the effect of different 'm' values would strengthen the paper. There can be a more critical evaluation regarding the experiments.
    *   The integration with discrete flow matching, while mentioned, is not fully explored in this version of the paper and requires more work.
    *   The claim of achieving comparable quality with better efficiency than continuous diffusion models needs further benchmarking against state-of-the-art DiTs, considering the different computational burdens (e.g., the tokenizer).

*   **Influence:** If ReDDIT's method is generalizable and scalable, it could significantly influence the development of discrete visual generative models, particularly in areas where efficiency and integration with language models are important. It could inspire new approaches to noise design and sampling strategies in discrete diffusion.

*   **Critical Justification:**
    The paper presents a solid contribution, particularly in addressing known weaknesses in discrete diffusion. The rehashing noise concept is novel and shows promising empirical results. While the reliance on pre-trained tokenizers and some unaddressed questions regarding DFM integration limit the impact, ReDDIT has the potential to become a significant benchmark and inspire further research in this area.
