### APE: A Data-Centric Benchmark for Efficient LLM Adaptation in Text Summarization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19912v1)
- **Authors**: Javier Marín
- **Abstract**: We present Adjacent Possible Exploration (APE), a simple yet effective method for adapting large language models to specific tasks using minimal computational resources. Unlike traditional fine-tuning that requires extensive compute, APE iteratively fine-tunes models on small, carefully selected data batches (200 examples), retaining only improvements. On news summarization, APE achieves 40 percent BLEU improvement using just a T4 GPU in 60 minutes, matching or exceeding more complex methods like LoRA while remaining conceptually simple. Our approach is particularly valuable for researchers and practitioners with limited computational resources. We provide open-source code and demonstrate APE's effectiveness through both automatic metrics and human evaluation. While inspired by evolutionary theory's "adjacent possible", APE's core insight has a very practical application: small, iterative data perturbations can efficiently guide LLMs toward task-specific performance without expensive retraining.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "APE: A Data-Centric Benchmark for Efficient LLM Adaptation in Text Summarization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adjacent Possible Exploration (APE), a data-centric method for efficiently adapting large language models (LLMs) to specific tasks like text summarization. APE iteratively fine-tunes a model on small, carefully selected batches of data, retaining updates only if they improve performance. This approach, inspired by evolutionary theory's "adjacent possible," aims to achieve task-specific performance with minimal computational resources, avoiding the high costs of full fine-tuning and the potential limitations of parameter-efficient methods like adapters or prompt tuning.  The authors demonstrate APE's effectiveness on the CNN/DailyMail dataset, showing improvements in BLEU and other metrics compared to the baseline T5-base model. They also conduct human evaluations and ablation studies to analyze the impact of perturbation size. The paper highlights the balance between adaptability and stability offered by APE, particularly in resource-constrained settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Resource Efficiency:** APE presents a compelling alternative to computationally intensive LLM adaptation techniques, which is particularly important for researchers and practitioners with limited resources. The paper provides concrete evidence of performance gains using a standard T4 GPU, making the method accessible.
*   **Data-Centric Approach:** In line with the emerging trend of data-centric AI, APE shifts the focus from model architecture to data selection and refinement. This can be a more sustainable and often overlooked strategy for improving performance.
*   **Evolutionary Inspiration:** The connection to the "adjacent possible" concept provides a unique and intriguing framework for understanding the iterative improvement process. While presented as a heuristic, it adds a layer of theoretical grounding that is beyond typical ad hoc optimization strategies.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents quantitative results (BLEU, ROUGE, BERTScore, Perplexity), ablation studies (perturbation size), and human evaluations, providing a multi-faceted assessment of APE's performance.
*   **Clarity:** The paper does a decent job explaining the method and its inspiration.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide open-source code, improving the reproducibility of their findings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** The idea of iterative fine-tuning with a selection process isn't entirely new. Active learning, for example, uses similar principles. The novelty primarily lies in the specific application to LLMs, the "adjacent possible" framing, and the systematic evaluation.
*   **Heuristic Nature:** While the "adjacent possible" provides inspiration, the actual implementation of APE relies on several hyperparameters (batch size, learning rate, improvement threshold) that require tuning. The connection between the theory and the specific hyperparameter choices isn't always clear or directly derived from the model based on adjacent possible theory. The selection criteria of data batches is quite vague.
*   **Generalizability:** The evaluation is primarily focused on the CNN/DailyMail dataset and the T5-base model. The extent to which APE generalizes to other tasks, datasets, and models remains an open question. The limitation to T5-base due to numerical instability points to a practical challenge in scaling the approach.
*   **Comparison with Existing Methods:** While the paper compares APE to CL, AL, and parameter-efficient tuning, the comparisons are often based on estimated baselines from other papers. A direct comparison on the same dataset with optimized hyperparameters for each method would strengthen the argument.
*   **Human Evaluation Weaknesses:** The lack of inter-rater reliability measures like Cohen's kappa is a concern, as it makes the evaluation more difficult to interpret.

**Significance:**

APE has the potential to be a valuable tool for adapting LLMs in resource-constrained environments. The data-centric approach and the evolutionary inspiration offer a fresh perspective on LLM fine-tuning. However, more work is needed to address the limitations related to generalizability, comparison with existing methods, and hyperparameter tuning. Its potential impact rests on whether the community can further develop and refine APE, making it a robust and easily applicable technique.

**Score:** 6.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and potentially impactful method for LLM adaptation. Its strengths lie in resource efficiency, data-centricity, and the evolutionary inspiration. However, its limitations, especially the limited novelty when compared with existing active learning approaches, hyperparameter tuning requirements, restricted scope of evaluation, comparison against estimated base lines instead of the original models, and reliance on a single dataset, reduce its overall impact. The heuristic nature of the method and the lack of a fully developed theoretical underpinning also detract from its overall significance. Given the potential but also significant existing limitations, a score of 6.5 reflects the paper's promising but still early-stage contribution to the field. While the inspiration is strong, the practical implementation needs significantly more refinement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Enigmata: Scaling Logical Reasoning in Large Language Models with Synthetic Verifiable Puzzles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19914v1)
- **Authors**: Jiangjie Chen, Qianyu He, Siyu Yuan, Aili Chen, Zhicheng Cai, Weinan Dai, Hongli Yu, Qiying Yu, Xuefeng Li, Jiaze Chen, Hao Zhou, Mingxuan Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs), such as OpenAI's o1 and DeepSeek's R1, excel at advanced reasoning tasks like math and coding via Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), but still struggle with puzzles solvable by humans without domain knowledge. We introduce Enigmata, the first comprehensive suite tailored for improving LLMs with puzzle reasoning skills. It includes 36 tasks across seven categories, each with 1) a generator that produces unlimited examples with controllable difficulty and 2) a rule-based verifier for automatic evaluation. This generator-verifier design supports scalable, multi-task RL training, fine-grained analysis, and seamless RLVR integration. We further propose Enigmata-Eval, a rigorous benchmark, and develop optimized multi-task RLVR strategies. Our trained model, Qwen2.5-32B-Enigmata, consistently surpasses o3-mini-high and o1 on the puzzle reasoning benchmarks like Enigmata-Eval, ARC-AGI (32.8%), and ARC-AGI 2 (0.6%). It also generalizes well to out-of-domain puzzle benchmarks and mathematical reasoning, with little multi-tasking trade-off. When trained on larger models like Seed1.5-Thinking (20B activated parameters and 200B total parameters), puzzle data from Enigmata further boosts SoTA performance on advanced math and STEM reasoning tasks such as AIME (2024-2025), BeyondAIME and GPQA (Diamond), showing nice generalization benefits of Enigmata. This work offers a unified, controllable framework for advancing logical reasoning in LLMs. Resources of this work can be found at https://seed-enigmata.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ENIGMATA, a new suite designed to improve the logical reasoning skills of large language models (LLMs). ENIGMATA comprises three components: a diverse and scalable synthetic puzzle dataset (ENIGMATA-Data) with 36 tasks across seven categories, an evaluation benchmark (ENIGMATA-Eval), and a training recipe (ENIGMATA-Model) tailored for these puzzle tasks. A key feature of ENIGMATA-Data is its generator-verifier design, which allows for controllable difficulty, scalable generation, and automated evaluation, making it suitable for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR).  The authors demonstrate that models trained with ENIGMATA, such as Qwen2.5-32B-ENIGMATA, achieve state-of-the-art results on puzzle reasoning benchmarks and even exhibit improved performance on advanced math and STEM reasoning tasks when scaling to larger models like Seed1.5-Thinking.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in several aspects. First, it provides a *comprehensive*, unified, and controllable framework for advancing logical reasoning in LLMs, addressing a gap in existing puzzle datasets, which often lack either diversity, scalability, or controllability.  The generator-verifier framework is a solid design choice. Secondly, the demonstration of *transfer* of learning from these synthetic puzzles to more complex STEM reasoning problems is a significant and potentially surprising finding. This justifies using relatively simple logic puzzles as a stepping stone towards more complex reasoning skills. However, it would have been insightful to investigate *why* these puzzles help with STEM and mathematical problems.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the demonstrated ability to enhance LLMs' logical reasoning capabilities and achieve superior performance on puzzle benchmarks, including ARC-AGI.  The scaling results showing gains on AIME and GPQA are particularly promising.  If the results hold up to further scrutiny and independent replication, it suggests a novel avenue for improving LLMs' broader reasoning abilities without relying solely on domain-specific knowledge or data. The availability of ENIGMATA-Data as a synthetic dataset could also significantly lower the barrier for researchers seeking to improve logical reasoning in LLMs.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Suite:** ENIGMATA provides a complete ecosystem for puzzle reasoning research, including dataset generation, evaluation, and a training recipe.
*   **Scalability and Controllability:** The generator-verifier design enables scalable data generation with controllable difficulty, crucial for effective RLVR training.
*   **Transfer Learning:** The paper demonstrates that training on ENIGMATA can improve performance on more complex reasoning tasks like math and STEM.
*   **State-of-the-Art Results:** The trained models achieve competitive results on puzzle reasoning benchmarks.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and structured, clearly explaining the methodology and results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Ablation:** While the paper includes ablation studies, further investigations into the optimal data mixing strategies and the specific contributions of each component of ENIGMATA would be valuable. For instance, it's unclear *which* puzzles are most important for transfer learning.
*   **Synthetic Data Bias:**  There is the potential for overfitting the particular format of the synthetic data in ENIGMATA, which might lead to less generalizable reasoning abilities. While the gains on math benchmarks suggest this isn't a major issue, it's something to be aware of.
*   **Relatively Small Models:** The primary model used, Qwen2.5-32B, while solid, is not cutting edge, limiting the potential impact.  The results with larger models (Seed1.5-Thinking) are enticing but require further exploration.
*   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** Although the results highlight that the synthetic puzzles enhance general capabilities, the lack of detailed theoretical justifications/analysis for this observation is a major gap of the paper. Why and how puzzle enhance the reasoning abilities for other tasks like math?

**Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence research in several ways:

*   It provides a valuable resource (ENIGMATA) for improving LLMs' logical reasoning skills.
*   It encourages the use of synthetic data for training LLMs, especially in domains where data is scarce or difficult to obtain.
*   It promotes the RLVR paradigm for training LLMs on reasoning tasks.
*   It sparks further research into the transfer learning abilities of LLMs trained on puzzle tasks.
* It offers a controllable way to build curriculum learning for advanced reasoning tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7.5**. While the paper's contribution is significant due to its comprehensiveness, scalability, demonstration of transfer learning, and state-of-the-art performance, several limitations temper my enthusiasm. The ablation studies could be more thorough, and further analysis is necessary to fully understand the mechanisms behind the observed transfer learning effects. Moreover, the dependence on a relatively small model, as well as limited theoretical backing also reduce the overall value of this paper. Nevertheless, the research presented in this paper is indeed sound, the findings are novel and it offers a good platform for future work.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TCP: a Benchmark for Temporal Constraint-Based Planning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19927v1)
- **Authors**: Zifeng Ding, Sikuan Yan, Zhangdie Yuan, Xianglong Hu, Fangru Lin, Andreas Vlachos
- **Abstract**: Temporal reasoning and planning are essential capabilities for large language models (LLMs), yet most existing benchmarks evaluate them in isolation and under limited forms of complexity. To address this gap, we introduce the Temporal Constraint-based Planning (TCP) benchmark, that jointly assesses both capabilities. Each instance in TCP features a naturalistic dialogue around a collaborative project, where diverse and interdependent temporal constraints are explicitly or implicitly expressed, and models must infer an optimal schedule that satisfies all constraints. To construct TCP, we first generate abstract problem prototypes that are paired with realistic scenarios from various domains and enriched into dialogues using an LLM. A human quality check is performed on a sampled subset to confirm the reliability of our benchmark. We evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs and find that even the strongest models struggle with TCP, highlighting its difficulty and revealing limitations in LLMs' temporal constraint-based planning abilities. We analyze underlying failure cases, open source our benchmark, and hope our findings can inspire future research.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary, critical evaluation, and score for the provided paper, "TCP: a Benchmark for Temporal Constraint-Based Planning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TCP, a novel benchmark designed to evaluate the temporal reasoning and planning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in a collaborative project planning context. TCP instances are presented as naturalistic dialogues where participants discuss scheduling under various explicit and implicit temporal constraints. The benchmark generation process involves creating abstract problem prototypes, enriching them with realistic scenarios using LLMs, and performing human quality checks. The authors evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs on TCP, revealing their limitations in handling complex temporal constraints, particularly in asynchronous scheduling and time zone reasoning.  The benchmark and analysis aim to highlight key failure modes and inspire future research in improving LLMs' temporal planning abilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable and relatively novel contribution by focusing on *jointly* assessing temporal reasoning and planning, unlike existing benchmarks that often isolate these capabilities or present them under simplified conditions. The use of naturalistic dialogue is also a key aspect of the novelty.  While there are existing works on temporal reasoning and planning, their combination and the specific design of the benchmark (dialogue format, specific constraint types) fills a gap in the current landscape. The creation of a benchmark is itself a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant because temporal reasoning and planning are essential for real-world applications of LLMs.  The findings reveal that even advanced LLMs struggle with complex temporal constraints, indicating a substantial gap in their capabilities. By identifying specific failure modes (asynchronous planning, time zone errors, sensitivity to surface form), the paper provides valuable insights for future research directions.  The open-source nature of the benchmark enhances its potential impact by allowing other researchers to use it for evaluating and improving their models.  The construction pipeline is also an important practical contribution, by making possible the scaling of datasets in a way that remains reliable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark Design:** TCP is well-designed, incorporating a diverse set of temporal constraints and realistic scenarios. The two categories (short/long) enable the assessment of different reasoning skills.
    *   **Rigorous Construction and Validation:** The benchmark generation process combines automated prototype generation, LLM-based scenario enrichment, and human quality checks, ensuring high-quality and reliable instances.
    *   **Clear Identification of Failure Modes:** The analysis provides clear insights into the specific challenges that LLMs face in temporal constraint-based planning.
    *   **Reproducibility and Open-Source:**  The public availability of the benchmark promotes reproducibility and further research in the area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scale (Agents and Tasks):** The benchmark is limited to two agents and three tasks across all instances. This might restrict the assessment of model performance on more complex, large-scale planning problems. While this limitation has a clear purpose, it should be acknowledge that it is a limitation.
    *   **No Task-Specific Fine-Tuning:** The models are evaluated without task-specific fine-tuning, which might underestimate their full potential. While the zero-shot setting highlights core capabilities, some fine-tuning would provide a more complete picture.
    *   **LLM-generated scenarios:** There might be unintended biases because scenarios are generated by a different LLM.

*   **Potential Influence:** TCP has the potential to become a valuable resource for the LLM research community, driving advancements in temporal reasoning and planning capabilities.  The identified failure modes can guide the development of new model architectures, training strategies, and prompting techniques.  The insights can also be used to improve existing models.

**Score: 7.8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a novel benchmark that directly addresses the gap in evaluating joint temporal reasoning and planning in LLMs. The rigorous design, validation, and analysis provide valuable insights into the limitations of current models and identify key areas for improvement. While there are some limitations regarding the scale of the problem and lack of task-specific fine-tuning, the overall impact of the work is substantial.  The benchmark is well-motivated, thoughtfully constructed, and addresses a critical area in LLM research. The limitations should be considered, but they are not debilitating to its significance. For this reasons, the paper is not a 9 or a 10, since there are some areas to improve, but is much better than a 5 or a 6 because it identifies an important gap and provides new data, analysis, and a new resource for the community.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Subtle Risks, Critical Failures: A Framework for Diagnosing Physical Safety of LLMs for Embodied Decision Making
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19933v1)
- **Authors**: Yejin Son, Minseo Kim, Sungwoong Kim, Seungju Han, Jian Kim, Dongju Jang, Youngjae Yu, Chanyoung Park
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for decision making in embodied agents, yet existing safety evaluations often rely on coarse success rates and domain-specific setups, making it difficult to diagnose why and where these models fail. This obscures our understanding of embodied safety and limits the selective deployment of LLMs in high-risk physical environments. We introduce SAFEL, the framework for systematically evaluating the physical safety of LLMs in embodied decision making. SAFEL assesses two key competencies: (1) rejecting unsafe commands via the Command Refusal Test, and (2) generating safe and executable plans via the Plan Safety Test. Critically, the latter is decomposed into functional modules, goal interpretation, transition modeling, action sequencing, enabling fine-grained diagnosis of safety failures. To support this framework, we introduce EMBODYGUARD, a PDDL-grounded benchmark containing 942 LLM-generated scenarios covering both overtly malicious and contextually hazardous instructions. Evaluation across 13 state-of-the-art LLMs reveals that while models often reject clearly unsafe commands, they struggle to anticipate and mitigate subtle, situational risks. Our results highlight critical limitations in current LLMs and provide a foundation for more targeted, modular improvements in safe embodied reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and evaluation of the paper "Subtle Risks, Critical Failures: A Framework for Diagnosing Physical Safety of LLMs for Embodied Decision Making."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical issue of physical safety in Large Language Models (LLMs) used for embodied decision-making. Recognizing that current safety evaluations often rely on coarse-grained success rates, the authors introduce SAFEL, a framework designed to systematically evaluate and diagnose safety failures. SAFEL assesses two key competencies: (1) the ability to reject unsafe commands (Command Refusal Test) and (2) the capability to generate safe and executable plans (Plan Safety Test). The Plan Safety Test is further decomposed into functional modules: Goal Interpretation, Transition Modeling, and Action Sequencing, enabling fine-grained diagnosis. To support the framework, the authors introduce EMBODYGUARD, a new PDDL-grounded benchmark containing both overtly malicious and subtly hazardous instructions. Experiments on 13 state-of-the-art LLMs reveal that while models often reject clearly unsafe commands, they struggle with subtle, situational risks, indicating limitations in their physical reasoning abilities. The paper highlights the need for targeted improvements in safe embodied reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Problem Formulation:** The paper tackles a vital and increasingly relevant problem: the physical safety of LLMs in embodied agents. As LLMs are integrated into real-world applications, the consequences of safety failures become more significant.
*   **Systematic Framework (SAFEL):** The proposed SAFEL framework provides a structured and comprehensive approach to evaluating and diagnosing safety failures. The modular design allows for pinpointing specific weaknesses in the LLM's reasoning process. The decomposition of the "Plan Safety Test" into Goal Interpretation, Transition Modeling, and Action Sequencing is particularly valuable for identifying the root cause of errors.
*   **Comprehensive Benchmark (EMBODYGUARD):** The introduction of EMBODYGUARD significantly contributes to the field. The benchmark is designed to cover a range of scenarios, including both overtly malicious and subtly hazardous instructions. The use of PDDL grounding provides a formal basis for evaluation and allows for simulation-based execution. The dataset construction methodology, involving LLM generation, symbolic verification, and human annotation, is rigorous.
*   **Detailed Experimental Analysis:** The experiments on 13 LLMs offer valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of current models. The analysis reveals that even state-of-the-art models struggle with subtle risks and exhibit limitations in transition modeling and action sequencing.
*   **Practical Implications:** The results have practical implications for the deployment of LLMs in safety-critical applications. The paper highlights the need for more targeted, modular improvements in safe embodied reasoning.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Embodied Actions:** While iGibson and the Behavior benchmark are solid foundations, the set of embodied actions considered remains somewhat restricted and oriented towards household tasks.  It is a reasonable starting point, but expanding to more complex physical interactions (e.g., manipulating industrial equipment) would increase the breadth of impact.
*   **Scalability of PDDL verification:** The manual validation step, while rigorous, limits the scalability of EMBODYGUARD. Developing more efficient, automated methods for semantic verification would be beneficial.  Also, creating PDDL representations is an expertise in and of itself, and this may hinder the rapid adoption and community contribution to this work.
*   **Performance metrics focus:** The paper focuses heavily on recall metrics (detecting risks). Although justified, a more comprehensive analysis including precision (minimizing false positives) would offer a more balanced view of the model performance.
*   **Limited discussion of mitigation strategies**: While the paper is diagnostic, it would have been impactful to explore potential methods that may improve the safety of LLMs given the current weaknesses.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper demonstrates novelty through its holistic approach to physical safety assessment in LLMs. The combination of the SAFEL framework and the EMBODYGUARD benchmark represents a significant contribution to the field of embodied AI safety. The rigorous experimental analysis offers insights for researchers working on improving LLM safety and reliability. Although prior works consider safety concerns, the emphasis on *physical* safety combined with a diagnostic framework makes this contribution quite novel.

**Overall Score & Justification:**

Score: 8/10

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and rigorously executed study with significant contributions to the field of embodied AI safety. The SAFEL framework and EMBODYGUARD benchmark represent valuable tools for evaluating and diagnosing safety failures in LLMs. The paper is well-written, technically sound, and addresses a relevant and important problem.

The main limitations are related to the scope of embodied actions and scalability of benchmark verification, and a limited discussion of mitigation strategies. While these limitations do not diminish the core contributions of the paper, they do point to directions for future research. Given the impact this work has, however, a score of 8 indicates the significant value of this contribution to the field of AI safety, a field of increasingly critical importance.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### ALAS: Measuring Latent Speech-Text Alignment For Spoken Language Understanding In Multimodal LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19937v1)
- **Authors**: Pooneh Mousavi, Yingzhi Wang, Mirco Ravanelli, Cem Subakan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are widely used in Spoken Language Understanding (SLU). Recent SLU models process audio directly by adapting speech input into LLMs for better multimodal learning. A key consideration for these models is the cross-modal alignment between text and audio modalities, which is a telltale sign as to whether or not LLM is able to associate semantic meaning to audio segments. While various methods exist for fusing these modalities, there is no standard metric to evaluate alignment quality in LLMs. In this work, we propose a new metric, ALAS (Automatic Latent Alignment Score). Our study examines the correlation between audio and text representations across transformer layers, for two different tasks (Spoken Question Answering and Emotion Recognition). We showcase that our metric behaves as expected across different layers and different tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ALAS (Automatic Latent Alignment Score), a new metric for evaluating the quality of speech-text alignment in multimodal Large Language Models (LLMs). The approach analyzes the cross-modal cosine similarity between latent representations of audio and text at different layers of the LLM. It uses a monotonic alignment search algorithm and compares the resulting alignment path with a reference alignment obtained from Whisper.  The authors demonstrate ALAS on two different tasks: Spoken Question Answering (SQA) using the LibriSQA dataset and Emotion Recognition (ER) using the IEMOCAP dataset, analyzing the alignment patterns of the SALMONN-7B and Qwen2-Audio-Instruct-7B models. The paper shows that ALAS behaves as expected, reflecting the varying levels of alignment across different layers and tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in proposing a *metric* (ALAS) for evaluating speech-text alignment within LLMs, where previous work has primarily focused on alignment strategies and model architectures. The idea of using cross-modal cosine similarity and comparing against a reference alignment isn't entirely new in the broad field of multimodal learning, but its application and formalization as a metric for analyzing *latent* alignment within LLMs is a contribution. Existing alignment methods (like MFA or CTC) are either phoneme-level or require explicit labels, whereas ALAS seeks to measure latent alignment without manual annotation. This is a significant difference.
* **Significance:**  The significance rests on providing a tool for understanding how well LLMs are learning cross-modal relationships between speech and text.  A reliable alignment metric allows researchers to: (1) better understand the internal workings of multimodal LLMs, (2) compare different model architectures or training strategies in terms of their alignment capabilities, and (3) guide the development of models with improved cross-modal understanding. While the paper focuses on SQA and ER tasks, the metric could potentially be applied to other SLU tasks. The paper also shows how alignment patterns change across layers and tasks which could assist with network design.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Methodology:** The ALAS pipeline is well-defined and explained, making it relatively straightforward to implement and apply to other models.
    * **Empirical Evaluation:** The application of ALAS to two different tasks and two LLMs provides a solid foundation for demonstrating its utility and applicability. The observed trends (e.g., increasing alignment in later layers for SQA, decreasing alignment in later layers for ER) align with expectations and provide initial validation of the metric.
    * **Ease of Application:** The simplicity of calculating cross-modal similarities makes it a low barrier of entry metric.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Whisper:** The reference alignment is derived from Whisper, which itself is not perfect. This introduces a potential bias, and the metric's accuracy is inherently limited by the quality of Whisper's alignments.  While Whisper is a good baseline, an alternative approach or an ensemble of multiple alignment methods could be more robust. The authors acknowledge that Whisper provides an "approximation" of ground truth alignment which introduces a level of error.
    * **Limited Scope:**  The evaluation is confined to two specific LLMs (SALMONN-7B and Qwen2-Audio-Instruct-7B) and two tasks. While this provides a starting point, it is not clear how well ALAS would generalize to other models with different architectures or to more complex SLU tasks. Showing how ALAS score correlates with downstream task performance would strengthen the argument that is a meaningful metric.
    * **Interpretation of Alignment:** While the paper presents interesting alignment pattern observations, the implications of these patterns need more exploration. For instance, how can understanding these patterns inform the design of future multimodal LLMs.
    * **Absolute Score Meaning:** The paper doesn't offer a clear interpretation of what constitutes a "good" or "bad" ALAS score. It primarily focuses on relative comparisons between layers and tasks. This makes it difficult to use ALAS as an absolute measure of alignment quality.
* **Potential Influence:** ALAS has the potential to become a standard metric for evaluating speech-text alignment in LLMs, especially as multimodal LLMs become more prevalent. However, its widespread adoption will depend on its ease of use, reliability, and generalizability across different models and tasks. Future work exploring the correlation between ALAS scores and downstream task performance is required.

**Justification for Score:**

ALAS makes a reasonable contribution to the field of multimodal LLMs by addressing the need for a metric to quantify speech-text alignment. The paper offers novelty by focusing on latent alignment, however has limitations due to its dependency on Whisper and limited generalizability. Future work will have to correlate ALAS score with downstream task performance and establish absolute quality ratings.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Which Data Attributes Stimulate Math and Code Reasoning? An Investigation via Influence Functions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19949v1)
- **Authors**: Siqi Kou, Qingyuan Tian, Hanwen Xu, Zihao Zeng, Zhijie Deng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable reasoning capabilities in math and coding, often bolstered by post-training on the chain-of-thoughts (CoTs) generated by stronger models. However, existing strategies for curating such training data predominantly rely on heuristics, limiting generalizability and failing to capture subtleties underlying in data. To address these limitations, we leverage influence functions to systematically attribute LLMs' reasoning ability on math and coding to individual training examples, sequences, and tokens, enabling deeper insights into effective data characteristics. Our Influence-based Reasoning Attribution (Infra) uncovers nontrivial cross-domain effects across math and coding tasks: high-difficulty math examples improve both math and code reasoning, while low-difficulty code tasks most effectively benefit code reasoning. Based on these findings, we introduce a simple yet effective dataset reweighting strategy by flipping task difficulty, which doubles AIME24 accuracy from 10\% to 20\% and boosts LiveCodeBench accuracy from 33.8\% to 35.3\% for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Moreover, our fine-grained attribution reveals that the sequence-level exploratory behaviors enhance reasoning performance in both math and code, and the token-level influence patterns are distinct for math and code reasoning: the former prefers natural language logic connectors and the latter emphasizes structural syntax.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Which Data Attributes Stimulate Math and Code Reasoning? An Investigation via Influence Functions":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the attributes of training data that are most effective in improving the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in math and coding.  The authors introduce "Infra," an Influence-based Reasoning Attribution method, which uses influence functions to attribute an LLM's reasoning ability to specific training examples, sequences, and tokens. Key findings include: 1) High-difficulty math examples benefit both math and code reasoning, while easier code benefits code reasoning most. 2) Exploratory behaviors (searching for alternative solutions in reasoning traces) enhance both math and code reasoning. 3) Math CoTs (Chain-of-Thoughts) prioritize logical connectors, while code CoTs emphasize structural syntax. Based on these findings, the authors propose a dataset reweighting strategy (flipping difficulty) that improves performance on math and code benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The application of influence functions to analyze the data characteristics affecting math and code reasoning in LLMs is moderately novel. While influence functions have been used in LLMs before, particularly in pre-training analysis, their application to the post-training phase for specifically dissecting the interplay between data difficulty, reasoning styles, and task performance is a good contribution. The fine-grained token-level analysis also offers some novel insights.

*   **Significance:** The paper provides valuable insights into *what* types of data are most effective for training LLMs for reasoning tasks. This is significant because current data curation strategies often rely on heuristics that may be sub-optimal. The concrete findings, such as the cross-domain benefits of high-difficulty math and the importance of exploratory behaviors, are useful for practitioners. The dataset reweighting strategy provides empirical evidence of the practical application of their findings. The discovery about token-level preferences, too, is significant.
* **Strengths:**
    * Rigorous methodology: The paper employs a well-established methodology, leveraging influence functions and performing comprehensive experiments. The ablation studies on exploration behaviors and the examination of token-level influences provide robust support for their claims.
    * Empirical Validation: The paper provides solid empirical results demonstrating the effectiveness of their proposed dataset reweighting strategy, leading to improved performance on standard benchmarks.
    * Actionable insights: The paper offers practical guidelines for data curation and model training, enabling researchers and practitioners to improve the reasoning abilities of LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    * Hessian Approximation:  The approximation of the Hessian matrix using EK-FAC, while necessary for computational feasibility, introduces potential inaccuracies. The paper could benefit from a discussion of the limitations of this approximation and its potential impact on the results.
    * Limited Scope of Tasks: The study focuses primarily on math and coding reasoning. While these tasks are important, the findings may not generalize to other reasoning domains, such as commonsense reasoning or natural language understanding.
    * Overthinking claim: While the paper notes its results challenge existing research that classifies exploration as detrimental, more should have been said about why and in what specific contexts the authors' results are different from previous ones.

*   **Impact and Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the data curation strategies for LLMs used in reasoning tasks. The emphasis on data difficulty, cross-domain effects, and exploratory behaviors provides a more nuanced understanding of effective training data.  The findings can potentially inform the design of more targeted and efficient training datasets.
* **Justification for Score:**
    The paper offers a solid contribution with some novelty in its application of influence functions and its insightful findings regarding data attributes and reasoning performance. The empirical validation is strong, and the insights are actionable. However, the reliance on Hessian approximation, the limited scope of tasks, and the failure to better compare the results with those of overthinking classification detract somewhat from the overall impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multimodal Reasoning Agent for Zero-Shot Composed Image Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19952v1)
- **Authors**: Rong-Cheng Tu, Wenhao Sun, Hanzhe You, Yingjie Wang, Jiaxing Huang, Li Shen, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: Zero-Shot Composed Image Retrieval (ZS-CIR) aims to retrieve target images given a compositional query, consisting of a reference image and a modifying text-without relying on annotated training data. Existing approaches often generate a synthetic target text using large language models (LLMs) to serve as an intermediate anchor between the compositional query and the target image. Models are then trained to align the compositional query with the generated text, and separately align images with their corresponding texts using contrastive learning. However, this reliance on intermediate text introduces error propagation, as inaccuracies in query-to-text and text-to-image mappings accumulate, ultimately degrading retrieval performance. To address these problems, we propose a novel framework by employing a Multimodal Reasoning Agent (MRA) for ZS-CIR. MRA eliminates the dependence on textual intermediaries by directly constructing triplets, <reference image, modification text, target image>, using only unlabeled image data. By training on these synthetic triplets, our model learns to capture the relationships between compositional queries and candidate images directly. Extensive experiments on three standard CIR benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. On the FashionIQ dataset, our method improves Average R@10 by at least 7.5\% over existing baselines; on CIRR, it boosts R@1 by 9.6\%; and on CIRCO, it increases mAP@5 by 9.5\%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of Zero-Shot Composed Image Retrieval (ZS-CIR), where the goal is to retrieve target images given a reference image and a modifying text, without relying on annotated training data.  The authors argue that existing methods which generate synthetic target text using Large Language Models (LLMs) as an intermediate step introduce error propagation.  To overcome this, they propose a novel framework called Multimodal Reasoning Agent (MRA) for ZS-CIR.  MRA directly constructs triplets of <reference image, modification text, target image> using unlabeled image data, eliminating the reliance on intermediate textual representations.  The MRA leverages a pre-trained VLM to extract image embeddings and identify moderately similar image pairs, followed by a context-aware semantic reasoning strategy utilizing an MLLM to generate modification texts. The VLM is then fine-tuned using an InfoNCE loss computed via token-level maximum cosine similarity over the synthesized triplets. The authors present extensive experiments on three standard CIR benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core idea of bypassing the intermediate text generation step in ZS-CIR and directly constructing triplets from unlabeled data is indeed a novel contribution. The use of a multi-modal reasoning agent (MRA) based on MLLM to generate modification text, conditioned on both reference and target image *captions*, is also a non-trivial extension.  However, some elements build upon existing trends (e.g., using LLMs for synthetic data generation in zero-shot learning, adapting VLMs for retrieval tasks). The paper claims the use of InfoNCE loss computed via token-level maximum cosine similarity and provides a theoretical analysis, but the novelty of this aspect is less pronounced, as other works have explored similar ideas in different contexts.
* **Significance:**  The results presented are significant in terms of performance improvements over existing ZS-CIR methods on standard benchmarks, particularly on FashionIQ.  This suggests that the proposed method effectively captures the relationships between compositional queries and candidate images. The theoretical analysis of the loss function adds rigor and justification for the approach, which increases the confidence in this method. The elimination of error propagation provides potential for a better solution in the long run for this problem.

* **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined and explained methodology with a rationale behind each step.
    *   Strong experimental results on multiple benchmarks.
    *   Rigorous theoretical analysis of the proposed loss function.
    *   Thorough ablation studies to validate the contribution of each component.
    *   The use of image captions to condition the generation of text modification, improving the text quality and therefore, the CIR performance

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty, while present, is arguably incremental in the context of recent advancements in VLMs and LLMs for zero-shot learning. Some ideas have been used in other contexts.
    *   The method's performance is contingent on the quality and capabilities of the underlying MLLM, which could limit its applicability in resource-constrained environments or with less capable models. The dependence on unlabeled ImageNet-1k limits its transfer capabilities to unseen datasets.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations related to potential biases inherited from the MLLM and imbalances in the unlabeled dataset, but doesn't propose concrete solutions beyond mentioning potential future work.
    *   The computational cost associated with the complex image processing and MLLM generation needs to be further examined.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field of ZS-CIR by shifting the focus away from relying heavily on intermediate text generation and towards directly learning relationships between visual and textual modalities using self-supervised approaches. The method provides a competitive baseline and a potentially more robust solution to the error propagation problem.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:** The paper presents a solid and well-executed approach to ZS-CIR with demonstrably superior performance. It addresses a crucial weakness in existing methods and provides a novel solution. While the method's novelty isn't groundbreaking (building on existing VLM and LLM trends), the results are compelling and provide new insights into the problem. The clear methodology, strong experimental validation, and theoretical analysis further strengthen the paper. However, the limitations related to MLLM dependence, data biases, lack of robustness and transfer capabilities, and computational cost prevent it from achieving a higher score. While incremental, it's a significant and beneficial improvement in a rapidly evolving field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### An Explainable Diagnostic Framework for Neurodegenerative Dementias via Reinforcement-Optimized LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19954v1)
- **Authors**: Andrew Zamai, Nathanael Fijalkow, Boris Mansencal, Laurent Simon, Eloi Navet, Pierrick Coupe
- **Abstract**: The differential diagnosis of neurodegenerative dementias is a challenging clinical task, mainly because of the overlap in symptom presentation and the similarity of patterns observed in structural neuroimaging. To improve diagnostic efficiency and accuracy, deep learning-based methods such as Convolutional Neural Networks and Vision Transformers have been proposed for the automatic classification of brain MRIs. However, despite their strong predictive performance, these models find limited clinical utility due to their opaque decision making. In this work, we propose a framework that integrates two core components to enhance diagnostic transparency. First, we introduce a modular pipeline for converting 3D T1-weighted brain MRIs into textual radiology reports. Second, we explore the potential of modern Large Language Models (LLMs) to assist clinicians in the differential diagnosis between Frontotemporal dementia subtypes, Alzheimer's disease, and normal aging based on the generated reports. To bridge the gap between predictive accuracy and explainability, we employ reinforcement learning to incentivize diagnostic reasoning in LLMs. Without requiring supervised reasoning traces or distillation from larger models, our approach enables the emergence of structured diagnostic rationales grounded in neuroimaging findings. Unlike post-hoc explainability methods that retrospectively justify model decisions, our framework generates diagnostic rationales as part of the inference process-producing causally grounded explanations that inform and guide the model's decision-making process. In doing so, our framework matches the diagnostic performance of existing deep learning methods while offering rationales that support its diagnostic conclusions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for the differential diagnosis of neurodegenerative dementias (Alzheimer's, Frontotemporal Dementia subtypes, and normal aging). The framework combines a pipeline to convert 3D T1-weighted brain MRIs into textual radiology reports, and leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to assist clinicians with differential diagnosis based on these reports. The core innovation lies in using Reinforcement Learning (specifically Group Relative Policy Optimization, GRPO) to incentivize diagnostic reasoning in the LLM, allowing it to generate structured diagnostic rationales grounded in neuroimaging findings, without the need for supervised reasoning traces.  The authors demonstrate that this approach achieves diagnostic performance comparable to existing deep learning methods while providing causally grounded explanations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty stems from the specific combination of techniques:
    *   **MRI to Text Conversion:** While MRI to text is not new, the modular pipeline focused on clinical fidelity and interpretable intermediate steps adds value. It avoids end-to-end deep learning which often obscures the critical anatomical details. The framework's modularity allows for future improvements and verification of individual components.
    *   **GRPO for Diagnostic Reasoning:**  The application of GRPO to fine-tune LLMs for medical diagnosis, particularly without relying on labeled reasoning traces, is a strong contribution. It allows for the emergence of reasoning capabilities in smaller models.
    *   **Causally Grounded Explanations:** The framework generates diagnostic rationales as part of the inference process, offering a more transparent and justifiable decision-making process compared to post-hoc explainability methods.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in medical AI – the "black box" nature of deep learning models.  The framework's potential to improve diagnostic accuracy and transparency in neurodegenerative dementias is clinically relevant. If validated in real-world settings, this approach could increase clinician trust and adoption of AI-based diagnostic tools. The focus on differential diagnosis between FTD subtypes, a challenging clinical problem, further enhances the paper's significance. The ability to achieve such results with relatively small 8B parameter models, by using reinforcement learning, is significant in its own right.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The paper presents a well-defined and complete pipeline, from MRI to final diagnosis.
    *   **Clinically Relevant Focus:** Addresses a specific, challenging, and important clinical problem.
    *   **Explainability:** Provides transparent diagnostic rationales with grounding in neuroimaging findings.
    *   **Use of LLMs Effectively:** Leverages the reasoning capabilities of LLMs through strategic prompting and reinforcement learning.
    *   **Quantitative Results:** Demonstrates competitive diagnostic accuracy compared to existing deep learning methods.
    *   **Clear Evaluation:** Presents a thorough experimental setup and analysis of results, including comparisons to existing LLMs and deep learning methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Synthetic Report Dependency:** The framework relies on synthetic radiology reports. While the authors argue for their clinical fidelity, the framework's robustness depends on the quality of these reports. Validation with real-world reports is important.
    *   **Dataset Size:** The dataset size, although decent, is relatively limited for deep learning applications.  The availability of training data, particularly for rare FTD subtypes, might be a limiting factor.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the authors mention general thresholds, further investigation into the framework's ability to generalize to different datasets, scanners, and patient populations is important.
    *   **Clinical Validation:** The paper lacks clinical validation by expert neuroradiologists and neurologists. While diagnostic performance is measured, expert review of the generated rationales and diagnoses would strengthen the claims.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper provides a pathway for developing more transparent and clinically useful AI systems in medical imaging. The use of reinforcement learning to fine-tune LLMs for diagnostic reasoning could be applied to other medical domains. By addressing the explainability challenge, this work encourages clinician trust and acceptance of AI-based diagnostic tools.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper has some limitations, the novel combination of techniques, the significant clinical problem addressed, the focus on explainability, and the competitive diagnostic performance justify a relatively high score.  The framework provides a strong foundation for future research. However, the limitations regarding dataset size, generalizability, clinical validation, and reliance on synthetic reports prevent it from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### DCG-SQL: Enhancing In-Context Learning for Text-to-SQL with Deep Contextual Schema Link Graph
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19956v1)
- **Authors**: Jihyung Lee, Jin-Seop Lee, Jaehoon Lee, YunSeok Choi, Jee-Hyong Lee
- **Abstract**: Text-to-SQL, which translates a natural language question into an SQL query, has advanced with in-context learning of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, existing methods show little improvement in performance compared to randomly chosen demonstrations, and significant performance drops when smaller LLMs (e.g., Llama 3.1-8B) are used. This indicates that these methods heavily rely on the intrinsic capabilities of hyper-scaled LLMs, rather than effectively retrieving useful demonstrations. In this paper, we propose a novel approach for effectively retrieving demonstrations and generating SQL queries. We construct a Deep Contextual Schema Link Graph, which contains key information and semantic relationship between a question and its database schema items. This graph-based structure enables effective representation of Text-to-SQL samples and retrieval of useful demonstrations for in-context learning. Experimental results on the Spider benchmark demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach, showing consistent improvements in SQL generation performance and efficiency across both hyper-scaled LLMs and small LLMs. Our code will be released.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, novelty and significance evaluation, and justified score for the paper "DCG-SQL: Enhancing In-Context Learning for Text-to-SQL with Deep Contextual Schema Link Graph".

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of effectively using in-context learning for Text-to-SQL translation, specifically focusing on how to select relevant demonstrations for Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors argue that existing methods, which primarily rely on question embedding similarity, don't adequately capture the crucial role of the database schema in determining SQL query structure and semantics. To overcome this, they propose DCG-SQL, a novel approach that constructs a Deep Contextual Schema Link Graph. This graph jointly represents the question and the relevant database schema items, capturing contextual relationships between them. The method consists of: 1) pruning irrelevant schema items using a classification model and 2) linking question tokens to schema items via attention scores. A graph encoder is then trained using contrastive learning to create embeddings that capture relationships, facilitating the retrieval of useful demonstration. The method is evaluated on the Spider benchmark and its variants, showing improved performance and efficiency across hyper-scaled and small LLMs compared to existing approaches.

**Novelty and Significance:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the construction of the Deep Contextual Schema Link Graph and its use in demonstration retrieval. While prior works have attempted to incorporate schema information into Text-to-SQL in-context learning, this paper is differentiated by the attention scores derived from cross-encoder model which classify relevant schema item, to perform schema linking. This is a more nuanced approach than simple word matching. The graph representation, combined with contrastive learning for graph embeddings, is also a notable contribution for demonstration retrieval. Further, combining all above with the proposed automatic-CoT prompting is a significant innovation. The pruning step is also a plus since it mitigates effects of noises or irrelevant information in the DB schema.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is threefold:

    1.  **Performance improvement:** The experimental results on the Spider benchmark and its variants demonstrate consistent and substantial improvements in SQL generation accuracy, especially for smaller LLMs. This is important because it makes Text-to-SQL more accessible and practical in resource-constrained settings.
    2.  **Improved demonstration retrieval:** The ablation studies provide evidence that the proposed graph-based retrieval method is more effective than existing approaches at identifying relevant demonstrations, suggesting a more targeted in-context learning process.
    3.  **Practicality:** The method does not require training a large encoder-decoder model, relying instead on in-context learning and a moderately sized graph encoder. This makes it easier to implement and adapt compared to traditional Text-to-SQL approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   The problem is well-motivated, addressing a clear limitation of existing Text-to-SQL in-context learning methods.
*   The proposed DCG-SQL is technically sound and well-explained.
*   The experimental evaluation is thorough, with comparisons to strong baselines and ablation studies.
*   The results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach, particularly for smaller LLMs.
*   Qualitative examples provide insights into how the method selects relevant schema items and demonstrations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scope of "deep contextual" relationship**: The contextual information used for schema linking, while better than simple word matching, is still based on attention scores from a classification model. A deeper semantic understanding or integration of external knowledge sources could further improve the representation.
*   **Dependency on Spacy and NLTK for relations in the graph**: It will be useful to include studies on how different libraries affect the performance of edge generation in schema linking graph for reproducibility.
*   **The computational overhead of the schema pruning and the graph construction**. While the approach avoids training a large encoder-decoder, the pre-processing steps might introduce additional overhead, although paper argues that the latency is reduced. A detailed analysis of the pre-processing time compared to end-to-end methods would be beneficial.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the above, I assign a score of **8** to this paper.

*   The paper presents a novel and well-engineered approach to Text-to-SQL in-context learning, effectively addressing the limitations of existing methods.
*   The use of deep contextual schema link graph is innovative, and the experimental results demonstrate its significant impact on performance, especially for smaller LLMs.
*   The paper contributes a practical and efficient method for Text-to-SQL, with the potential to make it more accessible in resource-constrained settings.
*   While there are some limitations, such as the reliance on self-attention for schema linking and potential computational overhead in pre-processing, these are relatively minor compared to the overall contribution. The paper is well-written, and the results are clearly presented and analyzed. The novelty and significance are above average but with room for improvements. This suggests a high degree of potential influence on future research in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### UltraVSR: Achieving Ultra-Realistic Video Super-Resolution with Efficient One-Step Diffusion Space
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19958v1)
- **Authors**: Yong Liu, Jinshan Pan, Yinchuan Li, Qingji Dong, Chao Zhu, Yu Guo, Fei Wang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have shown great potential in generating realistic image detail. However, adapting these models to video super-resolution (VSR) remains challenging due to their inherent stochasticity and lack of temporal modeling. In this paper, we propose UltraVSR, a novel framework that enables ultra-realistic and temporal-coherent VSR through an efficient one-step diffusion space. A central component of UltraVSR is the Degradation-aware Restoration Schedule (DRS), which estimates a degradation factor from the low-resolution input and transforms iterative denoising process into a single-step reconstruction from from low-resolution to high-resolution videos. This design eliminates randomness from diffusion noise and significantly speeds up inference. To ensure temporal consistency, we propose a lightweight yet effective Recurrent Temporal Shift (RTS) module, composed of an RTS-convolution unit and an RTS-attention unit. By partially shifting feature components along the temporal dimension, these two units collaboratively facilitate effective feature propagation, fusion, and alignment across neighboring frames, without relying on explicit temporal layers. The RTS module is integrated into a pretrained text-to-image diffusion model and is further enhanced through Spatio-temporal Joint Distillation (SJD), which improves temporal coherence while preserving realistic details. Additionally, we introduce a Temporally Asynchronous Inference (TAI) strategy to capture long-range temporal dependencies under limited memory constraints. Extensive experiments show that UltraVSR achieves state-of-the-art performance, both qualitatively and quantitatively, in a single sampling step.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the UltraVSR paper:

**Summary:**

The UltraVSR paper proposes a novel framework for real-world video super-resolution (VSR) that aims to achieve high visual quality and temporal coherence with a focus on computational efficiency. The key innovations include:

1.  **Degradation-aware Restoration Schedule (DRS):** A one-step reconstruction process that directly maps low-resolution (LR) to high-resolution (HR) video frames by estimating a degradation factor.  This bypasses the iterative denoising steps common in diffusion models, leading to faster inference.
2.  **Recurrent Temporal Shift (RTS) Module:** A lightweight module incorporating shifted feature components to capture inter-frame dependencies without relying on heavy temporal layers like 3D convolutions or attention mechanisms, enhancing temporal consistency.
3.  **Spatio-temporal Joint Distillation (SJD):** A training strategy using dual temporal regularizers to jointly optimize for realistic details and temporal coherence.
4.  **Temporally Asynchronous Inference (TAI):** A memory-efficient inference scheme processing mini-batches independently before propagating temporal information.

The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art results on multiple synthetic and real-world benchmarks, emphasizing both qualitative improvements and significant gains in inference speed compared to existing diffusion-based VSR approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates solid novelty in several aspects, particularly the DRS and RTS modules. The DRS strategy to move to one step diffusion is a substantial departure from standard multi-step methods. The RTS module offers a novel way to achieve temporal consistency without explicit temporal layers, which is computationally advantageous. The combination of SJD and TAI for training and inference is also noteworthy.
*   **Significance:** The potential impact of UltraVSR is substantial. The ability to perform high-quality video super-resolution with significantly reduced computational cost could democratize access to this technology, enabling its use in resource-constrained environments and real-time applications. This would be particularly valuable for content creation, video streaming, and other areas.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Efficiency:**  The focus on one-step diffusion dramatically improves inference speed compared to other diffusion-based methods. The lightweight nature of the RTS module and the TAI inference strategy further contribute to efficiency.
    *   **Visual Quality:**  The results show clear qualitative improvements in terms of texture detail and temporal coherence. Quantitative metrics on several datasets support this claim.
    *   **Real-world applicability:** The focus on real-world degradations makes this research practically relevant, differentiating it from methods that primarily address idealized settings.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on pre-trained model:** UltraVSR is based on a pre-trained Stable Diffusion model, limiting its generalizability to other domains and potentially introducing biases. The architecture requires pretrained large text-to-image diffusion models.
    *   **Limited Long-Range Temporal Modeling:** The RTS, although effective, is inherently limited in capturing long-range temporal dependencies due to its sliding window approach. More advanced methods might be needed for videos with complex and prolonged dynamics.
    *   **Ablation study clarity:** While comprehensive, the ablation study could benefit from more direct comparisons of the computational cost of different components, providing more clarity on the trade-offs between performance and efficiency.

*   **Potential Influence:** UltraVSR could significantly influence the direction of VSR research by demonstrating the feasibility of high-quality, efficient diffusion-based techniques. Its approach is likely to inspire further investigations into methods to reduce the computational burden of diffusion models while maintaining or improving performance. Its contributions should also be useful for researchers tackling similar problems such as video frame interpolation.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes significant contributions to the field of video super-resolution by achieving impressive results with a novel and efficient approach. The one-step diffusion, RTS module, SJD, and TAI contribute uniquely to this performance. While the reliance on a pretrained model is a minor drawback, the focus on efficiency and its practical implications are compelling.
The paper's potential to influence the development of more efficient and practical VSR techniques is considerable.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### MiniLongBench: The Low-cost Long Context Understanding Benchmark for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19959v1)
- **Authors**: Zhongzhan Huang, Guoming Ling, Shanshan Zhong, Hefeng Wu, Liang Lin
- **Abstract**: Long Context Understanding (LCU) is a critical area for exploration in current large language models (LLMs). However, due to the inherently lengthy nature of long-text data, existing LCU benchmarks for LLMs often result in prohibitively high evaluation costs, like testing time and inference expenses. Through extensive experimentation, we discover that existing LCU benchmarks exhibit significant redundancy, which means the inefficiency in evaluation. In this paper, we propose a concise data compression method tailored for long-text data with sparse information characteristics. By pruning the well-known LCU benchmark LongBench, we create MiniLongBench. This benchmark includes only 237 test samples across six major task categories and 21 distinct tasks. Through empirical analysis of over 60 LLMs, MiniLongBench achieves an average evaluation cost reduced to only 4.5% of the original while maintaining an average rank correlation coefficient of 0.97 with LongBench results. Therefore, our MiniLongBench, as a low-cost benchmark, holds great potential to substantially drive future research into the LCU capabilities of LLMs. See https://github.com/MilkThink-Lab/MiniLongBench for our code, data and tutorial.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MiniLongBench: The Low-cost Long Context Understanding Benchmark for Large Language Models."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MiniLongBench, a compressed version of the existing LongBench benchmark for evaluating the long context understanding (LCU) capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  The authors observed redundancy within LongBench, leading to high evaluation costs (time and computational resources). MiniLongBench addresses this by using a data compression method tailored for long-text data, which exploits the sparse nature of information within these texts. They prune LongBench, creating a smaller benchmark with only 237 test samples across six major task categories. The paper demonstrates that MiniLongBench achieves significant cost reduction (down to 4.5% of original) while maintaining high correlation (average rank correlation coefficient of 0.97) with LongBench results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Problem Addressed:**  The paper directly tackles the real-world issue of high evaluation costs associated with LCU benchmarks. This is especially relevant as LLMs grow in size and complexity, making thorough evaluation resource-intensive.
    *   **Effective Compression Method:** The proposed data compression method appears to be both simple and effective. By leveraging the observation that long texts are often sparse in meaningful information, the authors create a more efficient benchmark.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper presents extensive experimental results, analyzing over 60 LLMs.  The high correlation between MiniLongBench and LongBench results is compelling, suggesting that the compressed benchmark provides a reliable approximation of full-scale LCU performance.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology, experimental setup, and results.  The figures and tables are informative and support the claims made.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide access to their code, data, and tutorials, enhancing the reproducibility of their work, which is a critical aspect of scientific research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty of Compression Technique:** While the *application* of the compression is novel in this context, the underlying techniques (PCA, logistic regression, k-means clustering) are well-established. The key novelty lies in their combination and adaptation to the specific characteristics of LCU benchmarks.
    *   **Dependence on Existing LLM Performance:** The data compression method relies on the performance records of existing LLMs to identify and retain representative test samples. This creates a potential bias toward the capabilities of the LLMs used in the compression process. New LLMs that differ significantly from the training set could face inaccurate assessment.
    *   **Generalizability Concerns:**  Although the authors claim that LLMs not used in training can be evaluated accurately, this needs further validation across LLMs with significantly different architectures or training datasets. The observed Sp of 0.97 is for LLMs with similar model architecture.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper focuses primarily on empirical validation. A more in-depth theoretical analysis of the compression method's properties, its potential biases, and limitations would strengthen the work. A theoretical bound on approximation quality of MiniLongBench would be good.
    *   **Dependence on hyperparameters:** Several hyperparameters, such as the compression ratio *p* and the dimension *d* for PCA reduction, needs to be specified before MiniLongBench can be constructed, as evidenced by Section 5.
*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper makes a practical contribution to the field by providing a low-cost alternative for evaluating LCU capabilities of LLMs. This can accelerate research in this area by reducing the barrier to entry for researchers with limited computational resources.
    *   The work highlights the importance of data efficiency in LLM evaluation. The identification of redundancy in existing benchmarks encourages further exploration of methods for creating more concise and informative evaluation datasets.
    *   If the benchmark can be effectively validated across a broader range of LLMs with drastically different model architecture, it has the potential to become a widely adopted tool in the LLM community.
    *   Furthermore, the idea of using existing models' performance records to distill training datasets could be applicable for other complex LLM-related tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper addresses an important practical problem and presents compelling empirical results, the limited novelty of the underlying techniques and the potential biases in the compression method prevent it from receiving a higher score. The work demonstrates a clever application of existing methods to achieve a valuable outcome, but it lacks significant theoretical contributions or groundbreaking insights. Further analysis to mitigate bias and more thorough testing across different LLM architectures are needed.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Limits of Preference Data for Post-Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19964v1)
- **Authors**: Eric Zhao, Jessica Dai, Pranjal Awasthi
- **Abstract**: Recent progress in strengthening the capabilities of large language models has stemmed from applying reinforcement learning to domains with automatically verifiable outcomes. A key question is whether we can similarly use RL to optimize for outcomes in domains where evaluating outcomes inherently requires human feedback; for example, in tasks like deep research and trip planning, outcome evaluation is qualitative and there are many possible degrees of success. One attractive and scalable modality for collecting human feedback is preference data: ordinal rankings (pairwise or $k$-wise) that indicate, for $k$ given outcomes, which one is preferred. In this work, we study a critical roadblock: preference data fundamentally and significantly limits outcome-based optimization. Even with idealized preference data (infinite, noiseless, and online), the use of ordinal feedback can prevent obtaining even approximately optimal solutions. We formalize this impossibility using voting theory, drawing an analogy between how a model chooses to answer a query with how voters choose a candidate to elect. This indicates that grounded human scoring and algorithmic innovations are necessary for extending the success of RL post-training to domains demanding human feedback. We also explore why these limitations have disproportionately impacted RLHF when it comes to eliciting reasoning behaviors (e.g., backtracking) versus situations where RLHF has been historically successful (e.g., instruction-tuning and safety training), finding that the limitations of preference data primarily suppress RLHF's ability to elicit robust strategies -- a class that encompasses most reasoning behaviors.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the limits of using preference data for post-training large language models (LLMs). It argues that even with idealized conditions (infinite, noiseless, online preference data), reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) can fail to achieve optimal or even approximately optimal solutions. The authors formalize this impossibility result using voting theory, drawing an analogy between how a model chooses an answer and how voters elect a candidate. They find that preference data limitations disproportionately impact eliciting reasoning behaviors, where robust strategies (like backtracking) are penalized, contrasting with the relative success of RLHF in instruction-tuning and safety training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its theoretical analysis of the inherent limitations of preference data for outcome-based optimization in LLMs. While prior work has noted challenges with RLHF, this paper provides a more fundamental, impossibility-type result. The connection to voting theory is insightful and offers a new perspective on the problem. It goes beyond merely stating that RLHF has problems, and identifies *why* it encounters such issues.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they question the reliance on preference data as the primary means for improving LLMs in complex, qualitative tasks. If preference data fundamentally limits achievable outcomes, it motivates the exploration of alternative feedback mechanisms, grounded human scoring, or algorithmic innovations. The analysis of reasoning behaviors and robustness adds practical value.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a clear formal model of LLM post-training using circuits.
    *   The analogy to voting theory provides a powerful framework for analysis.
    *   The impossibility result is mathematically rigorous.
