    *   The analysis differentiating reasoning behavior from other tasks (instruction tuning) is insightful.
    *   The paper provides actionable suggestions, such as explicitly coarsen feedback or use a combination of preference and cardinal data.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The formal model, while general, is still an abstraction of real-world LLM post-training. It relies on a circuits-based view that may not fully capture the complexities of LLM behavior.
    *   The theoretical nature of the results limits immediate practical solutions. While suggestions are provided, they require further empirical validation.
    *   The experiments on backtracking are helpful but limited in scope.
    *   The voting theory analogy, while insightful, could be more deeply explored, perhaps with different voting rules or preference structures.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence the field by:
    *   Shifting focus away from solely relying on scaling preference data.
    *   Motivating research into alternative feedback modalities.
    *   Inspiring more theoretical analysis of LLM training.
    *   Guiding the development of new algorithms that account for the limitations of preference data.
    *   Highlighting the need for different strategies for training reasoning vs. other LLM capabilities.

*   **Overall:** The paper is a valuable contribution that provides a cautionary tale about the limits of preference data in LLM training. The theoretical framework is well-developed, and the findings have important implications for future research directions. However, the abstract nature of the model and the limited empirical validation warrant a somewhat lower score.

**Score: 7**

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper delivers a novel theoretical result that challenges the prevailing wisdom around RLHF and preference data. Its use of voting theory to model LLM post-training is ingenious. However, the direct practical impact is somewhat limited. While the identified limitations are important, further research is needed to translate the theoretical insights into concrete, scalable solutions. The empirical analysis of backtracking adds practical value, but requires further investigation. The theoretical contribution, combined with the identified weaknesses, leads to a score of 7. It is not groundbreaking enough to fundamentally shift the field but provides a vital reality check that deserves careful consideration.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Location Hierarchy Learning for Long-Tailed Mobility Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19965v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Wang, Junshu Dai, Yuchen Ying, Yuxuan Liang, Tongya Zheng, Mingli Song
- **Abstract**: Human mobility prediction is crucial for applications ranging from location-based recommendations to urban planning, which aims to forecast users' next location visits based on historical trajectories. Despite the severe long-tailed distribution of locations, the problem of long-tailed mobility prediction remains largely underexplored. Existing long-tailed learning methods primarily focus on rebalancing the skewed distribution at the data, model, or class level, neglecting to exploit the spatiotemporal semantics of locations. To address this gap, we propose the first plug-and-play framework for long-tailed mobility prediction in an exploitation and exploration manner, named \textbf{A}daptive \textbf{LO}cation \textbf{H}ier\textbf{A}rchy learning (ALOHA). First, we construct city-tailored location hierarchy based on Large Language Models (LLMs) by exploiting Maslow's theory of human motivation to design Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompts that captures spatiotemporal semantics. Second, we optimize the location hierarchy predictions by Gumbel disturbance and node-wise adaptive weights within the hierarchical tree structure. Experiments on state-of-the-art models across six datasets demonstrate the framework's consistent effectiveness and generalizability, which strikes a well balance between head and tail locations. Weight analysis and ablation studies reveal the optimization differences of each component for head and tail locations. Furthermore, in-depth analyses of hierarchical distance and case study demonstrate the effective semantic guidance from the location hierarchy. Our code will be made publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ALOHA (Adaptive Location HierArchy learning), a novel plug-and-play framework designed to improve long-tailed mobility prediction.  ALOHA addresses the challenge of skewed location visit frequencies by creating a city-tailored location hierarchy using Large Language Models (LLMs) informed by Maslow's hierarchy of needs.  The framework then explores the learned hierarchy by introducing Gumbel disturbance and node-wise adaptive weights within a hierarchical tree structure.  The Adaptive Hierarchical Loss (AHL) unifies exploitation and exploration. Experiments across six datasets and various state-of-the-art models demonstrate the framework's effectiveness in balancing head and tail location predictions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its approach to addressing the long-tailed mobility prediction problem through a semantically-aware hierarchical framework, specifically using LLMs and Maslow's hierarchy to guide location grouping. This is a fresh perspective, as existing methods focus primarily on rebalancing at the data, model, or class level without explicitly incorporating spatiotemporal semantics.  The introduction of adaptive hierarchical loss with Gumbel disturbance is also novel, offering a mechanism for both exploiting and exploring mobility patterns.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a very important problem in mobility prediction, namely the long-tailed distribution of location visits, which significantly affects the performance and fairness of existing methods. By improving tail location prediction, ALOHA has the potential to enhance various downstream applications like urban planning and personalized recommendations for less frequently visited locations. The plug-and-play nature of ALOHA also makes it easier to integrate with existing mobility prediction models. The consistent improvements achieved across various datasets and backbone architectures further demonstrate the practical significance of the framework.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Relevance:** Targets a crucial problem in mobility prediction.
*   **Novel Approach:** Combines LLMs, Maslow's hierarchy, and a hierarchical learning framework in a novel way.
*   **Plug-and-Play Design:** Makes the framework highly adaptable to different base models.
*   **Extensive Experiments:** Thorough evaluations across multiple datasets and backbones.
*   **Detailed Analysis:**  Includes weight analysis, ablation studies, and hierarchical distance analysis to gain insights into the framework's workings.
*   **Code Availability:** Enhances reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs and External Knowledge:** The dependence on LLMs and resources like Foursquare for deriving the location hierarchy adds complexity and potential bias.  The quality of the hierarchy heavily depends on the LLM and the provided prompts. While the paper mentions expert validation, a more detailed analysis of the hierarchy's quality and potential biases introduced by the LLM would strengthen the evaluation.  The transferability of this approach to cities where such external information is unavailable is also a concern.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** Although the results indicate the model is somewhat robust to different values of the temperature parameter, the inclusion of an "adaptive" temperature setting adds to the model complexity and demands additional evaluation to assess the specific conditions or scenarios where this adaptive strategy is beneficial.
*   **Computational Cost:** Although the paper says that most plugins add minimal computational overhead, there should be more details on the computational time of the LLM used in the creation of the hierarchy.

**Justification for the Score:**

While the paper presents a genuinely interesting and well-executed approach, some concerns slightly moderate its overall impact.
*   The approach is conceptually novel in its fusion of LLMs, hierarchical knowledge, and tailored loss function.
*   The plug-and-play is valuable for wider adoption and comparison to other models.
*   The approach has been tested on multiple data sets, suggesting it has the potential to be quite practical.

However, questions remain.
*   The paper does not go deep into the practical trade-offs of using LLMs in this way: creation costs, power requirements, and LLM API rates.
*   Similarly, the dependence on human-created location type data may not be easily available in many areas, decreasing generality.

Overall, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field but may not be wholly groundbreaking.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning to Select In-Context Demonstration Preferred by Large Language Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19966v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng Zhang, Shaocheng Lan, Lei Song, Jiang Bian, Yexin Li, Kan Ren
- **Abstract**: In-context learning (ICL) enables large language models (LLMs) to adapt to new tasks during inference using only a few demonstrations. However, ICL performance is highly dependent on the selection of these demonstrations. Recent work explores retrieval-based methods for selecting query-specific demonstrations, but these approaches often rely on surrogate objectives such as metric learning, failing to directly optimize ICL performance. Consequently, they struggle to identify truly beneficial demonstrations. Moreover, their discriminative retrieval paradigm is ineffective when the candidate pool lacks sufficient high-quality demonstrations. To address these challenges, we propose GenICL, a novel generative preference learning framework that leverages LLM feedback to directly optimize demonstration selection for ICL. Experiments on 19 datasets across 11 task categories demonstrate that GenICL achieves superior performance than existing methods in selecting the most effective demonstrations, leading to better ICL performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Learning to Select In-Context Demonstration Preferred by Large Language Model":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of selecting effective demonstrations for in-context learning (ICL) with large language models (LLMs).  Existing retrieval-based methods often use surrogate objectives like metric learning, which don't directly optimize ICL performance and struggle when high-quality demonstrations are scarce. The authors propose GENICL, a novel generative framework based on Bayesian optimization and preference learning. GENICL uses LLM feedback to directly optimize the demonstration selection process.  It treats ICL as a generative process and uses a latent variable to model the LLM's demonstration preference. The framework leverages preference learning to distinguish between effective and ineffective demonstrations, allowing it to capture finer-grained information and better align with the intrinsic objective of ICL.  Experiments across various datasets and tasks demonstrate that GENICL outperforms existing methods in selecting demonstrations and improving ICL performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a genuinely novel approach to demonstration selection for ICL. The core idea of treating demonstration selection as a generative Bayesian optimization problem and using preference learning based on LLM feedback is a significant departure from traditional retrieval-based methods. The use of a latent variable to represent LLM preference is clever. This is a strong step toward directly optimizing the intended goal instead of relying on proxies.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for the field of ICL. The effectiveness of ICL is highly dependent on demonstration selection. The ability to more accurately select effective demonstrations is crucial for improving the performance and reliability of LLMs in few-shot learning scenarios. The authors demonstrate this empirically across a diverse set of tasks, highlighting the practical value of their approach. The work also addresses the critical issue of the scarcity of effective demonstrations, making it more robust in real-world scenarios. Furthermore, the method is model-agnostic and scalable, a strong asset for a growing field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong theoretical foundation:** The paper provides a solid theoretical justification for GENICL, grounding it in Bayesian optimization and preference learning.
    *   **Direct optimization of ICL:**  Unlike many existing methods that rely on surrogate objectives, GENICL directly optimizes the ICL objective.
    *   **Addressing demonstration scarcity:** The use of preference learning allows the model to learn from relatively few effective demonstrations.
    *   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:**  The authors conduct thorough experiments across a wide range of datasets and tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of GENICL compared to various baselines. The ablation studies validate the key design choices.
    *   **Model agnostic and scalable.** Validated on a wide number of LLMs of different size.
    *   **Careful analysis.** A case study and analysis of the number of demonstrations helps illuminate the details of GENICL's effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational cost:** The method requires scoring each candidate demonstration in the pool. While E5base is used to reduce the search space, the computational cost remains a potential bottleneck, especially for larger demonstration pools. The discussion on scaling addresses it, but the core problem remains.
    *   **Independence Assumption:** The approach treats each demonstration independently during optimization, ignoring potentially important interactions between them. This could limit the performance. This is brought up in the limitations, but remains a potential area for future work.
    *   **Reliance on LLM feedback:** The reliance on LLM feedback during the demonstration selection process makes the method susceptible to biases and limitations inherent in the LLM itself. Addressing potential biases is important.
    *   **Implementation complexity.** The proposed approach is complex, and while clearly written, requires significant engineering to implement.

*   **Potential influence:** This work has the potential to significantly influence future research on ICL.  It opens up new avenues for exploring generative approaches and preference learning in demonstration selection. The findings regarding the importance of directly optimizing ICL and addressing demonstration scarcity are valuable insights for the community.

*   **Why not a higher score?** Despite the paper's strengths, the weaknesses related to computational cost and the independence assumption limit its immediate practical impact. The reliance on LLM feedback also introduces potential biases that need to be addressed. While these limitations are acknowledged, they prevent the paper from achieving a truly exceptional score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### CP-Router: An Uncertainty-Aware Router Between LLM and LRM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19970v1)
- **Authors**: Jiayuan Su, Fulin Lin, Zhaopeng Feng, Han Zheng, Teng Wang, Zhenyu Xiao, Xinlong Zhao, Zuozhu Liu, Lu Cheng, Hongwei Wang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have significantly improved long-chain reasoning capabilities over Large Language Models (LLMs). However, LRMs often produce unnecessarily lengthy outputs even for simple queries, leading to inefficiencies or even accuracy degradation compared to LLMs. To overcome this, we propose CP-Router, a training-free and model-agnostic routing framework that dynamically selects between an LLM and an LRM, demonstrated with multiple-choice question answering (MCQA) prompts. The routing decision is guided by the prediction uncertainty estimates derived via Conformal Prediction (CP), which provides rigorous coverage guarantees. To further refine the uncertainty differentiation across inputs, we introduce Full and Binary Entropy (FBE), a novel entropy-based criterion that adaptively selects the appropriate CP threshold. Experiments across diverse MCQA benchmarks, including mathematics, logical reasoning, and Chinese chemistry, demonstrate that CP-Router efficiently reduces token usage while maintaining or even improving accuracy compared to using LRM alone. We also extend CP-Router to diverse model pairings and open-ended QA, where it continues to demonstrate strong performance, validating its generality and robustness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CP-Router: An Uncertainty-Aware Router Between LLM and LRM."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CP-Router, a novel, training-free, and model-agnostic framework designed to dynamically route prompts between Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Reasoning Models (LRMs). The routing decision is based on uncertainty estimates derived using Conformal Prediction (CP), offering rigorous coverage guarantees. The paper further introduces Full and Binary Entropy (FBE), a novel entropy-based criterion to refine uncertainty differentiation across inputs and adaptively select the CP threshold. Experiments across multiple-choice question answering (MCQA) benchmarks demonstrate that CP-Router effectively reduces token usage while maintaining or improving accuracy compared to using LRM alone. The authors also extend CP-Router to diverse model pairings and open-ended QA, showing its generality and robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of dynamically routing prompts between LLMs and LRMs based on uncertainty estimates is not entirely new. However, the specific approach of using Conformal Prediction (CP) for uncertainty quantification in this context is a valuable contribution. The introduction of Full and Binary Entropy (FBE) as an adaptive calibration method to refine the CP threshold seems like a practically oriented novelty. Furthermore, the training-free and model-agnostic aspect is a significant plus, differentiating it from other routing methods.

*   **Significance:** The overthinking issue of LRMs, as the paper highlights, is a growing concern in the field. An effective and efficient routing mechanism has the potential to significantly reduce computational costs without sacrificing accuracy. The experimental results presented in the paper across diverse benchmarks support the effectiveness of CP-Router in reducing token usage while maintaining or even improving accuracy. The extension to open-ended QA and model pairings further enhances the significance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled Approach:** The use of Conformal Prediction provides statistically grounded uncertainty estimates, which is a strength compared to heuristic or ad-hoc routing mechanisms.
    *   **Training-Free and Model-Agnostic:** Eliminating the need for training makes the framework more practical and adaptable to different model pairings.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments on diverse MCQA benchmarks and extension to open-ended QA demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of CP-Router.
    *   **Adaptive Calibration:** The FBE metric for adaptive threshold selection addresses a key challenge in applying CP and improves uncertainty differentiation.
    *   **Clear Motivation and Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies a relevant problem (LRM overthinking) and presents a well-motivated solution.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While CP provides statistical guarantees, the paper lacks theoretical analysis on how the routing decisions affect the overall accuracy and efficiency under different conditions.
    *   **Reliance on MCQA Format:** Adapting the framework to open-ended QA required prompting the LLM to generate candidate answers, limiting the direct applicability of CP to the model's own generative process. This extension might have introduced biases.
    *   **Limited Comparison with State-of-the-Art LLM routing algorithms:** A more thorough comparison against more recent routing algorithms (if applicable). While existing algorithms are described in related work, they are not extensively compared to in experiments.

*   **Potential Impact:** CP-Router can have a significant impact on the field by providing a practical and effective method for routing prompts between LLMs and LRMs. This can lead to more efficient and cost-effective use of these models in various applications. The training-free and model-agnostic nature of CP-Router makes it particularly attractive for real-world deployment.

**Justification of Score:**

Overall, CP-Router represents a solid contribution to the field of LLM routing. The use of conformal prediction is clever, the framework is training-free and model-agnostic which is advantageous, and the empirical results support its effectiveness. However, there is room for more theoretical analysis, comparisons with state-of-the-art algorithms and explorations of other types of QA tasks.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DFIR-Metric: A Benchmark Dataset for Evaluating Large Language Models in Digital Forensics and Incident Response
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19973v1)
- **Authors**: Bilel Cherif, Tamas Bisztray, Richard A. Dubniczky, Aaesha Aldahmani, Saeed Alshehhi, Norbert Tihanyi
- **Abstract**: Digital Forensics and Incident Response (DFIR) involves analyzing digital evidence to support legal investigations. Large Language Models (LLMs) offer new opportunities in DFIR tasks such as log analysis and memory forensics, but their susceptibility to errors and hallucinations raises concerns in high-stakes contexts. Despite growing interest, there is no comprehensive benchmark to evaluate LLMs across both theoretical and practical DFIR domains. To address this gap, we present DFIR-Metric, a benchmark with three components: (1) Knowledge Assessment: a set of 700 expert-reviewed multiple-choice questions sourced from industry-standard certifications and official documentation; (2) Realistic Forensic Challenges: 150 CTF-style tasks testing multi-step reasoning and evidence correlation; and (3) Practical Analysis: 500 disk and memory forensics cases from the NIST Computer Forensics Tool Testing Program (CFTT). We evaluated 14 LLMs using DFIR-Metric, analyzing both their accuracy and consistency across trials. We also introduce a new metric, the Task Understanding Score (TUS), designed to more effectively evaluate models in scenarios where they achieve near-zero accuracy. This benchmark offers a rigorous, reproducible foundation for advancing AI in digital forensics. All scripts, artifacts, and results are available on the project website at https://github.com/DFIR-Metric.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DFIR-Metric: A Benchmark Dataset for Evaluating Large Language Models in Digital Forensics and Incident Response":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DFIR-Metric, a new benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the domain of Digital Forensics and Incident Response (DFIR). DFIR-Metric comprises three components: a knowledge assessment section with multiple-choice questions, realistic forensic challenges based on CTF-style tasks, and practical analysis cases from the NIST CFTT program. The authors evaluated 14 LLMs using this benchmark, focusing on accuracy and consistency. They also propose a new evaluation metric, the Task Understanding Score (TUS), to better assess models in scenarios where standard accuracy metrics fail. The dataset, scripts, and results are made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper identifies a clear gap in the existing literature. There is a lack of comprehensive benchmarks specifically tailored for evaluating LLMs in the DFIR domain. This benchmark can potentially fill this gap.

    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** DFIR-Metric is reasonably comprehensive, covering theoretical knowledge, practical skills, and the ability to solve real-world-inspired forensic tasks. This multi-faceted approach is valuable.

    *   **Emphasis on Reliability:** The paper goes beyond simple accuracy metrics by focusing on reliability and consistency through repeated trials and the introduction of the TUS metric. The TUS is a valid attempt to reward partial success where LLMs often generate no response in its entirity.

    *   **Publicly Available Resource:** The open release of the dataset, scripts, and results is essential for reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **CTF component.** LLMs can be very easily prompted with an instruction to go to an online forum to search for answers, undermining the spirit and integrity of DFIR (to respond based on the model and its knowledge).
    *   **Static CTF, lack of dynamic scoring.** It seems as though the DFIR scenarios are static in nature; these require experts to set the correct parameters. The approach could be improved by using LLMs themselves to set the challenge parameters to create a dynamic CTF experience.
    *   **Limited novelty.** The overall novelty is limited given the plethora of benchmarks in cybersecurity. The authors admit that all source material comes from public sources, while this adds validity to the dataset, the method is not unique or innovative.

    *   **Reliance on Code Execution:** The string search component heavily relies on Python code generation and execution. This might not accurately reflect the capabilities of LLMs in other aspects of DFIR, such as textual analysis of reports or logs. The need to execute code to perform a forensic task also may prevent LLMs' integration into enterprise environments for security reasons.

    *   **Potential for Overfitting:** As with any benchmark dataset, there's a risk that LLMs could be fine-tuned specifically for DFIR-Metric, leading to inflated performance scores that don't generalize well to other DFIR tasks.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a standardized way to evaluate LLMs in DFIR. This could help researchers and practitioners make more informed decisions about when and how to use LLMs in this sensitive domain.

    *   The introduction of the TUS metric, while preliminary, is a valuable contribution to the development of more nuanced evaluation methods for LLMs.

    *   The paper also provides a valuable snapshot of the current state of LLM capabilities in DFIR, highlighting their strengths and weaknesses.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, this paper presents a useful and much needed contribution to the field of DFIR. However, the technical content is still limited, requiring a more dynamic, autonomous approach to the CTF and DFIR challenges. The methodology requires additional work, and there is a risk of the data set becoming stale over time.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### ICDM: Interference Cancellation Diffusion Models for Wireless Semantic Communications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19983v1)
- **Authors**: Tong Wu, Zhiyong Chen, Dazhi He, Feng Yang, Meixia Tao, Xiaodong Xu, Wenjun Zhang, Ping Zhang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models (DMs) have recently achieved significant success in wireless communications systems due to their denoising capabilities. The broadcast nature of wireless signals makes them susceptible not only to Gaussian noise, but also to unaware interference. This raises the question of whether DMs can effectively mitigate interference in wireless semantic communication systems. In this paper, we model the interference cancellation problem as a maximum a posteriori (MAP) problem over the joint posterior probability of the signal and interference, and theoretically prove that the solution provides excellent estimates for the signal and interference. To solve this problem, we develop an interference cancellation diffusion model (ICDM), which decomposes the joint posterior into independent prior probabilities of the signal and interference, along with the channel transition probablity. The log-gradients of these distributions at each time step are learned separately by DMs and accurately estimated through deriving. ICDM further integrates these gradients with advanced numerical iteration method, achieving accurate and rapid interference cancellation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ICDM significantly reduces the mean square error (MSE) and enhances perceptual quality compared to schemes without ICDM. For example, on the CelebA dataset under the Rayleigh fading channel with a signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of $20$ dB and signal to interference plus noise ratio (SINR) of 0 dB, ICDM reduces the MSE by 4.54 dB and improves the learned perceptual image patch similarity (LPIPS) by 2.47 dB.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel Interference Cancellation Diffusion Model (ICDM) for wireless semantic communications.  Recognizing the limitations of existing diffusion models in handling non-Gaussian interference, ICDM addresses interference cancellation as a maximum a posteriori (MAP) problem over the joint distribution of the signal and interference. It decomposes this joint posterior into independent priors (signal and interference) and a channel transition probability, each learned separately by dedicated diffusion models. The learned log-gradients are then integrated with an advanced numerical iteration method for efficient and accurate interference cancellation. Experimental results demonstrate substantial improvements in MSE and perceptual quality compared to schemes without ICDM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty lies in several aspects:

1.  **Problem Formulation:**  Modeling interference cancellation as a MAP problem over the *joint* signal and interference distribution is a sound theoretical foundation. It provides a more holistic approach than treating interference as mere noise. The derivation of theoretical guarantees related to MSE is a significant plus.
2.  **ICDM Architecture:** The decomposition of the joint posterior into independent priors for signal and interference, learned separately by diffusion models, is a clever design choice. This allows specialized learning of complex interference patterns without entangling them with the signal model. Separating learning signal and interference distribution can address some challenges such as lack of training data for certain interference types.
3.  **Hybrid Approach:** Combining diffusion models with advanced numerical iterative methods to solve the MAP problem provides a practical solution. This addresses the typical computational burden of diffusion models and offers faster, more accurate interference cancellation. The numerical iterations utilize the learned log-gradients which is also a novel feature.

However, there are potential limitations:

*   The assumption of independence between signal and interference (Assumption 1) may not always hold in real-world scenarios, particularly in coordinated multi-point (CoMP) systems or cognitive radio networks. While a common assumption, its impact on performance needs to be carefully considered and discussed.
*   Assumption 2 is also potentially limiting. While justifiable, it does simplify the complexity of the problem.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work is considerable:

1.  **Addressing a Critical Challenge:** Interference cancellation is a fundamental challenge in wireless communication. ICDM provides a promising new approach using the power of diffusion models.
2.  **Advancing Semantic Communications:** By effectively mitigating interference, ICDM enhances the reliability and performance of semantic communication systems, which are gaining increasing importance for future wireless networks.
3.  **Practical and Feasible Solution:** ICDM is designed to be practical, with a focus on rapid and accurate interference cancellation. The use of readily available components (DiT, established numerical solvers) makes implementation more accessible. This work has the potential to boost the performance of practical system designs that deal with interference.
4. The paper is very thorough in its treatment of the problem. The mathematical formulation, algorithmic design, and experimental evaluation seem reasonable.

**Weaknesses:**

1.  **Limited Interference Scenarios:** The experiments, while comprehensive, could benefit from exploring a wider range of interference types, including those beyond the Gaussian noise-like interference that the paper explicitly considers.  How well does ICDM perform against jamming attacks, for example?
2.  **Overstated Results:**  The LPIPS gains (e.g., 2.47 dB) are often presented without fully acknowledging the decibel scale.  While significant, the practical impact needs to be considered.
3.  The paper could benefit from more directly comparing against other methods which solve the interference cancellation problem and also are trained with diffusion models.

**Potential Influence:**

ICDM could significantly impact the field by:

*   Inspiring further research into diffusion model-based interference cancellation techniques.
*   Providing a foundation for developing more robust and reliable semantic communication systems.
*   Motivating the design of new numerical iterative methods tailored for diffusion model-based signal processing.

**Score:**

Score: 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of wireless communication. The theoretical formulation of the interference cancellation problem is solid. The architecture of ICDM is innovative and well-suited to address the challenges of non-Gaussian interference. The experimental results demonstrate substantial performance gains compared to existing schemes.

However, the paper also has weaknesses, mainly regarding the simplifying assumptions and the need for experiments with a wider variety of interference types, and comparison to diffusion model baselines. While the theoretical analysis is strong, the assumptions could limit the practical applicability of the proposed scheme. The novelty is adequate, although better comparison to the most relevant literature is possible. For these reasons, a score of 7.5 reflects a solid, impactful contribution that deserves attention.

- **Classification**: cs.IT
- **Score**: 7/10

### Structured Initialization for Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19985v1)
- **Authors**: Jianqiao Zheng, Xueqian Li, Hemanth Saratchandran, Simon Lucey
- **Abstract**: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) inherently encode strong inductive biases, enabling effective generalization on small-scale datasets. In this paper, we propose integrating this inductive bias into ViTs, not through an architectural intervention but solely through initialization. The motivation here is to have a ViT that can enjoy strong CNN-like performance when data assets are small, but can still scale to ViT-like performance as the data expands. Our approach is motivated by our empirical results that random impulse filters can achieve commensurate performance to learned filters within a CNN. We improve upon current ViT initialization strategies, which typically rely on empirical heuristics such as using attention weights from pretrained models or focusing on the distribution of attention weights without enforcing structures. Empirical results demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms standard ViT initialization across numerous small and medium-scale benchmarks, including Food-101, CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, STL-10, Flowers, and Pets, while maintaining comparative performance on large-scale datasets such as ImageNet-1K. Moreover, our initialization strategy can be easily integrated into various transformer-based architectures such as Swin Transformer and MLP-Mixer with consistent improvements in performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Structured Initialization for Vision Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of Vision Transformers (ViTs) generalizing poorly on small-scale datasets due to their lack of strong inductive biases. It proposes a novel "structured initialization" strategy for ViTs that embeds convolutional inductive biases into the attention maps of the transformer. This is achieved by initializing the attention weight matrices (Q and K) to produce attention maps that resemble random impulse convolution kernels. The approach is motivated by the empirical finding that random impulse filters in CNNs can achieve performance comparable to learned filters. The authors demonstrate that this initialization significantly improves ViT performance on small and medium-scale datasets while maintaining competitive performance on large-scale datasets.  The method is also shown to generalize to other transformer-based architectures like Swin Transformer and MLP-Mixer. The paper provides theoretical justification for the approach, linking it to concepts of rank deficiency in input embeddings and the span of kernel spaces.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using initialization to imbue ViTs with convolutional inductive biases is not entirely new. Existing works explored pre-trained models or replicated weight distributions of pre-trained ViTs. However, this paper distinguishes itself by *directly structuring* the attention maps during initialization, forcing them to resemble random impulse filters. This is a key difference and a significant step forward. Furthermore, the theoretical justification based on rank deficiency and kernel spaces offers a deeper understanding of why this approach works. The novelty, while not a complete paradigm shift, lies in the specific technique of structured initialization and the supporting theoretical analysis. This technique doesn't require pre-trained weights, is model-agnostic to a point and can be used even with very small datasets.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation of ViTs: their data-hungry nature. By enabling ViTs to perform well on smaller datasets, this work broadens their applicability to scenarios where large-scale pre-training isn't feasible or desirable. The fact that the initialization strategy doesn't significantly degrade performance on large-scale datasets is also a significant strength. The generalization to other architectures (Swin Transformer, MLP-Mixer) strengthens its practical impact. The improvement over "Mimetic initialization", and the ability to combine the method with existing pre-training approaches both are strong reasons to consider the paper impactful.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined approach.
    *   Strong empirical results on a range of datasets (small, medium, and large).
    *   Theoretical justification providing insights into the method's effectiveness.
    *   Generalizability to other transformer-based architectures.
    *   Compatibility with existing pre-training techniques.
    *   Well written and generally easy to understand (appendices very thorough).
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The theoretical analysis, while insightful, could be further refined. The connection between the theoretical results and the exact choice of *impulse* filters could be made stronger.
    *   The experimental evaluation, while comprehensive, could benefit from an ablation study on the parameters α, β, and γ in the initialization process. While the paper mentions that their values are not strictly constrained, it would be helpful to understand their sensitivity.
    *   While it's model-agnostic to some extent, it does require positional encoding in all the tested architectures, making other architectures difficult to test without any adjustments.
    *   No explanation of why impulse filters vs something like gaussian blurs are more effective, nor why a single impulse filter vs multiple would be better.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the way ViTs are initialized, particularly in resource-constrained settings. It offers a practical and theoretically grounded alternative to relying solely on large-scale pre-training or architectural modifications. Follow-up works might explore more sophisticated ways of structuring attention maps or investigate the interplay between initialization and fine-tuning. The paper will hopefully inspire new research into how to integrate inductive bias in initialization.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and useful technique for improving ViT performance on small datasets. It has sound theoretical motivation and strong empirical results, demonstrating its effectiveness and generalizability. The paper's potential influence is significant, broadening the applicability of ViTs. The score is not higher (above 7) primarily because the theoretical link is not completely convincing and the paper doesn't deeply explore the parameters controlling the initialization strategy. There are several aspects left unexplored that could have strengthened the paper overall. However, it represents a valuable contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### How Well Do Large Reasoning Models Translate? A Comprehensive Evaluation for Multi-Domain Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19987v1)
- **Authors**: Yongshi Ye, Biao Fu, Chongxuan Huang, Yidong Chen, Xiaodong Shi
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance in general-purpose machine translation, but their effectiveness in complex, domain-sensitive translation tasks remains underexplored. Recent advancements in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), raise the question of whether structured reasoning can enhance translation quality across diverse domains. In this work, we compare the performance of LRMs with traditional LLMs across 15 representative domains and four translation directions. Our evaluation considers various factors, including task difficulty, input length, and terminology density. We use a combination of automatic metrics and an enhanced MQM-based evaluation hierarchy to assess translation quality. Our findings show that LRMs consistently outperform traditional LLMs in semantically complex domains, especially in long-text and high-difficulty translation scenarios. Moreover, domain-adaptive prompting strategies further improve performance by better leveraging the reasoning capabilities of LRMs. These results highlight the potential of structured reasoning in MDMT tasks and provide valuable insights for optimizing translation systems in domain-sensitive contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "How Well do Large Reasoning Models Translate? A Comprehensive Evaluation for Multi-Domain Machine Translation":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) compared to traditional Large Language Models (LLMs) in multi-domain machine translation (MDMT). The authors conduct a comprehensive evaluation across 15 representative domains and four translation directions, considering factors like task difficulty, input length, and terminology density. They use a combination of automatic metrics (BLEU, COMET, CometKiwi) and an enhanced MQM-based evaluation hierarchy to assess translation quality. The results show that LRMs generally outperform traditional LLMs in semantically complex domains, especially with long texts and high difficulty. Domain-adaptive prompting strategies further improve LRM performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper's strength lies in its systematic and extensive evaluation across a wide range of domains and translation directions. This provides a granular understanding of LRM performance in MDMT.
    *   **Addressing an important gap:** The paper directly addresses the question of whether the newer class of reasoning-oriented LLMs can successfully address the unique challenges of MDMT, which hadn't been systematically explored before.
    *   **Multi-faceted Evaluation Metrics:**  The authors thoughtfully combine automatic metrics with an LLM-powered MQM hierarchy.  This allows for both quantitative assessment and qualitative analysis (via the MQM error categories). The MQM implementation, with its emphasis on capturing domain-specific nuances and drawing on translation professional input, is particularly valuable.
    *   **Insightful Findings:** The key findings are well-supported by the data. The observations regarding semantic accuracy vs. stylistic consistency, adaptability in document-level translation, and the effectiveness of domain-aware prompting are all important insights.
    *   **Practical Recommendations:**  The authors extract practical recommendations such as integrating terminology constraints into the reasoning chain of LRMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Language Coverage:** A significant limitation is the focus on high-resource language pairs (primarily English, German, and Chinese). The generalizability of the findings to low-resource languages remains uncertain.
    *   **MQM Scorer Bias:** While the MQM implementation improves granularity, the use of a single LLM scorer (DeepSeek-V3) for MQM introduces potential bias aligned with that model's inherent translation behaviors. The extent of this bias isn't explicitly addressed. The paper would have benefited from including inter-annotator agreement rate.
    *   **Overthinking Concept:** The "overthinking" hypothesis of LRMs is plausible but not rigorously proven. It could benefit from targeted experiments that specifically isolate and manipulate reasoning depth.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Still Relevant**: Though the domain aware prompting improves performance, it does not get rid of the need to engineer prompts to achieve the best result with LRMs.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   **Novel Application of LRMs:** The paper provides a novel and early assessment of Large Reasoning Models in the context of multi-domain machine translation. It contributes to understanding the strengths and weaknesses of these models beyond general-purpose translation.
    *   **Practical Insights for MDMT:** The findings offer actionable insights for optimizing MDMT systems. The exploration of domain-adaptive prompting, adaptation strategies and focus on structured reasoning has significant practical relevance.
    *   **Methodological Contribution:** The combination of automatic and MQM-based evaluation provides a robust and adaptable evaluation framework for MDMT research. The study provides valuable insights for future research.
    *   **Contribution to the MT Community**: The study identifies specific challenges and opportunities of adopting LRMs in complex translation scenarios. The results could be important for further research and development.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper can influence the direction of MDMT research by highlighting the importance of reasoning capabilities and domain adaptation in LLMs.
    *   It can guide the development of new LLM architectures and training strategies that are better suited for MDMT.
    *   The evaluation framework can be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of machine translation by providing a comprehensive evaluation of LRMs in multi-domain settings. It addresses a crucial gap in the literature and offers valuable insights into the potential of LRMs for MDMT. The multi-faceted evaluation methodology and practical recommendations further enhance its impact. However, the limited language coverage, reliance on a single LLM scorer for MQM, and under-explored "overthinking" hypothesis are weaknesses that slightly reduce its overall score. While valuable, the research is an evolution within the field of MT rather than a revolutionary innovation, hence the score reflects that impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automatic Metadata Extraction for Text-to-SQL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19988v1)
- **Authors**: Vladislav Shkapenyuk, Divesh Srivastava, Theodore Johnson, Parisa Ghane
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently become sophisticated enough to automate many tasks ranging from pattern finding to writing assistance to code generation. In this paper, we examine text-to-SQL generation. We have observed from decades of experience that the most difficult part of query development lies in understanding the database contents. These experiences inform the direction of our research. Text-to-SQL benchmarks such as SPIDER and Bird contain extensive metadata that is generally not available in practice. Human-generated metadata requires the use of expensive Subject Matter Experts (SMEs), who are often not fully aware of many aspects of their databases. In this paper, we explore techniques for automatic metadata extraction to enable text-to-SQL generation. Ee explore the use of two standard and one newer metadata extraction techniques: profiling, query log analysis, and SQL-to text generation using an LLM. We use BIRD benchmark [JHQY+23] to evaluate the effectiveness of these techniques. BIRD does not provide query logs on their test database, so we prepared a submission that uses profiling alone, and does not use any specially tuned model (we used GPT-4o). From Sept 1 to Sept 23, 2024, and Nov 11 through Nov 23, 2024 we achieved the highest score both with and without using the "oracle" information provided with the question set. We regained the number 1 spot on Mar 11, 2025, and are still at #1 at the time of the writing (May, 2025).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of automatic metadata extraction for text-to-SQL generation. It highlights the difficulty in understanding database content as the major bottleneck in query development, noting that existing benchmarks rely on extensive metadata often unavailable in real-world scenarios. The authors explore and combine three techniques for metadata extraction: database profiling, query log analysis, and SQL-to-text generation using Large Language Models (LLMs). They use the BIRD benchmark for evaluation. The paper reports achieving top scores on the BIRD leaderboard by using profiling-enhanced field metadata with a novel schema linking algorithm. The findings suggest that LLM-driven summarization of profile metadata and schema linking can significantly improve text-to-SQL accuracy, sometimes even outperforming manually curated metadata. The paper also explores query log analysis to discover undocumented join paths and business logic, and examines the use of LLMs for SQL-to-text generation to create training examples.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty stems from the combination of several existing techniques with a specific focus on metadata extraction for text-to-SQL.  While profiling and query log analysis are established methods, the authors’ application of LLMs to summarize profiling information and the introduction of a task-aligned schema linking algorithm appears to be novel. Fusing different sources of metadata is also an important step. The use of LLMs for SQL-to-text generation as a means of generating training data shows promise. A key claim of the paper is that their system is designed for complex industrial databases. The techniques such as profiling, query log analysis, and the emphasis on a profile-enhanced schema linking algorithm are critical to enable the effective utilization of the text-to-SQL in the absence of well-documented and maintained data.

**Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a critical gap in the text-to-SQL field: the dependence on manually curated metadata. The demonstration that automatically extracted metadata can improve performance, even beyond SME-provided metadata, is valuable. This opens up possibilities for applying text-to-SQL technology to databases where expert knowledge and resources for creating metadata are limited. The study’s success on the BIRD benchmark adds credibility, although the paper does note the simplistic nature of the BIRD dataset. The techniques such as query log analysis, even in a simple environment such as BIRD, can lead to the discovery of a large fraction of join constraints that are undocumented in the schema. 

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Focus:**  The paper is motivated by real-world challenges in query development.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** The BIRD benchmark results provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed techniques.
*   **Comprehensive Approach:** The authors explore multiple metadata extraction methods and combine them effectively.
*   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow.
*   **Addresses Limitations:** The paper acknowledges limitations, such as the simplicity of the BIRD schema and the need for further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Benchmark Limitations:** As mentioned, the BIRD benchmark, while a step up from Spider, is still relatively simple compared to truly complex industrial databases.  The generalizability of these results to much larger schemas needs further exploration. The paper acknowledges this.
*   **Limited Exploration of Alternatives:** While the paper presents a well-defined approach, it doesn't deeply analyze alternative schema linking algorithms or LLM prompting strategies. A comparative analysis would further strengthen the work.
*   **SQL-to-Text Evaluation:** While the SQL-to-text experiments are interesting, they could be more rigorous.  The subjective grading scale adds a layer of uncertainty, and the lack of alternative methods comparison makes the study preliminary. 

**Justification:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the text-to-SQL field by tackling the practical problem of limited metadata. The techniques presented are well-reasoned, empirically validated on a standard benchmark, and address an important area for improving the applicability of text-to-SQL in real-world settings. While the benchmark's simplicity and limited exploration of alternative methods constrain the overall impact, the work shows clear promise and provides a solid foundation for future research.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 7/10

### Embracing Imperfection: Simulating Students with Diverse Cognitive Levels Using LLM-based Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19997v1)
- **Authors**: Tao Wu, Jingyuan Chen, Wang Lin, Mengze Li, Yumeng Zhu, Ang Li, Kun Kuang, Fei Wu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are revolutionizing education, with LLM-based agents playing a key role in simulating student behavior. A major challenge in student simulation is modeling the diverse learning patterns of students at various cognitive levels. However, current LLMs, typically trained as ``helpful assistants'', target at generating perfect responses. As a result, they struggle to simulate students with diverse cognitive abilities, as they often produce overly advanced answers, missing the natural imperfections that characterize student learning and resulting in unrealistic simulations. To address this issue, we propose a training-free framework for student simulation. We begin by constructing a cognitive prototype for each student using a knowledge graph, which captures their understanding of concepts from past learning records. This prototype is then mapped to new tasks to predict student performance. Next, we simulate student solutions based on these predictions and iteratively refine them using a beam search method to better replicate realistic mistakes. To validate our approach, we construct the \texttt{Student\_100} dataset, consisting of $100$ students working on Python programming and $5,000$ learning records. Experimental results show that our method consistently outperforms baseline models, achieving $100\%$ improvement in simulation accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of simulating students with diverse cognitive levels using LLM-based agents. Current LLMs, optimized to generate "perfect" responses, struggle to replicate the imperfections and typical errors of lower-performing students, leading to unrealistic simulations. The authors propose a training-free framework that leverages cognitive prototypes, constructed from a student's past learning records using a knowledge graph, to capture their understanding of concepts. This prototype is then mapped to new tasks to predict performance and simulate student solutions, which are iteratively refined using a beam search method to replicate realistic mistakes. The authors create a new dataset, Student_100, consisting of 100 students working on Python programming tasks, to validate their approach. Experimental results show that their method consistently outperforms baseline models, improving simulation accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper offers a novel approach to student simulation by moving away from directly prompting LLMs to "act like" students, which tends to produce overly advanced responses. The use of cognitive prototypes based on knowledge graphs to explicitly model a student's understanding and predict potential errors is a significant step forward. While fine-tuning on error-rich datasets exists, the authors' training-free method, which adaptively introduces errors based on a student's cognitive state, is more personalized and potentially less prone to embedding incorrect knowledge.  The iterative refinement process using beam search is also a valuable contribution for generating more nuanced and realistic student behaviors.

**Significance:** The work has significant implications for education research. Accurate student simulations are crucial for evaluating teaching strategies, assessing intelligent tutoring systems, and enhancing AI educational tools. By addressing the limitations of existing LLM-based simulations, the authors pave the way for more realistic and useful educational applications. The construction of the Student_100 dataset provides a valuable resource for future research in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies a critical limitation of current LLM-based student simulations.
*   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The cognitive prototype approach is logically justified and grounded in educational theory.
*   **Technical Innovation:**  The knowledge graph construction, cognitive prototype mapping, and beam search-based self-refinement demonstrate strong technical contributions.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The creation of the Student_100 dataset and extensive experimental results provide solid evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The in-depth analysis, including ablation studies and case studies, helps to understand the contributions of different components.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Data:** While the Student_100 dataset is valuable, it is limited to Python programming tasks. The generalizability of the approach to other subjects (e.g., mathematics, natural language) needs further exploration, though the authors do construct small Java and C++ datasets.
*   **Reliance on LLMs:** The entire framework relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs, which are themselves subject to biases and limitations.  While the authors mitigate this by using the same model throughout the pipeline for consistency, the quality of the cognitive scores and refinement steps is ultimately dependent on the LLM's performance.
*   **Complexity:** The framework is fairly complex, involving multiple stages and components. This might make it difficult for others to replicate or adapt the approach.
*   **Negligible novelty in one of the key aspects.** The idea of using a student’s record to define their cognitive profile for simulation purposes is not novel, however the use of beam search could be considered novel.
*   **Lack of generalizability of the method** the results in Java and C++ show only negligibly better results when compared with the baseline models.

**Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on student simulation and AI in education. The cognitive prototype approach could be adopted and extended by others to develop more realistic and personalized educational applications. The Student_100 dataset will serve as a valuable benchmark for evaluating new simulation methods.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the approach, the technical innovation, the empirical validation, and the potential influence on the field, the paper warrants a high score. However, the limitations regarding the scope of data, the reliance on LLMs, and the complexity of the framework prevent it from achieving a truly exceptional score. I'll assign a score of 7.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### NEXT: Multi-Grained Mixture of Experts via Text-Modulation for Multi-Modal Object Re-ID
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20001v1)
- **Authors**: Shihao Li, Chenglong Li, Aihua Zheng, Andong Lu, Jin Tang, Jixin Ma
- **Abstract**: Multi-modal object re-identification (ReID) aims to extract identity features across heterogeneous spectral modalities to enable accurate recognition and retrieval in complex real-world scenarios. However, most existing methods rely on implicit feature fusion structures, making it difficult to model fine-grained recognition strategies under varying challenging conditions. Benefiting from the powerful semantic understanding capabilities of Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs), the visual appearance of an object can be effectively translated into descriptive text. In this paper, we propose a reliable multi-modal caption generation method based on attribute confidence, which significantly reduces the unknown recognition rate of MLLMs in multi-modal semantic generation and improves the quality of generated text. Additionally, we propose a novel ReID framework NEXT, the Multi-grained Mixture of Experts via Text-Modulation for Multi-modal Object Re-Identification. Specifically, we decouple the recognition problem into semantic and structural expert branches to separately capture modality-specific appearance and intrinsic structure. For semantic recognition, we propose the Text-Modulated Semantic-sampling Experts (TMSE), which leverages randomly sampled high-quality semantic texts to modulate expert-specific sampling of multi-modal features and mining intra-modality fine-grained semantic cues. Then, to recognize coarse-grained structure features, we propose the Context-Shared Structure-aware Experts (CSSE) that focuses on capturing the holistic object structure across modalities and maintains inter-modality structural consistency through a soft routing mechanism. Finally, we propose the Multi-Modal Feature Aggregation (MMFA), which adopts a unified feature fusion strategy to simply and effectively integrate semantic and structural expert outputs into the final identity representations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses multi-modal object re-identification (ReID), a challenging task where objects need to be identified across different sensor modalities (e.g., RGB, NIR, TIR). The key idea is to leverage Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to extract semantic information from the visual appearance, and then combine this with structural information for more robust ReID. The approach involves:

1.  **A reliable multi-modal caption generation method:**  This enhances the quality of text descriptions generated by MLLMs, making them more reliable especially for low-quality modalities. It uses a confidence-aware attribute generation and multi-modal aggregation to improve the description.

2.  **A multi-grained mixture of experts framework (NEXT):** This framework contains semantic experts that sample fine-grained features guided by the generated text, capturing intra-modality cues.  It also has structural experts that capture the overall object structure across modalities using a shared routing mechanism.

3.  **Multi-Modal Feature Aggregation:** A fusion strategy that integrates the outputs of the semantic and structural experts into the final identity representation.

The paper evaluates the approach on several multi-modal object ReID datasets, demonstrating significant improvements over existing state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper offers a good combination of using MLLMs for feature extraction and combining it with expert system design with the ability to sample fine-grained semantic aspects and coarse structure features. It improves caption generation techniques and proposes a new ReID architecture specifically designed to leverage text guidance. The improvements achieved are significant compared to other works in the field. The paper makes tangible improvements in a complicated area of research. However, the ideas feel intuitive, and the results, although strong, are not groundbreaking. It builds on existing works of text semantic understanding with improved implementation.

*Strengths:*

*   **Strong Performance:** The method achieves state-of-the-art results on several challenging datasets, demonstrating its effectiveness.
*   **Well-designed Architecture:** The mixture of experts framework is well-motivated and designed to address the specific challenges of multi-modal ReID.
*   **Improved Caption Generation:** Enhancing MLLM-based captioning for the task helps bridge the gap between modalities.
*   **Detailed Ablation Studies:**  The paper includes comprehensive ablation studies to demonstrate the contribution of each component.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the approach is effective, the individual components (MLLMs, mixture of experts) are not entirely novel.  The primary novelty lies in the combination and application of these techniques to multi-modal ReID.
*   **Dependency on MLLM Performance:** Performance relies heavily on the quality of text descriptions generated by the MLLMs. While the paper addresses this, the approach is still sensitive to potential errors or biases in the MLLMs.
*   **Computational Cost:** Using multiple expert branches increases the computational complexity of the method, potentially limiting its applicability in resource-constrained settings.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper provides a solid approach for multi-modal object ReID, and the results suggest that it will influence future research in this area. By effectively combining MLLMs with carefully designed expert networks, the paper sets a new benchmark for performance. It also shows the effectiveness in improving textual descriptions with confidence metrics.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant improvement over existing methods, and it makes a valuable contribution to the field of multi-modal object ReID. The approach is well-designed, thoroughly evaluated, and clearly presented. However, the novelty of the individual components is somewhat limited, and the method depends on external tools, i.e., MLLMs for its operation. For these reasons, a score of 7 is assigned.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### TabPFN: One Model to Rule Them All?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20003v1)
- **Authors**: Qiong Zhang, Yan Shuo Tan, Qinglong Tian, Pengfei Li
- **Abstract**: Hollmann et al. (Nature 637 (2025) 319-326) recently introduced TabPFN, a transformer-based deep learning model for regression and classification on tabular data, which they claim "outperforms all previous methods on datasets with up to 10,000 samples by a wide margin, using substantially less training time." Furthermore, they have called TabPFN a "foundation model" for tabular data, as it can support "data generation, density estimation, learning reusable embeddings and fine-tuning". If these statements are well-supported, TabPFN may have the potential to supersede existing modeling approaches on a wide range of statistical tasks, mirroring a similar revolution in other areas of artificial intelligence that began with the advent of large language models. In this paper, we provide a tailored explanation of how TabPFN works for a statistics audience, by emphasizing its interpretation as approximate Bayesian inference. We also provide more evidence of TabPFN's "foundation model" capabilities: We show that an out-of-the-box application of TabPFN vastly outperforms specialized state-of-the-art methods for semi-supervised parameter estimation, prediction under covariate shift, and heterogeneous treatment effect estimation. We further show that TabPFN can outperform LASSO at sparse regression and can break a robustness-efficiency trade-off in classification. All experiments can be reproduced using the code provided at https://github.com/qinglong-tian/tabpfn_study (https://github.com/qinglong-tian/tabpfn_study).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates TabPFN, a transformer-based deep learning model for tabular data, which the original authors claim outperforms previous methods with less training time and can be considered a "foundation model" for tabular data. The authors provide a statistics-oriented explanation of how TabPFN works, interpreting it as approximate Bayesian inference. They present empirical evidence demonstrating TabPFN's performance in semi-supervised parameter estimation, prediction under covariate shift, and heterogeneous treatment effect estimation, outperforming specialized state-of-the-art methods in these areas. Further, they analyze TabPFN's inductive biases, showing it can outperform LASSO in sparse regression and break the robustness-efficiency trade-off in classification. The authors argue that TabPFN represents a potentially paradigm-shifting approach to statistical modeling, mirroring the impact of large language models in other AI domains, although acknowledging its limitations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by critically examining TabPFN and its implications for the statistics community. The statistics-focused explanation is beneficial for a broader audience to understand this potentially transformative technology. The paper's novelty and significance can be assessed through several dimensions:

*   **Empirical Validation:** The thorough empirical evaluation is a significant strength. By replicating and extending experiments from recently published top statistics journals, the paper offers a fair and grounded comparison. Showing TabPFN's superior performance across diverse statistical tasks like semi-supervised learning, covariate shift adaptation, and HTE estimation provides substantial evidence for its potential.
*   **Inductive Bias Analysis:** The analysis of TabPFN's inductive biases is insightful. Identifying scenarios where TabPFN outperforms or underperforms traditional methods like LASSO helps illuminate its implicit assumptions and limitations. This helps to understand *when* to use TabPFN and highlights where future work could focus on improving its performance.
*   **Statistics Community Perspective:** The paper directly addresses the statistics community and contextualizes TabPFN within existing statistical practices and thinking. The discussion about "in-context learning" is particularly valuable, as it bridges the gap between machine learning and statistics terminology.
*   **Limitations:** The paper acknowledges TabPFN's limitations, which is a sign of rigor. Specifically, the authors recognize TabPFN's struggles with dense regression, high-dimensional data, and plateauing performance beyond a certain sample size.
*   **Novelty and Significance relative to TabPFN paper:** The key strength is *not* proposing a fundamentally new method, but rather *evaluating an existing one* within established statistical settings. The paper *adds* to the original TabPFN work by providing statistical insight and demonstrating broad applicability to important statistical problems. It highlights TabPFN's value to statisticians as a potential "foundation model" that can rival existing sophisticated methods.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** The empirical evaluations primarily rely on synthetic datasets. While this allows for controlled comparisons and replicability, it raises questions about TabPFN's performance in real-world scenarios. It would be very interesting to see similar results reproduced using real datasets.
*   **Limited Theoretical Understanding:** The paper provides a statistical interpretation of TabPFN, but a deeper theoretical understanding of its behavior, generalization properties, and limitations is still lacking. The Bayesian perspective gives some insights but doesn't fully explain what's happening.

**Impact and Influence:**

The paper's potential impact stems from its ability to stimulate discussion and exploration of TabPFN within the statistics community. It can encourage statisticians to adopt and adapt TabPFN for their problems, potentially leading to new methodological developments and applications. The open-source code further facilitates adoption and experimentation. Given the broad reach and importance of tabular data in various domains, the potential for this work to significantly impact statistical practice is high.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of 7. The paper offers significant value by carefully evaluating and explaining a promising machine learning method for a statistical audience. The extensive empirical evaluation adds strong support. The focus on statistical significance and analysis, combined with the open-source code, lowers the barrier for entry to the statistical community. While the core method (TabPFN) is not novel to this specific paper, the perspective, the statistical analysis, and the demonstration of broad applicability *is* novel and valuable.
